Executive Summary
Globally, solar photovoltaic (PV) and battery energy storage system (BESS) supply chains are
dominated by products manufactured in China and elsewhere by Chinese companies. However,
the recent growth of U.S. PV and BESS manufacturing is bolstering energy security via domestic
supply chains that create U.S. manufacturing jobs.

This report analyzes U.S. PV and BESS supply chains and costs in 2024 for PV module and
battery technologies, structural and electrical balance of system (BOS) components, and PV
recycling. The report concludes with an analysis of technology installation trends, government
support for domestic manufacturing, manufacturing jobs, and the domestic content of PV
systems installed in the United States in 2024. Figure ES-1 summarizes the report’s analysis of
U.S. PV and BESS supply chains in 2024, showing the most vulnerability in the crystalline-
silicon (c-Si) module and BESS supply chains, with stronger supply chains for cadmium telluride
(CdTe) modules and structural and electrical BOS components.

U.S. Market and Policies in 2024
In 2024, 47.1 GW direct current (GWdc) of PV was installed in the United States, bringing total
installations to 224.1 GWdc, which accounted for 7% of U.S. electricity generation. About 67%
of the U.S. PV installed in 2024 was in utility-scale systems using single-axis tracking systems
and large, bifacial c-Si (mostly passivated emitter and rear cell, or PERC) modules, with another
16% in utility-scale systems using CdTe modules. The remaining PV capacity mainly consisted
of residential systems (mostly smaller PERC modules and module-level power electronics
[MLPE] including microinverters and dc optimizers), with the balance installed in commercial
systems (ground-mount and rooftop) and utility-scale systems using fixed-tilt racking. PV
systems are increasingly being physically paired or brought online with BESS technologies,
which typically provide 1–6 hours of energy storage, compensating for the variability of PV
generation. Between 2020 and 2024, U.S. BESS installations grew more than tenfold to 96.0
GWh (33.6 GW alternating current [GWac]), including 31.1 GWh (12.3 GWac) installed in 2024
(Masson, Van Rechem, et al. 2025). Together, PV and BESS represented 84% of new U.S.
electricity capacity additions in 2024.

Many trade policies and domestic incentives in place in 2024 were designed to impact the supply
chains behind U.S. PV and BESS installations. To illustrate historical trends, Figure ES-2 shows
U.S. production of PV components between 2007 and 2024. The U.S. Department of Commerce
determined that the steep drop in U.S. module production between 2011 and 2012 was due to
competition from low-price Chinese modules (U.S. Federal Register 2011). Beginning in 2012,
the United States enacted a series of trade barriers on PV components made by Chinese
companies. Production of U.S. modules recovered modestly for several years and then began
growing substantially in 2019. Manufacturers attributed this growth to a reduction in the
corporate tax rate and passage of a tariff on virtually all imported PV modules in 2018.
Additional trade barriers over the next several years were joined by a legislative package in
2022, which created incentives for U.S. PV and BESS manufacturers as well as PV and BESS
project owners that used domestic products. Domestic module manufacturing continued to grow
rapidly. However, even with incentives, domestic manufacturing of wafers and cells was
minimal through 2024 as low-cost products from Chinese supply chains continued to dominate

 vi

these markets. As of 2024, additional trade investigations were initiated to hamper importation of
these products into the United States.

Crystalline-Silicon Modules
The introduction of new U.S. tariffs since 2018, coupled with federal incentives introduced in
2022, have coincided with many companies investing in the U.S. module supply chain. In 2024,
the United States manufactured 7.6 GWdc of c-Si modules, a 155% increase over 2023, with
approximately 41 GWdc of domestic c-Si module assembly capacity operational by the end of
2024. 1 By the second quarter (Q2) of 2025, an estimated 43 GWdc of domestic c-Si module
assembly capacity was operational with an additional 6 GWdc under construction. Still, most U.S.
demand in 2024 was met by imports. In 2024, record levels of c-Si modules (56.1 GWdc) and
cells (13.8 GWdc) were imported into the United States. The United States imported 89% of c-Si
modules in 2024 from Vietnam, Thailand, Cambodia, Malaysia, and the remaining countries
within the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN). ASEAN (64%) and South Korea
(32%) were the source for 96% of c-Si cell imports. Although U.S. trade restrictions have limited
cell and module imports directly from China, many PV components and much of the equipment
used to make cells and modules are from China. In addition, many Southeast Asian PV
manufacturing companies are Chinese controlled.

In 2024, the single U.S. c-Si cell manufacturer could serve 2% of U.S. PV modules produced,
and there was no domestic wafer production. This could change in 2025 if the 5 GWdc of cell
manufacturing capacity (which would increase total domestic c-Si cell manufacturing capacity to

1
 See (Basore and Feldman 2022a) for a detailed description of the manufacturing processes involved in the
production of c-Si PV modules, cells, wafers, and polysilicon.

 viii

8 GWdc) and 10 GWdc of c-Si wafer manufacturing capacity under construction in Q2 2025
comes online.

Also in 2024, the United States produced 11 kt (or 4.3 GWdc) of polysilicon for PV use from
96.3 kt (or 39.3 GWdc) of manufacturing capacity. The United States also had 190 kt of
metallurgical-grade silicon (mg-Si) manufacturing capacity, of which 74 kt (or 35.7 GWdc) was
for PV, with the remaining serving semiconductors and other commercial applications. Wafers
for cells, glass for modules, and many other subcomponents were almost exclusively imported,
mostly from China. Domestically manufactured modules and cells in 2024 were most likely sold
for domestic use, while all U.S. polysilicon manufactured for PV was exported to Vietnam,
Malaysia, and Turkey as a source of legally compliant polysilicon for products to be sold back to
the U.S. market.

Most c-Si PV modules installed in the United States in 2024 were bifacial. While global
manufacturing has shifted to producing tunnel oxide passivated contact (TOPCon) cells (Fischer
et al. 2025), most manufacturers importing cells into the United States—and most operational
and planned domestic cell plants—produced lower-efficiency PERC cells in 2024, because
TOPCon technology faces restrictions due to lawsuits over patent infringement.

The direct cost for manufacturing U.S. modules with domestic cells, wafers, and polysilicon is
double that of imported modules ($471/kWdc vs. $229/kWdc). While U.S. labor costs are higher
than in competing manufacturing locations, more of the difference is due to higher costs for
procuring and shipping other materials and parts from Asia as well as tariffs on those product
components and the manufacturing equipment. In particular, the most competitive large-format
wafer technologies depend most heavily on Chinese suppliers. Quartz crucibles, diamond wire,
and other manufacturing equipment most suitable for PV ingot and wafer production are not
widely available outside China. Downstream, screen-printable metallization pastes for cell
production are also not widely available outside China. For module assembly, PV-specialized
glass, junction boxes, the electrical diodes within junction boxes, and cell stringing, tabbing, and
bussing ribbons are not widely available outside China. Manufacturing equipment for ingots,
wafers, cells, and modules is also not widely available from U.S. suppliers and is generally
lowest cost if sourced from Chinese suppliers. While the United States is developing a local
supply chain, it is challenging to achieve the same economies of scale as China.

Cadmium Telluride Modules
Globally, CdTe modules accounted for 2% of all modules produced in 2024, while accounting
for 18% of U.S. PV installations. In 2024, one company (First Solar) was the only large-scale
producer of CdTe modules. First Solar began shifting to larger-format, more efficient, and
frameless Series 7 modules in 2023. In 2024, more than half its modules produced in the United
States were Series 7, while Series 6 (with a bifacial option) constituted 62% of global
production.

In 2024, there was approximately 22.0 GWdc of global CdTe module manufacturing capacity
(8.9 GWdc domestic) and 15.5 GWdc of production (6.3 GWdc domestic). About 5 GWdc of new
capacity was ramping up in the United States and India in 2024. First Solar announced in 2024
that it plans to expand domestic manufacturing capacity to 14 GWdc by 2026 (all new capacity
coming from Series 7 lines), growing global manufacturing capacity to 25 GWdc and 2025 sales

 ix

to between 15.5 GWdc and 19.3 GWdc. First Solar indicates that Series 7 modules produced in the
United States have 100% domestic sources for components listed in the U.S. Treasury domestic
content guidance, providing significant added value over other available modules in the
marketplace (including its own imported modules). While its Vietnamese and Malaysian
facilities remain active, imports of CdTe modules from those countries into the United States fell
in 2024 by an estimated 15%. Some of this decline was replaced by shipments from India into
the United States, but due to a suspected mislabeling issue, this is difficult to corroborate in
public data.

In general, balance-of-module components are imported for Series 6 modules—from trade
partners such as Malaysia—while components for Series 7 modules produced in the United
States come from domestic suppliers. There is enough domestic production of cadmium for 6
GWdc of modules. No tellurium or selenium refining exists in the United States. First Solar
partners with 5N Plus in Canada to refine U.S. tellurium and produce CdTe powder. Some of the
tellurium-containing residues 5N Plus refines come from Utah, but the company also sources
tellurium elsewhere. Given that Canada’s leading source of imported tellurium is China, the
world’s leading producer (which in early 2025 placed export control on tellurium and CdTe
compounds), there is a high probability that the CdTe modules produced in the United States in
2024 relied on tellurium from China.

Structural Products
The United States hosts a growing base of manufacturing capacity for many PV system structural
components, particularly for utility-scale systems. Some elements—such as torque tubes, piles,
and select tracker assemblies—are widely produced domestically due to their size and shipping
constraints. Others, such as fasteners and certain rooftop components, still rely heavily on
imports.

Major steel components used in utility-scale PV systems represent only a small fraction of total
steel consumption in the United States, which overall relies mostly (87%) on domestic steel mills
for its products. China is a major steel producer as well as a dominant manufacturer of finished
PV steel products. Given China’s scale, low labor costs, and political strategy to export cheap
goods, its products are often sold at a significant discount in global markets. As a result, both raw
and finished steel products from China are a part of some U.S. PV installations. However, the
United States maintains its presence in steel’s upstream supply chain through domestic iron ore
production. It also has a large domestic steel manufacturing capacity and 25% tariffs on imported
steel from most countries in 2024 (increasing to 50% in the first half of 2025), and much of the
steel used in PV installations has a relatively low value-to-weight ratio, which favors a domestic
supply with shorter shipping distances. It is also easier for PV suppliers to source steel within
needed specifications from domestic suppliers, plus there are incentives for PV systems that
depend on the use of domestically produced steel. We estimate that approximately 75% of torque
tubes and driven piles are produced domestically using domestic steel.

However, most generic steel fasteners are still imported (more than 50% from Taiwan and
China), due to their high value-to-weight ratio and the significant size and cost advantage of
foreign supply chains. Generic fasteners used in the PV industry represent a very small part of
the larger fastener fabrication industry. Thus, the major U.S. scale-up required to overcome the
current 40% price premium is unlikely, and U.S. fastener manufacturers may focus on niche

 x

markets such as tracker-specific structural fasteners that are uniquely eligible for the 45X
advanced manufacturing tax credit and 45/48 domestic content bonus incentives.

Racking rails and flashing/feet used in residential and commercial PV rooftop applications are
predominantly aluminum. Approximately 80% of these mounting components are aluminum-
based, accounting for roughly 1% of domestic aluminum consumption. Owing to limited U.S.
smelting capacity and limited U.S. mining of bauxite ore, almost half of aluminum-based
products are imported. The other half is domestically produced from primary smelters using
imported alumina and secondary smelters using domestically available recycled aluminum
products. We estimate approximately 50% of extruded rails used in the U.S. PV industry are
made domestically.

Electrical Products
Electrical components can vary significantly by market segment. Different sizes of inverters are
used to convert direct current (dc) power from PV systems to alternating current (ac) compatible
with the electric grid. Large-scale ground-mount PV systems have historically used central
inverters, although utility-scale string inverters—which are the global norm—are gaining U.S.
market share. In contrast, the rooftop PV sector mostly uses MLPE (e.g., microinverters, dc-
optimized inverter systems), because electrical codes in many states require module-level instant
power shutoff. Instant shutoff can be achieved without an MLPE inverter, but MLPE inverters
offer performance and design-flexibility advantages as well. MLPE are more likely to use
advanced technology power electronics such as SiC or GaN transistors. Most small ground-
mount PV systems and some rooftop applications use string inverters.

Most U.S. inverter manufacturing closed in the middle of the last decade, but it has grown in the
past few years. At the end of 2024, utility-scale string inverter assembly had the largest
manufacturing capacity at around 9.5 GW ac, followed by central inverters (9 GW ac) and MLPE
(7 GWac). Inverter manufacturing has a significant percentage of U.S.-headquartered companies.
There are Japanese, Israeli, and European manufacturers as well. In 2024, many MLPE inverters
were produced in the United States, while most central inverters (which represented 80% of 2024
U.S. inverter shipments) came from Europe and China. Domestic PV inverters cost more to
produce and have higher market pricing compared with inverters made elsewhere owing to
higher material and labor costs as well as various incentives.

Power transistors are key components of PV inverters. MLPE started using metal-oxide-
semiconductor field-effect transistors (MOSFETs) around 2019. MOSFETs are faster and more
efficient at lower voltages than transistors used for larger applications. Historically, larger
inverters have used insulated-gate bipolar transistors (IGBTs), but they are beginning to switch
to SiC MOSFETs, because MOSFETs are more efficient with the higher-voltage module strings
that are increasingly common in U.S. PV systems. Power electronics manufacturers in the United
States generally specialize in SiC or GaN transistor technologies, but some domestic PV inverter
manufacturers have supply agreements that, given the manufacturing locations of their suppliers,
have a high probability of sourcing transistors from Germany and Malaysia.

Printed circuit board assemblies (PCBAs) are specified components within the Internal Revenue
Service (IRS) safe harbor table for the investment tax credit domestic content bonus, which has
helped drive demand for domestic PCBA suppliers. Some PCBA manufacturers operate in the

 xi

United States, enabling domestic manufacturers Enphase, SolarEdge, and Yaskawa Solectria
Solar to provide inverters that partially meet the criteria for the ITC domestic content bonus.

Medium-voltage transformers (defined in this report as having high-side voltages of 1–69 kV)
are another critical electrical product used in large-scale PV applications, which step-up the ac
voltage supplied by the PV system to be fed onto the grid. There is a global supply shortage of
all transformers due to growing demand from renewable energy, electric vehicles, and data
centers combined with a slow build-out of supply. The resulting transformer shortage has
lengthened product lead times and increased prices. In 2024, developers were expected to wait
1–2 years for smaller step-up transformers and up to 3–5 years for larger step-up transformers,
and the delays could increase if projected demands outpace expanded supply. It is estimated that
around half of medium-voltage transformers deployed in the United States are made
domestically. Domestically produced medium-voltage transformers cost approximately 10%–
25% more than imports. Over half of large medium-voltage transformers imported came from
Mexico in 2024, and imports from South Korea and Colombia grew significantly. China
represented approximately 5% of imports in 2024, possibly as a result of Section 301 tariffs
significantly increasing the cost of those imports. The most critical and potentially constrained
inputs to transformer manufacturing include grain-oriented electric steel (GOES) and
continuously transposed conductor copper wire, both of which have limited production capacities
in the United States, but which are produced in large quantities in several countries, including
China and Russia. Foreign transformer manufacturers can purchase foreign GOES at a lower
price than U.S. transformer manufacturers can—because of U.S. tariffs on imported steel—and
then manufacture their products abroad and sell their transformers into the United States.

There are a variety of other electrical components within a PV system, particularly for large-
scale applications, such as high-voltage transformers, combiner boxes, switchgear, circuit
breakers, substation components, and transmission-related equipment. Many come from various
domestic and international manufacturing locations, including Mexico, Canada, and China.
Because of recent increases in U.S. deployment of PV, wind turbines, and electric vehicles—
which rely on many of these components—companies have made recent investments to expand
domestic manufacturing.

Battery Energy Storage Systems
Most batteries manufactured globally, including in the United States, are used in electric
vehicles. However, stationary storage has become a growing source of demand, representing
approximately 14% of global—and 22% of domestic—battery demand in 2024. Domestic utility-
scale BESS energy storage capacity grew by 66% in 2024, and 28% of residential PV systems
installed in 2024 were paired with BESS.

Utility-scale BESS manufacturing capacity in the United States was about 61 GWh at the end of
2024. For comparison, 22 GWh of BESS were imported into the United States in 2024, mainly
from China. Companies headquartered in the United States own over half of U.S. utility-scale
BESS manufacturing capacity, and over 25% is owned by Chinese-headquartered companies. 2

2
 The location of a company’s headquarters is where its executives work and key decisions are made; however, the
company may have manufacturing, sales, and other operations in other countries.

 xii

Residential BESS pack manufacturing also continues to scale, with more than 5 GWh of
manufacturing capacity added at the end of 2024, largely from Tesla. While non-BESS U.S.
battery cell production in 2024 was about 109 GWh, domestic BESS cell production was
insignificant. 3 Multiple companies started cell production in the first half of 2025.
Approximately 10.6 GWh of anode and 9.1 GWh of cathode manufacturing capacity existed in
the United States in 2024, all of which came online after 2022. 4 An additional 22.0 GWh of
anode and of 13.6 GWh of cathode manufacturing capacity was under construction at the end of
2024.

The cost of producing distributed and large-scale battery packs in the United States is higher than
the global average cost of production, in large part because the supply chain is mostly located in
China, which means higher shipping costs and Section 301 tariffs for the relevant materials,
parts, and manufacturing equipment shipped to the United States from China. Additionally, final
products incur 3–5 times higher U.S. labor costs to assemble domestically.

Discussion
PV supply chain growth has resulted in U.S. jobs and U.S.-produced PV products used within
domestic installations—we estimate 13,260 manufacturing jobs in 2024 across segments of the
PV and BESS supply chains. This figure is less than the 33,300 solar manufacturing jobs
reported by the Interstate Renewable Energy Council (IREC) National Solar Jobs Census in
2023. We do not estimate manufacturing jobs for several areas of the supply chain—including
many structural and electrical elements—and the IREC manufacturing jobs total includes some
equipment sales and delivery roles plus other positions not included in our estimates.

Much of the growth in domestic production capacity utilized various tax credits available for
U.S. manufacturers in 2024. We estimate the PV and BESS supply chains absorbed $3.2 billion
in 45X tax credits in 2024 (Figure ES-3). However, this estimate relies on several assumptions,
including full production at some new facilities, which we believe were still ramping during
2024, and the omission of some products whose production is wholly unknown.

3
  Most of the domestic battery cells produced in 2024 used lithium/nickel/manganese/cobalt (NMC) chemistry,
while 2024 BESS products were mostly designed to use lithium/iron (Fe)/phosphate (LFP) cells. Additionally, while
most battery cells produced for non-BESS use (e.g., for electric vehicle applications) are very similar in
specifications to those used for BESS, there are typically minor differences made during the manufacturing process.
Manufacturing lines can usually be altered for either application, but domestic battery cell manufacturers used their
lines for existing orders for non-BESS applications.
4
  We use a conversion factor of 1,034 t/GWh for anode materials and 2,202 t/GWh for cathode materials (Gohlke et
al. 2024). Anode and cathode material capacities serve multiple battery applications, including but not limited to
BESS.

 xiii

 * Assumes facilities are operating at half capacity for the full year. Actual 45X claims in 2024 may differ due to
 ramping of new facilities.
 + In the case of a battery module that does not use battery cells, $45/kWh.

In addition to the 45X tax credit, manufacturing companies also received federal support from a
variety of programs, including:

 • 48C (Qualifying Advanced Energy Project Tax Credit): between May 2024 and January
 2025, the IRS allocated $10 billion in tax credits (worth 30% of eligible project costs) to
 approximately 250 applicants over two rounds of funding. To date, 59 applicants have
 self-disclosed their 48C awards, of which three will recycle solar modules, one will
 produce solar-grade polysilicon, and one will produce solar-grade glass.
 • The 48D (Advanced Manufacturing Investment Tax Credit) provides a 25% tax credit for
 purchasing and commissioning property to build a facility whose primary purpose is the
 manufacturing of semiconductors or semiconductor manufacturing equipment. The
 United States Treasury released its final rules for 48D in October 2024, in which they
 clarified that solar wafer manufacturing facilities were eligible for the credit. Shortly
 after, Norsun signed an agreement with an engineering, procurement, and construction
 company, Clayco, to build its wafer facility in Oklahoma. Corning also confirmed soon
 after the final rule release that it would manufacture solar wafers.
 • In October 2024, the U.S. Department of Commerce announced a $325 million award to
 Hemlock Semiconductor from the CHIPS Incentives Program to support the construction
 of a new manufacturing facility that will produce polysilicon for the solar and
 semiconductor industries. The award was finalized in January 2025.
 • In December 2024, the DOE Loan Programs Office awarded a $1.45 billion loan
 guarantee to Qcells to produce ingots, wafers, cells, and finished modules in Georgia.
 • Since the domestic content bonus credit of Sections 48 and 45 was introduced and a safe
 harbor table was published that provides clearer pathways to qualification, several
 manufacturers have announced solar products with higher levels of domestic content to
 help projects qualify for the bonus. MLPE companies Enphase and SolarEdge; tracking

 xiv

 companies Nextracker, Array Technologies, and GameChange; wafer, cell, and module
 manufacturers Corning, Suniva, and Heliene; and cell manufacturer ES Foundry have all
 announced increasing the domestic content of their products to help projects qualify for
 the bonus. While the domestic content bonus credit is given to electric generation asset
 owners, not manufacturers, it indirectly supports domestic manufacturing by giving those
 products and components more value.
We evaluated U.S. manufacturers’ ability to supply the various sectors of the 47.1 GWdc U.S.
market for PV systems installed in 2024 under the domestic content criteria for Section 48 and
45 tax credits. 5 The total U.S. market for PV systems installed during 2024 is shown in Figure
ES-4. Domestic content in this figure is defined following the 2024 safe harbor tables and can be
split into four system types defined in the IRS document: ground-mount tracking, ground-mount
fixed-tilt, rooftop string inverter, and rooftop MLPE. When considering all these system types
together, the domestic content is roughly 13.5% by value, in a total market size of $24.1 billion.
However, this percentage varies between system types and between individual PV system
projects.

As shown in Figure ES-4, the domestic value for systems installed during 2024 primarily comes
from domestic racking, trackers, and CdTe modules as well as inverters to a much smaller
degree. Per the domestic content bonus credit criteria, domestic CdTe modules already qualify as
having a domestic cell, and the Series 7 module produced by First Solar contains 100% domestic
components as defined by the IRS table, which means it also qualifies for the domestic
production value in the table.

While the domestic content average in this diagram is well below the minimum investment tax
credit threshold of 40% for 2024, it represents an average of many systems, which can vary
significantly in content. We estimate that up to 8 GWdc of systems met the domestic content
bonus criteria in 2024. Based on domestic production in 2024—documented throughout this
report—we expect the installed capacity in 2025 that meets the domestic content bonus criteria to
greatly increase.

  This analysis assumes a 1-year delay between shipping and installation; therefore, the Sankey diagrams reflect
2024 PV installation totals but rely on 2023 manufacturing totals and sourcing. The data in this section rely heavily
on utility system installation totals, and technical features reported by the U.S. Energy Information Administration
are presented in the Discussion section. Total 2024 installation data are from the International Energy Agency’s
Photovoltaic Power Systems Programme data provided by the U.S. Department of Energy’s Solar Energy
Technologies Office. Information on commercial and residential installation also draws upon some technology
trends reported by Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory’s 2024 Tracking the Sun report.

1 Introduction
In 2010, solar photovoltaics (PV) accounted for 0.1% of total U.S. electricity generation and less
than 5% of new electricity capacity additions. In 2024, PV generated approximately 7% of U.S.
electricity and—after years of being the leading source of new U.S. generation capacity—was
the second largest source of capacity, representing tens of billions of dollars of products within a
variety of supply chains (Figure 1). At the end of 2024, the United States had a cumulative PV
capacity of 224.1 GW direct current (GWdc), with 47.1 GWdc of PV installed in 2024 alone.

 Figure 1. Annual U.S. electric capacity additions, 2010–2024
 Reproduced from (Feldman et al. 2024; Feldman, Zuboy, Dummit, Heine, Grossman, et al. 2025); ac = alternating
 current.

PV systems provide electricity when the sun is up, but production wanes around the hours of
peak demand in some parts of the country, typically 4–7 p.m. across U.S. time zones. Short-term
battery energy storage systems (BESS) can help meet demand in those later hours, charging with
PV-generated electricity from morning to late afternoon and then, most commonly, discharging
over a period of 1–6 hours. BESS use has been growing with the help of falling lithium-ion
battery (LIB) cell and system costs. From 2020 to 2024, U.S. BESS installations grew more than
tenfold, with a year-end cumulative capacity of 96.0 GWh (33.6 GWac), including 31.1 GWh
(12.3 GWac) installed in 2024 (Masson, Van Rechem, et al. 2025). Together, PV and BESS
represented more than 65% of new U.S. electricity capacity additions in 2023 and 84% in 2024.

Figure 2 shows annual U.S. PV installations from 2010 to 2024 across the utility-scale sector and
the two distributed sectors: nonresidential (commercial and industrial applications, carports,
schools, etc.) and residential. Utility-scale systems have accounted for most U.S. PV
installations, with a compound annual growth rate of 35%. Despite a dip in 2024, distributed PV
(mostly residential) achieved a compound annual growth rate of 20%.

 1

 Figure 2. Annual U.S. PV additions, 2010–2024
As PV and BESS have grown in importance, they have increasingly become subject to federal
policies and trade actions aimed, at least in part, at supporting U.S. manufacturing (Figure 3).
From 2009 to 2012, the average global price of PV modules dropped by around 30% per year
owing to the rapid expansion of low-cost Chinese production. The U.S. PV industry, which had
been operating around trade parity before 2010, began importing more Chinese modules to meet
growing demand. As a result, many U.S. and foreign manufacturers went bankrupt. In 2012, the
United States began placing duties on Chinese PV modules—see Figure 3, “China AD/CVD
final determination (USITC) Dec 2012.” Chinese companies shifted supply chains to avoid the
duties, and then duties were placed on products from the new supply chains—see Figure 3,
“Taiwan AD/CVD final determination, Dec 2014,” “Circumvention AD/CVD final
determination, Aug 2023,” and “SE Asia AD/CVD preliminary determination, Nov 2024.” In
2014—seemingly in response to U.S. trade actions—China imposed tariffs on U.S. polysilicon,
which effectively shut U.S. polysilicon producers out of the largest market for PV polysilicon.

Through 2018, U.S. PV manufacturing continued to stagnate (Figure 4), while U.S. PV module
and cell pricing dropped and domestic deployment grew (Figure 2). After 2018, U.S. module
pricing began to increase, and U.S. module manufacturing began to grow, which manufacturers
attributed to a reduced corporate tax rate (2017) and a tariff on virtually all imported PV
modules—see Figure 3, “Section 201 tariffs begin at 30%, Feb 2018.” Module manufacturers in
the United States still relied on China, or Chinese companies, for upstream components and
material, particularly given the exclusion on the first 2.5 GWdc/yr of imported PV cells as well as
tariff exceptions for bifacial modules. In June 2019, the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative
ruled that bifacial modules were exempt from the Section 201 duties because they differed in
design from the monofacial modules U.S. manufacturers were producing. At the time, U.S.
manufacturers were selling modules into the rooftop market, while bifacial modules were
designed and sold for ground-mount applications. The exemption was revoked and reinstated
multiple times, but over time many imported modules were not subject to the Section 201 duty.

 2

  Energy; ITC = investment tax credit; MPTC = Advanced Manufacturing Production Tax Credit; UFLPA = Uyghur
 Forced Labor Prevention Act; USITC = U.S. International Trade Commission; WRO = withhold release order.

                                      2025; PV Tech Research 2025)

Further trade restrictions, as well as incentives for manufacturers and project owners who used
domestic products, boosted U.S. module manufacturing capacity. However, many upstream
products continued to be imported from China or Chinese-controlled companies, causing
continued investigations and duties on products entering the United States from new areas. These
measures included AD/CVD investigations in 2021 and 2024 (which continued through new or
existing investigations in 2025) on Chinese companies, or companies that relied on the low-cost
Chinese supply chain, operating in Southeast Asia. In 2025, these investigations expanded to
India. Measures also included the Hoshine withhold release order in June 2021 and UFLPA in
June 2022, both of which aimed to stop importation of products that could contain material made
using forced labor in Xinjiang, where much of China’s metallurgical-grade silicon (mg-Si) and
solar-grade polysilicon is produced.

In the past several years, the U.S. government enacted legislation providing tax credits, loans,
and grants to PV module, component, and subcomponent manufacturing as well as PV inverter,
PV tracker component, and BESS and component manufacturing. Among other goals, these
incentives aimed to help U.S. products compete with imported products.

This report provides information about PV and BESS component supply chains and
manufacturing costs as of 2024:

 • Section 2: Crystalline-Silicon Modules
 • Section 3: Cadmium Telluride Modules
 • Section 4: Structural Components

 4

 • Section 5: Electrical Components
 • Section 6: Energy Storage
 • Section 7: Prospective Supply Chains
For each subcomponent in these sections, the report provides analysis of technology trends,
domestic production, imports/exports, regional costs, and upstream inputs. Section 8 concludes
with analysis of U.S. technology installation trends, government support, manufacturing jobs,
and domestic content. Technologies with relatively small market shares in 2024 are not covered
in the report, such as III-V, perovskite, copper-indium-gallium-diselenide (CIGS), and organic
PV technologies.

 5

2 Crystalline-Silicon Modules
2.1 Overview
In 2024, U.S. demand for crystalline-silicon (c-Si) PV modules was increasingly met by modules
manufactured in the United States, driven by the trends discussed in Section 1. The United States
imported 45.9 GWdc of c-Si modules in 2024, which was only 0.4 GWdc more than in 2023 but
up 100% from 2022. In 2024, 89% of c-Si modules imported into the United States came from
Vietnam, Thailand, Cambodia, Malaysia, and the remaining countries within the Association of
Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN)—up from 77% in 2023 (Feldman, Zuboy, Dummit, Stright,
et al. 2025). 6 However, module imports in the fourth quarter (Q4) of 2024 were 52% lower than
quarterly average imports during Q1–Q3 2024, coinciding with growth in U.S. module
production. In total, the United States produced 7.6 GWdc of c-Si modules in 2024 (PV Tech
Research 2025)—most likely for domestic use—with a year-end manufacturing capacity of 41.0
GWdc, as estimated by National Laboratory of the Rockies (NLR). If domestic module
manufacturing continues to ramp up, including startup of plants under construction at the end of
2024, it may meet near-term demand.

Domestic production of c-Si cells was much smaller in 2024 at 0.2 GWdc—most likely used in
domestic module manufacturing—with a year-end manufacturing capacity of 1.5 GWdc (PV
Tech Research 2025) and another 6.3 GWdc under construction. ASEAN (64%) and South Korea
(32%) accounted for 96% of the 13.8 GWdc of c-Si cells imported in 2024 (Feldman, Zuboy,
Dummit, Stright, et al. 2025). While global manufacturing has shifted to producing tunnel oxide
passivated contacts (TOPCon) cells (Fischer et al. 2025), most U.S. cell imports—and most
operational and planned domestic manufacturing—are lower-efficiency passivated emitter and
rear cell (PERC) cells, because TOPCon technology faces restrictions due to lawsuits over patent
infringement (see Section 2.4.1).

NLR estimates that there were approximately 10 GWdc of c-Si wafer manufacturing capacity
under construction at the end of 2024; however, no c-Si wafers were produced domestically in
2024 (PV Tech Research 2025). PVTech reports that 11 kt (or 4.3 GWdc) of U.S. polysilicon was
produced for PV use in 2024—all of which was exported to Vietnam, Malaysia, and Turkey as a
source of UFLPA-compliant polysilicon for products to be sold back to the U.S. market (PV
Tech Research 2025). The United States also had 190 kt of mg-Si manufacturing capacity of
which 74 kt (or 35.7 GWdc) was for PV, with the remaining serving semiconductors and other
commercial applications.

Although U.S. trade restrictions have limited direct importation of cells and modules from China,
many PV components and much of the equipment used to make cells and modules are from
China. In addition, many ASEAN PV manufacturing companies are Chinese controlled.

The direct cost for manufacturing U.S. modules with domestic cells, wafers, and polysilicon
($471/kWdc) is twice that of imported modules ($229/kWdc) (Woodhouse 2025). While U.S.
labor costs are relatively high, more of the difference is due to higher costs for procuring and
shipping other materials and parts from Asia as well as tariffs on the product components and
6
 These countries are within ASEAN, which also includes other countries with growing solar exports to the United
States, such as Laos and Cambodia.

 6

manufacturing equipment. Various critical items are not widely available outside China: (1)
quartz crucibles, diamond wire, and other manufacturing equipment most suitable for PV ingot
and wafer production; (2) screen-printable metallization pastes for cell production; and (3) PV-
specialized glass, junction boxes, electrical diodes within junction boxes, and cell stringing,
tabbing, and bussing ribbons for module assembly. Manufacturing equipment for ingots, wafers,
cells, and modules is not widely available from U.S. suppliers and is generally lowest cost from
Chinese suppliers. While the United States is developing a local supply chain, it is challenging to
achieve economies of scale.

2.2 Global Context
More than 98% of global PV module manufacturing capacity was c-Si in 2024 (Figure 5). The c-
Si supply chain has primarily scaled in Asia since 2010, mostly in China. ASEAN also hosts
significant manufacturing centers owing to its proximity to China and its low labor costs as well
as U.S. trade policies that hinder direct imports from China.

 Figure 5. Global PV annual manufacturing capacities in 2024.
 Data source: “Solar Manufacturing Assets” Database, Accessed December 2024 (BNEF 2025b)

Much of the c-Si manufacturing capacity in Asia was underutilized in 2024, as large investments
in manufacturing capacity made during 2019–2023 led to oversupply by the second half of 2023.
Over 1,000 GWdc of global PV manufacturing capacity existed at the end of 2024, but only 580
GWdc of PV was installed (BNEF 2025b). Market prices fell below cash costs for most

 7

manufacturers as 705 GWdc of modules were produced globally in 2024 (PV Tech Research
2025), and more than 100 GWdc of excess inventory carried over into 2025. Longer term, the
continuation of global oversupply conditions depends on Chinese industrial policies and the
balance sheets of firms operating there.

U.S. policies have sought to support domestic PV manufacturing, including using tariffs and
AD/CVD duties, which raise the administrative burden and prices for imports. Since the original
AD/CVD cases against China in 2011, a large difference between U.S. module prices and prices
elsewhere has persisted. In 2024, U.S. module prices were $250–$350/kWdc compared with $80–
$120/kWdc in the rest of the world (Feldman, Zuboy, Dummit, Heine, Narayanaswami, et al.
2025).

While global PV manufacturers enjoy higher profits in the U.S. market, they must manage
supply chain considerations and logistics, which has driven a change in the source of U.S.
imports over the past 15 years. Considerations include the following:

 • UFLPA inspection: Manufacturers of detained products must provide rigorous
 documentation for materials as far upstream as mg-Si—and sometimes even to the
 originally mined silica. In 2024, 47% ($1.5 billion) of U.S. Customs and Border
 Protection detentions of electronics were PV-related (Jacobo 2024a; Philip Shen 2025),
 representing 5–8 GWdc of imports, or 10%–18% of all modules imported into the United
 States.
 • Uncertainty over the timing of tariffs and AD/CVD: Countries and companies face
 changing duties and tariffs and may not know the final rates until years after product
 sales.
Such factors continue to create uncertainty for U.S. PV developers while motivating U.S.
component manufacturing. Federal incentives for domestic manufacturing, including the 45X
Advanced Manufacturing Production Tax Credit (hereafter referred to as the “45X MPTC”) and
the Section 45 and Section 48 domestic content bonus credits (hereafter referred to as the
“domestic content bonus credit”)—along with the long-term uncertainty of import price
stability—explain the emergence of U.S. wafer and cell capacity in 2024 (Figure 6). 7 In previous
years, little or no U.S. PV ingot, wafer, or cell manufacturing existed, while U.S. polysilicon
producers made limited sales to overseas PV ingot manufacturers. 8

Wafer and cell manufacturing require substantial capital expenditure (CapEx) to achieve
minimum effective scales ($500 million to $1.5 billion), long construction times (at least twice as
long as for module assembly), and more extensive permitting and engineering complexities
around chemical and other safety hazards that are not present for module assembly, which are
manageable yet create public concern and heightened regulatory oversight. Concurrently, they
face competition from overseas operations that are already producing at scale and often sell

7
  Nameplate manufacturing capacity assumes operation ongoing 24 hours per day, 7 days per week, 365 days per
year. Utilization rates account for the labor rates, maintenance downtime, etc., which result in less than full-capacity
production. Figure 6 displays relatively large levels of nonutilized c-Si module manufacturing capacity in 2024 due
to new plants ramping up.
8
  These sales were primarily for the semiconductor market, because U.S. polysilicon producers can offer purity
advantages that translate to higher selling prices in this market.

 8

below cost during times of industry oversupply (as seen in 2024). Up to around 15% of U.S. PV
installations could be met by domestic cell suppliers by 2027, assuming all operating facilities
and facilities under construction in Q2 2025 come online (see Section 2.4.2). In 2024, the single
U.S. cell manufacturer could serve 2% of U.S. PV modules produced (PV Tech Research 2025).
There were no shipments of domestic wafers in 2024, with facilities under construction expected
to start producing in 2025 and fully ramp by 2027. In Q2 2025, domestic c-Si wafer
manufacturing capacities under construction exceeded domestic c-Si cell manufacturing capacity
operating or under construction. Market pricing, trade policies, and the stability of manufacturing
and system-level incentives will play a role in future production. The following sections provide
detail on each segment of the c-Si supply chain.

 Figure 6. U.S. production and operating capacity for polysilicon (P), wafers (W), cells (C), and
 modules (M), 2015–2024; capacity under construction, 2024.
Polysilicon production is reported for PV applications only; polysilicon production for semiconductor applications is not
reported and represents an undetermined amount of the unutilized polysilicon capacity shown in this figure. Some of
 the increase in polysilicon capacity is due to fewer needed grams of polysilicon per watt of module power.
 Sources: (PV Tech Research 2025), internal NLR analysis

2.3 Module Assembly
This section discusses direct inputs for c-Si module assembly, excluding PV cells and glass
(Figure 7).

 9

 Figure 7. Supply chain diagram for c-Si module assembly

2.3.1 Technology Trends for Crystalline-Silicon Modules
The overall trends for c-Si modules have reflected the demand pull for higher-efficiency modules
with larger area and higher power ratings, all of which lower balance of system (BOS) costs.
Higher module efficiencies have been achieved by switching from full-area aluminum back
surface field cells, which dominated the global market up until around 2018, to PERC cells,
which led in global market share from 2019 to 2022. TOPCon cells led globally with a 55%
market share in 2024, while PERC fell to a 35% share. Manufacturers in the United States have
not consistently followed global technology trends. In addition, c-Si cells and modules represent
a smaller percentage of the U.S. market than the global average, as the U.S. has lagged global
movements toward more efficient technologies. Even while many leading PV manufacturers
selling to the U.S. market have switched most of their global production to TOPCon (e.g., 90%
of JinkoSolar’s Q4 2024 shipments were TOPCon), PERC continues to hold the largest share of
the U.S. market. Section 2.4.1 discusses U.S. lawsuits over TOPCon intellectual property (IP).

Monocrystalline module efficiencies have also increased by reducing cell-to-module power
losses (by optimizing cell interconnection and soldering) and by adopting larger cells with a
squarer shape (vs. older technologies with more dead space on the module due to rounded cell
corners). Total module power ratings doubled from 250–300 Wdc in 2014 to 500–600+ Wdc in
2024 for utility applications through 25% improvement in cell efficiency, up to 40%
improvement in module area efficiency through better cell shapes and interconnection, and 50%
larger module area (Zuboy et al. 2022, 2023). Larger module area and higher power rating helps
reduce utility-scale system costs by reducing the module count and total land area needed to
achieve the desired system size (Ardani et al. 2021; Ramasamy et al. 2025). Figure 8 illustrates
some of the principal c-Si module technology advances since 2014.

 10

 Figure 8. PV module technology trends.
 The CTM power rating ratio is calculated by dividing the power rating of a module by the power rating of all the
 module’s cells.
                                          Source: (Zuboy et al. 2022, 2023)

Also illustrated in Figure 8 is the adoption of bifacial module technologies. For residential
applications using monofacial modules, bifacial cells can be placed on white or black backsheets.
More commonly, bifacial cells are placed in modules with transparent back glass or a transparent
backsheet. Globally, bifacial c-Si cells accounted for less than 3% of the market in 2015 (Cellere
et al. 2016) and are projected to have greater than 90% market share in 2024, including cells for
utility and residential applications (Fischer et al. 2025).

The United States was an early adopter of bifacial c-Si technologies for technological reasons
(including improvements in system power production) and trade policy reasons. Beginning in
2018, all c-Si cells and modules imported into the United States were tariffed at 30%, with a
scheduled phase down to 15% in 2021 (Pickerel 2021). Bifacial modules were granted a full
exemption from these Section 201 tariffs in 2019 (Pickerel 2019) in large part because bifacial
modules were most applicable to utility-scale applications, and U.S. manufacturing was
producing PV modules for distributed PV applications. The exemption was repealed in 2020,
reinstated in 2021, and finally removed in 2024 (Pickerel 2024b).

Until February 2026, all imported c-Si modules (regardless of bifaciality or country of origin,
with a few minor exceptions) are tariffed at 14.25% under Section 201 of the Trade Act (U.S.
International Trade Administration 2025b). Analysis of U.S. Customs and Border Protection data
found that 42% of modules imported into the United States were bifacial in 2020, and 82% were
bifacial in 2023 (Pickerel 2024b; U.S. International Trade Administration 2025b). Analysis of
the California and New York distributed PV databases (NYSERDA 2025; California Public
Utilities Commission 2025) found that less than 10% of distributed systems larger than 5 MWdc
installed in 2020 were bifacial, but constituted 80% of those sized systems installed in 2024 (vs.
5% of systems with capacities of 2.5–10 kWdc).

 11

2.3.2 Domestic Production of Crystalline-Silicon Modules
PV Tech reported that in 2024 the United States produced 7.6 GWdc of c-Si modules (up 155%,
year over year), with an NLR-estimated year-end U.S. module assembly capacity of 41.0 GWdc, 9
up 488% year over year ( PV Tech Research 2025); NLR estimates that there was an additional
9.1 GWdc of manufacturing capacity under construction at the end of 2024, of which 3.5 GWdc
came online in the first half of 2025. 10 At the same time, 1.4 GWdc of U.S. manufacturing
capacity went offline in the first half of 2025 when Meyer Burger filed for bankruptcy. The inset
of Figure 9 shows operational capacity and capacity with financing secured and under
construction as of Q2 2025, organized by location of company headquarters. 11

In 2024, Korean-headquartered Qcells had the largest U.S. c-Si module manufacturing capacity,
with capacity expansion plans including upstream investments in cell and wafer manufacturing.
The next largest U.S. c-Si module producers in 2024 were Canadian Solar, which is
headquartered in Ontario but has zero c-Si module assembly capacity in Canada, 41 GWdc in
China, and 56 GWdc total across the globe (BNEF 2025b); T1 Energy, which announced in Q1
2025 that it was rebranding from a Norwegian-founded company, Freyr Battery, to a U.S.-
headquartered c-Si module assembly firm, with technology licensed from Chinese-headquartered
Trina Solar (Pickerel 2025b), and U.S.-headquartered Illuminate Energy, with technology
licensed from Chinese-headquartered LONGi. Of the total 43.0 GWdc of annual operating
capacity in the United States as of Q2 2025, and the total 5.6 GWdc under construction, at least
20 GWdc can be identified as leveraged from Chinese operations and technologies.

9
  Wood Mackenzie reported 13.0 GWdc of U.S. PV production in 2024, with a year-end capacity of 31.8 GWdc
(Wood Mackenzie Power & Renewables and Solar Energy Industries Association 2025b). PVTech reported a U.S.
module assembly year-end capacity of 35.3 GWdc, which NLR adjusted based on additional research of individual
companies.
10
 NLR estimates for manufacturing capacity operating or under construction as of Q2 2025 represent values to the
best of our knowledge, as some companies do not publicize their activity.
11
 The location of the company, or parent company, headquarters is organized as “U.S. HQ,” “China HQ,” or “Other
HQ” according to the headquarters identified on the website of each company or parent company. The organization
is not based on where each company acquired the underlying IP and licensing agreements for their process, whether
they acquired any assets from a company headquartered in a different country, whether most of the company’s
operations are in a different country from the headquarters of record (e.g., Canadian Solar), or the nationalities of the
company’s owners.

2.3.3 Imports/Exports of Crystalline-Silicon Modules
Trends in U.S. module imports by origin region during 2020–2024 are shown in Figure 10. The
United States imported 45.9 GWdc of c-Si modules in 2024, which was only 0.4 GWdc more than
in 2023 but up 100% from 2022. However, module imports in Q4 2024 were 52% lower than
quarterly average imports during Q1–Q3 2024, coinciding with growth in U.S. module
production.

In 2024, 89% of modules imported into the United States came from Vietnam, Thailand,
Cambodia, Malaysia, and the remaining countries within ASEAN—up from 77% in 2023.
Despite imported modules from India contracting 32%, year over year, India was still the fifth-
leading source of imports into the United States, having grown fourfold since 2022 and fortyfold
since 2021. Additionally, some modules from India may be incorrectly categorized as thin-film
modules, which would imply a reduction in c-Si module imports from India of only 6%. 12

In contrast to China’s share of capacity shown in Figure 5, U.S. imports from China declined
from less than 7% in Q1 2020 to near zero by the end of 2024. The direct trade of PV modules,
cells, wafers, and polysilicon from China to the United States is affected by Section 301 tariffs
and comparatively high AD/CVD rates vs. other countries. Total AD/CVD duties for modules

12
  The 2024 U.S. c-Si import value of 45.9 GWdc includes 3.5 GWdc of modules from India classified as c-Si;
however, this does not include 1.3 GWdc imported to the United States from India classified as non-c-Si modules.
While First Solar did inaugurate its 3.3-GWdc CdTe module plant in India in January 2024, some of the imports from
India may have been misclassified, and a larger volume of c-Si imports occurred in 2024.

 13

averaged between 23% and 34% in 2012, when the statute was first used and have climbed in
parallel with Section 301 duties that were as high as 310% in 2024.

 Figure 10. U.S. c-Si module imports by country or region.
 Data source: (Feldman, Zuboy, Dummit, Stright, et al. 2025)

The United States has imported most of its c-Si modules from ASEAN for more than 5 years due
to those countries’ low labor costs and proximity to the large and established PV supply chain in
China, which can provide training, manufacturing equipment, and materials. India became a
significant source in 2022, with a relatively new local PV production base and manufacturing
costs to similar to ASEAN’s. Of the 45.9 GWdc of 2024 imports shown in Figure 10, at least 37
GWdc from ASEAN would be subject in 2025 to pending AD/CVD decisions and new duty rates
set by the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative, U.S. International Trade Commission, and
U.S. Department of Commerce.

Modules from Canada and South Korea also appear within the U.S. import data, benefiting from
historically low tariff and AD/CVD rates but having less production than India and ASEAN
owing to higher labor costs. PV manufacturing in Mexico could be a low-cost option, but
investment in PV manufacturing has been very limited there. One reason for low U.S. reliance on
Canada or Mexico is that port-to-port direct overseas module shipping costs account for only
around 2% of total utility-scale system costs and less than 1% of total residential system costs
(Ramasamy et al. 2025; Woodhouse et al. Submitted).

In 2024, 55 kWdc of U.S. modules were exported (representing 0.001% of 2024 U.S. c-Si module
production), possibly for product research and testing or niche products not used to generate
electricity connected to the electric grid.

2.3.4 Regional Costs for Crystalline Silicon Modules
Figure 11 shows NLR-calculated cost breakdowns for module assembly in China, ASEAN,
India, the United States, and Europe. The net impacts due to labor and utility cost differentials

 14

are not even half the impact of materials cost differentials. China has the lowest materials cost.
Module assembly firms in China benefit from local supply chains for all materials, which
minimizes delivery costs and makes supply chain management easier than for the rest of the
world. ASEAN and India have similar materials costs. Module assembly firms in ASEAN are
near Chinese materials suppliers and benefit from free trade agreements with China. Most
production in India requires the transport of materials and equipment from China, but India is
actively developing local materials supply chains including glass and encapsulants. The largest
differentials for materials costs are in Europe (due to further distances and tariffs on solar glass)
and the United States (due to further distances and tariffs on all materials imported from China
except for front glass). For all countries outside China, there are ongoing efforts to localize
supply chains, most commonly for balance-of-module materials.

 Factory gate modeled price excludes solar cells (Figure 16). Tariffs on finished PV modules are given in Figure 12.
 R&D = research and development; SG&A = selling, general, and administrative

 • Imports from (1) ASEAN and (2) India, the sources of more than 85% of c-Si modules
 imported into the United States in 2024
 • U.S. module assembly with cells imported from ASEAN (the most common U.S.
 production supply chain)
 • A theoretical U.S. module made with U.S. polysilicon, wafers, and cells.

because we did not receive sufficient data to determine the price premium paid for domestic
module, inverter, BOS, and BESS products relative to the benefit received by the project owner

between the time left available before the tax credits expire vs. the more typically assumed useful lifetime for
manufacturing equipment and facilities—5 to 10 years for module assembly, after ramping up—and a discount in
value as the tax credits are most typically sold to third parties. The NLR discounted cash flow calculator for PV
manufacturing assumed three equipment CapEx investments and one facility investment for a 15-year project,
remaining production costs and overhead shown in Figure 11, a 7.5% rate of return on investment, and 45X MPTCs
declining from 90% of full value for the first 4 years (i.e., $63/kWdc from 2025 to 2029) and incrementally down to
zero 3 years later (i.e., the 45X MPTC is scheduled to have 75% of its value in 2030, 50% in 2031, and 25% in the
last eligible year, 2032).

for the domestic content bonus credits. 14 However, considering the additional project value for
achieving the domestic content threshold, the price for a given U.S. product could be higher by
some amount and still be competitive with imports, as shown in a separate analysis (Woodhouse
2025). Anza Renewables reported a $0.15/Wdc to $0.20/Wdc price premium for PV modules
assembled in the United States with U.S.-made cells relative to imported PV modules—relatively
consistent with the price differences shown in Figure 12 (Anza Renewables 2025).

2.3.5 Upstream Inputs for Crystalline-Silicon Modules
Table 1 reports cost estimates for the primary module assembly materials and equipment in the
United States and Asia, as well as estimated use of U.S. suppliers in 2024. The information is
from NLR interviews of U.S. module assembly firms and materials and equipment suppliers.

 • Cells: Discussed in Section 2.4.
 • Tabbing and busbar ribbons: Domestic availability of this material in 2024 is
 estimated at around 5%, and only one module vendor has been confirmed to use
 domestically produced metal ribbons.
 • Encapsulant: Interview data suggest more domestic encapsulant use in 2024 than use of
 any other balance-of-module material. One U.S. module assembly firm estimated that
 total planned domestic encapsulant capacity could ramp to around 25 GWdc-equivalent by
 2027. Encapsulant extrusions are also easier to set up than glass lines (beginning with the
 fact glass melts at a much higher temperature) and requires much less capital.
 • Backsheets: For use in monofacial modules, there was significant U.S. backsheet
 production in 2024. Three U.S. module assembly firms were confirmed to use domestic
 backsheets in 2024. The combined 2024 capacity of these firms is greater than 7 GWdc,
 with the largest of the three also selling utility-size modules. Residential modules made in
 the United States with domestic backsheets are estimated at around 50% of the market.
 • Glass: Discussed in Section 2.8.
 • Frames: With enough demand pull, switching metal extrusions to the profile for PV
 module frames is straightforward, and at least 10 operations could do so within the
 United States. However, only two U.S. module assembly firms with total combined
 production up to 2.7 GWdc in 2024 were known to use domestic frame materials. PV
 frames represented a very small share of sales from U.S. aluminum extrusion companies
 in 2024, which we believe was due to competition from low-cost imports and the
 competing uses for aluminum extrusions including construction, automotive, electronics,
 and ubiquitous consumer products. In February 2025, Heliene and Solx Energy
 announced agreements with domestic steel-framed provider Origami Solar for their PV
 modules (Heliene, Inc. 2025; Jacobo 2025d) .

14
  The value of the domestic content bonus credit also varies by the cost of the project under the Section 48 ITC
(which can vary widely between the cost per watt of a utility-scale and residential PV system) or the amount of
electricity generated by the project under the Section 45 production tax credit (which can vary widely by location
and system design). The domestic content bonus credit premium, relative to the base credit, is lower under the
Section 45 production tax credit than the Section 48 ITC. However, project owners may still receive more total
credits under Section 45, depending on the number of bonus credits taken, the cost of the project, and the amount of
electricity the project generates. Cost analysis may also be difficult given the practice of PV system owners to blend
domestic and foreign PV modules to attain a domestic content percentage slightly above the threshold required for
receiving the bonus credit.

 17

 • Junction boxes: Only one U.S. supplier of junction boxes was found in 2024, Amphenol.
 Two others were mentioned as exploring production during industry interviews. One
 module assembly firm estimated that total planned domestic junction box capacity could
 be up to 10–15 GWdc-equivalent by 2027.
 • Equipment: While there are numerous small-scale U.S. research efforts to improve
 module assembly processes and equipment, there were no known U.S. equipment
 suppliers in 2024 with gigawatt-level sales and installations within U.S. facilities. The
 only known gigawatt-level capabilities were equipment suppliers from Spain, Italy, India,
 South Korea, and China.

2.4 Solar Cell
This section focuses on c-Si cell fabrication. Direct inputs to cell fabrication are discussed,
excluding wafers, which are covered in Section 2.5. Figure 13 is a simplified supply chain
diagram for 2024 benchmark U.S. cell production. The steps and direct inputs reflect production
of PERC cells—the technology choice of the only producing U.S. cell supplier in 2024. This
section discusses this choice within the portfolio of other c-Si cells options.

 Figure 13. Simplified supply chain diagram for c-Si PERC cell fabrication.
 CVD = chemical vapor deposition; IPA = isopropyl alcohol

2.4.1 Technology Trends for Crystalline-Silicon Cells
Prior to 2017–2018, the dominant cell architecture for multicrystalline and monocrystalline cells
was commonly full-area aluminum back surface field cells, but the market switched to PERC by
2022 owing to its higher efficiency (Fischer et al. 2025). TOPCon, an even higher-efficiency cell
architecture, captured most of the global market during 2022–2024 (Fischer et al. 2025).

 19

TOPCon most commonly uses n-type wafers and a higher-quality tunneling oxide layer on the
bottom that is covered by a heavily doped polycrystalline silicon layer deposited by plasma-
enhanced chemical vapor deposition or low-pressure chemical vapor deposition (Glunz et al.
2021). Typical commercial cell efficiencies jumped from 23% in 2022 to 25% in 2024 as the
market switched from PERC to TOPCon (Fischer et al. 2025). While the steps for making
TOPCon cells differ slightly, the process uses much of the same manufacturing equipment as
PERC cell processing lines already in place. In addition to efficiency benefits, it is not
uncommon to find TOPCon cells priced lower than PERC cells in 2025 (InfoLink Consulting
Co. 2025), although our analysis indicates this is due to severe TOPCon cell overcapacity in
China.

Demand for PERC cells remains in the United States. Because it is an older technology, PERC
has more data around outdoor field performance, which can reduce uncertainty around lifetime
system performance. Also, at least one U.S. cell producer has said it is faster to get equipment
and ramp capacity when choosing PERC over TOPCon (Card 2024), which can be critical when
considering incentive expiration dates.

In addition, controversy around patents and IP rights for TOPCon limit its adoption. First Solar
and Maxeon (a spinoff from SunPower) began to challenge the legality of imports based on
TOPCon technology in 2024 (Pickerel 2025d). Only one U.S. cell producer (Talon PV) is known
to have licensed the technology from First Solar in 2024. As of the first half of 2025, it is
unknown whether the First Solar (Tetrasun) and Maxeon patents are legally all-encompassing
enough to disqualify all TOPCon modules, because there are many variations in metallization
and layer sequencing, doping, and deposition methods (e.g., low-pressure chemical vapor
deposition or plasma-enhanced chemical vapor deposition). Several Chinese manufacturers being
sued by First Solar and Maxeon have also filed lawsuits concerning TOPCon solar, alleging
patent infringement. For example, Trina Solar has sued Canadian Solar for $1.5 billion (V. Shaw
2025d), Jinko Solar has sued LONGi (V. Shaw 2025b) and Waaree Energies (V. Shaw 2025c),
and JA Solar has sued Astroenergy (Bellini 2024). The IP around each cell architecture creates
opportunities for some manufacturers but may limit other manufacturers attempting to compete
with demand for high-performance PV.

2.4.2 Domestic Production of Crystalline-Silicon Cells
PV Tech reported that, in 2024, the United States produced 0.2 GWdc of c-Si cells, with a year-
end U.S. manufacturing capacity of 1.5 GWdc (BNEF 2025b; PV Tech Research 2025); 15 NLR
estimates that there was an additional 6.3 GWdc of manufacturing capacity under construction, of
which 1.4 GWdc came online in the first half of 2025. 16

15
 Nameplate manufacturing capacity assumes operation ongoing 24 hours per day, 7 days per week, 365 days per
year. Utilization rates account for the labor rates, maintenance downtime, etc., which result in less than full-capacity
production.
16
 NLR estimates for manufacturing capacity operating or under construction as of Q2 2025 represent values to the
best of our knowledge, as some companies do not publicize their activity.

 20

The inset of Figure 14 shows operational capacity and capacity with financing secured and under
construction as of Q2 2025, organized by the locations of company headquarters. 17 Cell
production in the United States was dormant from 2021 to the second half of 2024. Suniva’s
once-idled facility in Georgia completed retooling in 2024 and produced approximately 200
MWdc of PERC cells. Many cell companies announced plans to establish U.S. facilities after the
45X MPTC and criteria for the domestic content bonus credit were announced in 2022. In the
most recent January 2025 guidance from the Internal Revenue Service (IRS), the value of
domestic cell conversion (even on an imported wafer) is 40% greater than the value of all
balance-of-module materials combined (U.S. Internal Revenue Service 2025b).

 Figure 14. U.S. PV cell capacity, production, and ownership.
 Source: (BNEF 2025b; PV Tech Research 2025).

2.4.3 Imports/Exports of Crystalline-Silicon Cells
As shown in Figure 15, the United States imported 13.8 GWdc of c-Si cells in 2024, up 274%
year over year, with 96% of the capacity coming from ASEAN (64%) and South Korea (32%).
The countries of origin for U.S. cell imports differ substantially from the global distribution of c-
Si cell production (Figure 5) owing to tariff and AD/CVD considerations. Imported cells from
South Korea would have some of the lowest duties, and the largest c-Si module assembly firm in
the United States (Qcells) is headquartered in that country. Qcells had an annual cell capacity of
7.6 GWdc in South Korea in 2024 (BNEF 2025b), and 4.4 GWdc of cells were imported from

17
 The location of company or parent company headquarters is organized as “U.S. HQ,” “China HQ,” or “Other HQ”

acquired any assets from a company headquartered in a different country, whether most of the company’s operations
are in a different country from the headquarters of record, or the nationalities of the company’s owners.

 21

South Korea into the United States that year. Through Q3 2024, there had been five consecutive
quarters with greater than 20% growth in cell imports and module assembly, with most of the
growth in cell supply from Thailand and Malaysia. Quarterly shipments from Q3 2024 to Q1
2025 have remained relatively flat at around 4 GWdc.

As growing U.S. module assembly operations increased demand for cells in 2024, the U.S. cell
tariff rate quota was surpassed. The tariff rate quota allows duty-free cells until the total volume
of imports into the United States exceeds a certain capacity per year (with the year starting
February 7 and ending February 6 of the next calendar year). It was originally set at 2.5 GWdc in
2018 but was raised to 5 GWdc in 2022 when the Section 201 duties were extended. The 5-GWdc
annual cell tariff rate quota was almost reached in 2023 and was passed for 2024 within the first
half of the year. To avoid increased costs to U.S. module assembly operations, the tariff rate
quota was extended to 12.5 GWdc. That higher mark was reached on December 30, 2024. The
annually reset 12.5-GWdc limit for duty-free cell imports was reached in July 2025. Once the cell
tariff rate quota is met, the Section 201 duties are 14%, on top of any other duties or tariffs. For
example, the AD/CVD on imported cells is 8.6% for most cells shipped from Malaysia and
125% for shipments from Cambodia (U.S. International Trade Administration 2025b). Only a
trace of imports from Cambodia can be found within the U.S. trade data in Q3 2024. Cell imports
from Malaysia and other countries in ASEAN, including Indonesia and Laos, increased
substantially in 2024. Section 201 duties are set to expire on February 7, 2026. AD/CVD rates
are reviewed on a rolling basis by the U.S. Department of Commerce International Trade
Administration (U.S. International Trade Administration 2025b).

 Figure 15. U.S. imports of c-Si PV cells.
 Source: (Feldman, Zuboy, Dummit, Stright, et al. 2025)

In 2024, 1.3 MWdc of c-Si cells were exported from the United States, primarily bound for
Mexico (33%), Canada (15%), the United Kingdom (12%), Guatemala (10%), and Spain (4%).
Exports to South Korea, China, ASEAN, and India were only around 5% of total U.S. cell
exports in 2024. The small amount of U.S. cell exports could have been primarily for product

 22

research and testing. A U.S. cell counts significantly toward the domestic content bonus credit,
and this could deter exports in the future as well.

2.4.4 Regional Costs for Crystalline-Silicon Cells
Figure 16 shows NLR-calculated PERC cell costs by region. Outside China, variations in
equipment and facilities CapEx as well as materials costs are driven by distance from suppliers,
trade policies, manufacturing experience, and engineering, procurement, and construction (EPC)
costs. The materials and equipment for cell manufacturing can be entirely Chinese supplied,
although some materials are available from ASEAN and some manufacturing equipment from
the European Union. China’s supply chain advantages drive their cost advantage. All countries
outside China are relatively inexperienced, which also impacts EPC costs, and the United States
and Europe have higher labor and management costs for EPC.

The contributors to direct cost of goods sold vary by location. In the United States, Section 301
tariffs on some materials supplied from China can add 25% (see Section 2.4.5). The United
States and Europe have significantly higher labor costs because of higher labor rates and more
total employees (from using more shifts). Equipment CapEx is highest in the United States
owing to small scale and redesign according to standard U.S. operations and safety guidelines
from Underwriters Laboratory (UL). Building and facilities CapEx is also highest in the United
States because of higher EPC labor costs as well as additional permitting time and expense for
using large amounts of wet chemical solutions and specialty gases. To translate between CapEx
and depreciation, which we categorize as a direct cost in Figure 17, we assume a 5-year useful
lifetime for equipment and 20-year useful lifetime for the manufacturing facility.18

 Figure 16. Solar cell manufacturing costs by point of origin in 2024

18
  These assumptions may be too long considering the possibility of technology obsolescence and/or restructuring of
company assets (e.g., moving to a new cell technology and writing down old equipment as an impairment loss). For
these and other reasons, actual depreciation expenses could differ from Figure 16 and similar figures elsewhere.

 23

Overhead—including research and development (R&D) and selling, general, and administrative
(SG&A)—is typically a larger relative cost for smaller companies and companies with higher
salaries for R&D, sales, and administrative staff, such as those in the United States and Europe.
Financing and profit requirements are also greater in the United States and Europe, because the
risk is higher vs. other investment opportunities. Following advice from U.S. PV manufacturing
stakeholders, NLR assumes gross margins between factory gate price and direct cost of goods
sold are greater for U.S. projects than for projects in China, ASEAN, or India.

Figure 17 adds wafer costs (see Section 2.5) to the cell conversion costs from Figure 16. It
includes a separate column for Section 301 tariffs on materials from China. In 2024, Section 301
tariffs would have added 25% to the price of materials and equipment from China, but tariffs on
most cell equipment were exempted beginning in September 2024, retroactive to May 2024.
Therefore, Section 301 tariffs on cell equipment are not included in our cost analysis. On May
31, 2025, the 1-year tariff exemption for cell equipment from China was extended to August 31,
2025. 19 Its value is around $3.7/kWdc as equipment depreciation. The relevant tariffs on cell
materials are reflected in Figure 16.

 firms.
Section 301 tariffs do not apply to wafers but apply to about 50% of cell materials. The U.S. facilities are assumed to
 be smaller scale (<2 GWdc) compared with ASEAN and Indian facilities (>5 GWdc).

2.4.5 Upstream Inputs for Crystalline-Silicon Cells
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to the c-Si cell fabrication process.
Many upstream inputs are not discussed here owing to limited supply chain information or
relatively low importance (for inputs such as aluminum paste, argon, and nitric acid).

 In August 2025, the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative extended the exclusions another three months to
19

November 29, 2025.

 24

 • Silicon wafers: See Section 2.5.
 • Silver paste: Silver paste for screen-printing cell contacts typically contains silver
 powder, glass frit, and solvent (Zhao et al. 2024). Glass frit content is higher in front-side
 paste to create etching during firing (Thirsk 2011), and it is usually a PbO-based
 borosilicate glass (Fields et al. 2016). Interviews with industry in early 2025 indicated
 that silver and aluminum pastes must be imported from China or Southeast Asia. In 2024,
 U.S. silver demand was 30% for physical investment (bars), 29% for electronic and
 electrical applications, 12% for PV, 12% for jewelry and silverware, 6% for brazing and
 solder, and 7% for all other applications, including photography (U.S. Geological Survey
 2025m). Industry interviews indicate that Dupont is establishing a U.S. supply of silver
 paste for c-Si PV module applications.
 • Process chemicals: Interviews with industry in early 2025 indicated that U.S. suppliers
 are generally used for process chemicals such as SiH4, H2, N2, TMAl, POCl3, BBr3, KOH,
 and HCl. Imports are unnecessary and can be hazardous to ship. However, raw precursors
 (e.g., KOH pellets or concentrated HCl) may be imported, and HF is generally imported.
 • Equipment: U.S. cell manufacturers report needing to import manufacturing equipment
 from China or Europe. European equipment was reported to have similar or slightly
 higher costs even if the 25% Section 301 tariffs on Chinese equipment were in effect.
 Cell patents affect the selection of manufacturing equipment (see Section 2.4.1). Cell
 manufacturers need access to variable material choices for each cell layer, different
 diffusion and deposition methods, and so forth to reduce litigation risk. Cell IP and
 equipment engineering and procurement issues are believed to be key vulnerabilities.
Table 2 summarizes estimated costs for materials and equipment in the United States and Asia,
and the availability of priority inputs for PERC cell production. The data shows cell materials costs
in the United States are around 50% higher than cell materials costs within Asia, and equipment
costs are closer to twice as high. Section 301 tariffs can account for 25% of the difference between
metallization paste costs, while the rest of the difference is a combination of smaller scale for U.S.
manufacturers, greater processing costs for U.S. suppliers, and possibly higher profit margins.
