2.5 Ingot/Wafer
This section focuses on c-Si ingot and wafer production, excluding polysilicon, which is the
focus of the next section. A simplified supply chain diagram for monocrystalline silicon ingot
and wafer production by the Czochralski (Cz) process is shown in Figure 18, based on a process
previously described (Basore and Feldman 2022a; Woodhouse et al. 2019).

 26

Figure 18. Simplified supply chain diagram for monocrystalline silicon ingot and wafer production

2.5.1 Technology Trends for Ingots and Wafers
Most PV ingots and wafers were multicrystalline until 2017, after which monocrystalline wafers
quickly became dominant, due in part to higher-efficiency monocrystalline cell technologies
(Fischer et al. 2025). Until 2022, monocrystalline cells primarily used p-type wafers, which cost
less than their n-type counterparts. However, n-type cells can be more efficient than p-type cells,
and automation, improved dopant control, and larger ingots have reduced n-type wafer costs. For
this reason, n-type monocrystalline wafers are projected to have greater than 90% global market
share beyond 2025 (Fischer et al. 2025). PERC cells based on p-type wafers continue to
represent a significant portion of U.S. imports and domestic production (see Section 2.4.1).
Another efficient ingot-production practice has also become more common: increasing the
silicon mass within the ingot crucible by adding fluidized bed reactor (FBR) polysilicon pellets,
which fill voids between Siemens polysilicon chunks. The resulting higher charge weight helps
lower the total cost of ownership for manufacturers by producing a greater ingot mass from each
loading cycle.

2.5.2 Domestic Production of Ingots and Wafers
Figure 19 reports PV wafer capacity and production in the United States between 2013 and 2024.
The inset shows capacity with financing secured and under construction as of Q2 2025,
organized by location of company headquarters. 20 While no ingot and wafer production appears
from 2015 through 2024, some U.S.-headquartered wafer interests—including startup and R&D-
focused CubicPV and equipment supplier Applied Materials—existed during that time. A history
of U.S. manufacturing during 2013–2020 is available (Basore and Feldman 2022b).

20
 The location of company or parent company headquarters is organized as “U.S. HQ,” “China HQ,” or “Other HQ”

acquired any assets from a company headquartered in a different country, whether most of the company’s operations
are in a different country from the headquarters of record, or the nationalities of the company’s owners.

 27

The ingot and wafer 45X MPTC became available in 2022, and Corning and Qcells began
constructing facilities in 2024. 21 Based on BNEF data, industry interviews, and publicly
available data, of the 10 GWdc of NLR-estimated domestic ingot and wafer capacity under
construction as of Q2 2025, 5 GWdc could come online by the end of 2025, reaching 10 GWdc in
2026. 22

We estimate there is a high probability that ingot and wafer production will constitute the largest
gap between U.S. PV supply and demand for at least two more years. The scheduled 45X MPTC
and domestic content bonus credit criteria help make the case for U.S. domestic wafer
manufacturing, but they are less supportive than incentives for solar cells (U.S. Internal Revenue
Service 2025b). Moreover, CapEx for ingot and wafer facilities that could come online in 2025 is
not expected to be paid off before the scheduled end of the 45X MPTC and domestic content
bonus credit.

 Source: (BNEF 2025b; PV Tech Research 2025)

2.5.3 Imports/Exports of Ingots and Wafers
Trade data on PV wafer imports are insufficient to determine the origin of imported PV wafers,
and the United States did not produce PV wafers in 2024 to export. 23 China hosts the vast
majority of global wafer manufacturing. However, the array of U.S. tariffs on Chinese PV
products probably means U.S. cell manufacturers are purchasing wafers from other regions, most

 CubicPV was developing a 10-GWdc U.S. wafer manufacturing facility but shifted to developing tandem modules.

likely Southeast Asia. PV Tech estimates that Southeast Asian countries produced 25.0 GWdc of
PV wafers in 2024 from a manufacturing capacity of 56.8 GWdc, growing from 1.9 GWdc
produced in 2021 from a manufacturing capacity of 2.9 GWdc. India grew its wafer
manufacturing capacity to approximately 2 GWdc by the end of 2024, but industry interviews
indicate these facilities were idled in the first half of 2025 owing to the low-cost global
marketplace (PV Tech Research 2025). Cell manufacturers in the United States are now setting
up supply chains to use domestic wafers. Qcells finalized a DOE loan guarantee for a plant to
begin operations in 2025 producing 3.3 GWdc of ingots, wafers, cells, and modules (Anselmo
2025). Cell manufacturer Suniva and module manufacturer Heliene announced a deal in March
with polysilicon and wafer manufacturer Corning to produce an all-domestic PV module (Suniva
2025).

2.5.4 Regional Costs for Ingots and Wafers
Figure 20 shows NLR-calculated costs for ingot and wafer production by region. The equipment
and materials for ingot and wafer manufacturing have primarily become centered in China; thus,
China has the lowest equipment and material delivery costs. Outside China, variations in
equipment and facilities CapEx and materials costs are driven by distance from the principal
suppliers, trade policies, manufacturing experience and workforce capabilities, and highly
variable EPC costs.

Section 301 tariffs on materials from China, excluding polysilicon, add 25% to U.S. costs. Most
ingot and wafer equipment was exempted from 25% tariffs in September 2024, retroactive to
May 2024, so these tariffs are not included in our cost analysis. On May 31, 2025, the 1-year
tariff exemption for solar wafer equipment from China was extended to August 31, 2025. 24 It has
around $3/kWdc of value assuming 7-year linear depreciation of CapEx for ingot and wafer
equipment. Equipment typically must be redesigned for U.S. customers who must meet UL
electrical requirements.

24
  In August 2025, the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative extended the exclusions another three months to
November 29, 2025. We calculate that the exemptions have approximately $2.6/kWdc of value as equipment
depreciation and $4.4/kWdc as materials costs.

 29

 Figure 20. Ingot and wafer production costs (excluding polysilicon) by point of origin in 2024
All countries outside China have higher EPC costs. The most important reason is scale. Some
ingot and wafer plants in China already produce 50 GWdc of wafers per year (or more), while
typical plants elsewhere are around one-tenth that size. In addition, facility designs differ owing
to different local permitting requirements, and the United States and Europe have higher EPC
labor and management costs as well as significantly more expensive building materials.

Wafer cost of goods sold calculations are most sensitive to labor and utility rates. Labor costs in
the United States and Europe are highest because of higher annual labor rates and possibly more
total employees (if using more shifts). We assume energy intensity is the same for all locations,
but local utility rates vary widely. High utility rates are particularly challenging for Europe.

Outside China, an investment in new ingot and wafer capacity at the minimum 5-GWdc scale
equated to a capital raise of $600–$800 million in 2024. Global spot prices of $0.15–$0.20 per
wafer ($18–$25/kWdc) were not uncommon in 2024, even including the polysilicon. Such prices
would not cover the cumulative cash costs for polysilicon-plus-wafer production.

In 2024, we estimate financing costs were lowest in China, ASEAN, and India and highest in the
United States and Europe owing to attractive returns offered from competing, lower-risk
investments. Higher CapEx and interest rates in the United States and Europe make financing
and profit costs much higher in those locations than in China, ASEAN, and India. Figure 21
includes polysilicon costs and tariffs in the wafer cost comparison.

 30

 Figure 21. Modeled price for wafer production and delivery to U.S. cell manufacturers in 2024.
 The U.S. facilities are assumed to be smaller scale (<2 GWdc) vs. ASEAN and India (>5 GWdc). Section 301 tariffs
 apply to an estimated 100% of ingot and wafer consumables. Finished product is an M10 wafer (247-mm diameter
 ingots for 182-mm wafer width).

2.5.5 Upstream Inputs for Ingots and Wafers
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to the c-Si wafer production process.
Many upstream inputs are not discussed because of limited supply chain information or
relatively low importance (for inputs such as dopants, glue, and chemicals).

 • Polysilicon: See Section 2.6.
 • Quartz crucibles: The Quartz Corporation and Sibelco mines in Spruce Pine, North
 Carolina, provide some of the world’s highest-purity quartz, which is used in some high-
 temperature quartz crucibles. However, quartz crucibles for PV are almost exclusively
 produced in China. French-headquartered Saint Gobain makes quartz crucibles for
 semiconductor applications, which typically use smaller ingots than PV does.
 • Diamond wire: Chinese companies dominate diamond wire production for large-scale
 PV. In the past, there were suppliers headquartered in Japan (Asahi, Nakamura), Europe
 (Meyer Burger), and the United States (Diamond Materials Technology in Colorado,
 which was acquired by Meyer Burger).
 • Equipment: Chinese companies dominate PV ingot and wafer production equipment.
 Japan, South Korea, and Taiwan have semiconductor ingot and wafer equipment
 suppliers. Semiconductor and large-diameter PV ingot and wafer equipment is available
 in Europe (e.g., PVA TePla, Lapmaster, Wolters, Rena), but European manufacturers
 have been unable to finance scale-up because of uncertainty about demand for non-
 Chinese products. In the past, U.S.-headquartered Applied Materials periodically sold
 cropping and squaring and wafer slicing equipment from 2007 to 2015, from their

 31

 Precision Wafering Systems subsidiary in Switzerland. GT Advanced Technologies, also
 headquartered in the United States, designed, built, and sold Cz and multicrystalline ingot
 growth furnaces from around 2012 to 2016. Linton Crystal Technologies in Rochester,
 New York, sold Cz-growth equipment over many years since its formation in 1954. It is
 now part of Chinese-headquartered Dalian Linton and is the most recent firm with U.S.
 operations and capabilities to provide Cz, brick shaping, and wafering equipment for PV.
 At the end of 2024, the company’s U.S. presence was expected to come to a complete
         close by Q1 2026.
Table 3 reports materials and equipment costs for ingot and wafer production in the United
States and Asia as well as the availability of priority inputs. This step of the c-Si supply chain is
most reliant on China, considering manufacturing capacities in China and ownership of ingot and
wafer facilities in ASEAN. More than 98% of global ingot and wafer assets were Chinese owned
in 2024.

Table 3 relates to ingot and wafer production customized for PV. The semiconductor industry
uses many of the same technologies and skill sets to make ingots—sometimes silicon, other
times germanium (Ge) or III-V materials (e.g., GaAs or InP). The principal differences between
PV and semiconductor ingots and wafers are the materials, growth techniques, and end goal. One
PV cell uses the whole wafer, whereas a single semiconductor substrate can be smaller and still
be used to fabricate hundreds of devices. PV applications require larger wafers. While countries

 32

including Taiwan, Japan, South Korea, some European countries, and the United States have
kept pace in semiconductor supply chains and capabilities, PV-specific ingot and wafer
manufacturing and capabilities have been largely limited to China since at least 2015. Thus, the
materials and equipment for PV ingot and wafer production have become concentrated in China.

2.6 Polysilicon
This section focuses on polysilicon production for c-Si PV, discussing direct inputs except for
mg-Si, which is the focus of Section 2.7. Figure 22 shows a simplified supply chain for
polysilicon production based on previously published information (Basore and Feldman 2022a;
Woodhouse et al. 2019).

 Figure 22. Supply chain diagram for polysilicon production by the Siemens process

2.6.1 Technology Trends for Polysilicon
The purity of polysilicon determines customer demand and selling price. Electronic-grade
polysilicon is needed for the n-type wafers used in the highest-efficiency solar cells. Since 2023,
n-type wafers have captured the greatest market share for the PV supply chain (Fischer et al.
2025), so more stringent polysilicon purity requirements have become the industry standard. The
challenge for polysilicon producers serving the PV market is to refine mg-Si feedstock, which
has a purity of 98%–99% silicon (by mass), to be as pure as electronic semiconductor-grade
silicon, which is >99.999999999% (>11N) free from contaminant metals (Semi 2025).

The Siemens chemical vapor deposition approach has been the most common route for solar and
semiconductor polysilicon production since its invention in the 1950s. A Siemens chemical
vapor deposition chamber is a bell-shaped reaction vessel containing around 36–48 pairs of
silicon rods (“filaments”) arranged so the growth of ultrapure silicon occurs equally on each rod.
An intricate network of pipelines connects the distillation columns used to refine the
trichlorosilane and silicon tetrachloride (SiCl4) chemical intermediates to all the chemical vapor
deposition reactors in the factory. Once deposition is finished, the rods are removed from the
chemical vapor deposition reactors and broken into smaller chunks for shipping to customers.

FBR is the other approach to polysilicon production. Bypassing many steps in the Siemens
process, the FBR process goes directly from silicon precursor gases—most commonly silane,
SiH4 (not trichlorosilane)—to granules around 2 mm in diameter (Bernreuter 2012). FBR
granules can be used stand-alone to make PV ingots or—more commonly—to increase crucible
charge weight by filling in the gaps between much larger polysilicon chunks produced by the
Siemens process. The 2025 International Technology Roadmap for Photovoltaic reports a 90%
market share for Siemens and 10% market share for FBR in 2024, projecting a 72% share for
Siemens and 28% share for FBR in 2035 (Fischer et al. 2025).

 33

2.6.2 Domestic Production of Polysilicon
PV Tech reported that, in 2024, the United States produced 4.3 GWdc of polysilicon for the PV
sector, with a year-end U.S. manufacturing capacity of 39.3 GWdc, as shown in Figure 23. The
remaining capacity was idled or used to produce polysilicon for the semiconductor industry. The
inset of Figure 23 shows operational capacity for the semiconductor or PV industry and
operational capacity for only the PV sector as of Q2 2025, organized by company headquarters
location. 25 NLR estimates that U.S. polysilicon manufacturing capacity decreased approximately
8 GWdc in the first half of 2025 with the closure of REC Silicon. 26, 27

Domestic polysilicon production for the PV sector has been inhibited since China placed duties
on U.S.-produced polysilicon in 2015. Since 2021, U.S. trade policies against China (e.g.,
UFLPA) have created a bifurcated market: Japan, Taiwan, and Germany import U.S. polysilicon
for semiconductors, and Vietnam—where three of the four largest Chinese PV module
companies have set up wafer plants—imports U.S. polysilicon for PV wafers.

 REC Silicon announced it was shutting down its Moses Lake facility in the United States due to lack of buyers for

(Qcells), followed by the customer communicating that it could no longer wait for delivery of the product to meet
those requirements. The company also stated that it had no other U.S. customers and limited customers outside the
United States (Norman 2025b, 2025a).

 34

Domestic FBR polysilicon producer REC Silicon maintained a facility for future polysilicon
production until 2024, when purity concerns caused cancellation of its primary remaining supply
agreement with Qcells, its largest shareholder (Norman 2025b, 2025a). This represents an 8.8-
GWdc decline in U.S. polysilicon capacity from 2024 to 2025. With cancellation of the REC
Silicon contract, Qcells announced in January 2025 that it would procure polysilicon from OCI
Holdings’ factory in Malaysia for its U.S. wafer manufacturing operations in Georgia (Samora
2025). The REC Silicon facility in Moses Lake, Washington, switched to serving the battery
supply chain. The silane intermediate it produces can be used to make higher-energy-density
battery anodes, and the company has entered into a long-term agreement to supply U.S.-
headquartered battery materials supplier Sila beginning in 2025 (REC Silicon 2024). 28 At the
same time, U.S.-headquartered Highland Materials announced it could begin constucting an 8-
GWdc-equivalent polysilicon facility in Tennessee in 2026 (Jacobo 2025b).

As shown in Figure 23, Hemlock and Wacker combined could offer up to 31 GWdc of
polysilicon capacity to PV. Historically, however, the semiconductor industry has paid higher
average selling prices for polysilicon and has provided more stability in offtake agreements with
customers. As of Q2 2025, Corning (7 GWdc) and Qcells (3 GWdc) had the only U.S. ingot and
wafer plants under construction, which could demand U.S. PV polysilicon (see Section 2.5.2).
Corning is one of the principal owners of Hemlock. In April 2025, Corning, Suniva, and Heliene
announced they would offer the only PV module with U.S.-manufactured polysilicon, wafers,
and cells (Corning Inc. 2025; Suniva 2025; C. Shaw 2025; Pickerel 2024c).

2.6.3 Imports/Exports of Polysilicon
PV wafer production has not occurred in the United States since 2015, so there was probably no
significant U.S. polysilicon importation for PV in 2024 (although this is difficult to see explicitly
in the trade data). 29 Of the wafer capacity shown in Figure 19, Corning (which is projected to
begin production in 2025) is expected to use U.S. polysilicon, and Qcells announced it would
secure polysilicon for domestic wafer manufacturing from OCI Holdings’ factory in Malaysia
(see Section 2.6.2) (Samora 2025).

As shown in Figure 24, the largest potential source of imported polysilicon is China, although
U.S. PV wafer manufacturers would generally have less access to Chinese polysilicon because of
trade restrictions. Polysilicon is also available from at least two suppliers in other countries: OCI
in Malaysia, with 28 GWdc-equivalent, and Wacker in Germany, with 38 GWdc-equivalent
(BNEF 2025b).

28
 Despite its development and commercialization in the United States, effective January 2025, all global FBR
capacity was within China, controlled by GGL Poly (BNEF 2025b).
29
 From 2014 to 2024, approximately 2 kilotons (kt) of 99.99% pure silicon (HTS code 2804.61.0000) were
imported into the United States per year, mostly from Germany, Taiwan, and Japan, with an average price of
$60/kg—suggesting these imports were for semiconductors.

 35

 Figure 24. Global polysilicon capacity by country and production for PV, 2013–2024.
 Source: (PV Tech Research 2025)

As shown in Figure 25, the United States exported approximately 37 kt ($1.1 billion) of silicon
with 4N purity or greater in 2024, down 5% year over year. Silicon at this level of purity can be
used for a variety of applications, including PV and semiconductors. Approximately 21 kt (58%)
went to Vietnam and Malaysia, the most probable locations for PV-related sales because of the
growing amount of PV wafer manufacturing in those countries; the threefold growth in exports
to those countries roughly coincides with their ramp-up in wafer manufacturing.

PV Tech reported (PV Tech Research 2025) that the United States produced 11 kt of polysilicon
for PV in 2024, or more than half of the amount 4N purity or greater silicon that went to Vietnam
and Malaysia. All or most of this production was for export in 2024, because there was no U.S.
PV wafer production. Given U.S. restrictions on Chinese polysilicon due to the UFLPA, which
drove wafer manufacturing capacity in Southeast Asia, U.S. polysilicon may have been exported
and eventually returned to the United States within PV cells and modules. There was greater than
30 GWdc of PV wafer capacity in Malaysia and Vietnam in 2024 and 2 GWdc of wafer capacity in
Turkey by a company named Kalyon (BNEF 2025b). South Korea, Japan, and Germany have
minimal PV wafer capacity but are very active in the semiconductor industry.

 Figure 25. U.S. silicon exports (>99.99% purity).

2.6.4 Regional Costs for Polysilicon
Figure 26 shows NLR-calculated polysilicon prices by region. Expenses are calculated in terms
of dollars per kilogram for different manufacturing locations by using total yielded labor
intensity and energy use. These metrics are commonly measured by tonnes produced per year per
full-time employee (t/yr per FTE) and kilowatt-hours of electricity and steam equivalent per
kilogram of polysilicon produced (kWhac/kg). Natural gas may also be included for FBR. Local
labor rates (dollars per hour or equivalent annual salary per FTE) and utility rates (dollars per
kilowatt-hour ac from the grid or produced locally) vary by manufacturing location. Figure 26
incorporates location-consistent 70 t/yr per FTE labor intensity and 55 kWhac/kg electricity and
steam equivalent and location-dependent labor and utility rates that are covered elsewhere
(Woodhouse et al. Submitted).

The principal input material for polysilicon production is mg-Si, which varies significantly in
price across different periods and locations. Some information regarding mg-Si prices is publicly
available (Shanghai Metals Market 2025), although the price paid for mg-Si can vary
significantly from these spot price benchmarks and depends on volume. Moreover, polysilicon
producers located outside China typically pay significantly more to import mg-Si from China
(and pay shipping and tariffs) or pay more for the material from local suppliers.

This is the most energy-intensive step in the c-Si supply chain, and utilities are a major cost
driver for polysilicon production. Labor costs are also significant in the United States and
Europe. Recent data suggest labor costs are around 3 times greater in the United States and

 37

Europe vs. China. Overhead and financing costs are also greater in the United States and Europe.
Labor costs are approximately half in ASEAN vs. China.

 Figure 26. Factory gate modeled price for polysilicon production by the Siemens process ($/kg).
 Equipment depreciation and facilities represent greenfield investments.

The initial CapEx for greenfield polysilicon plants—including permitting-related expenses—is
significantly higher outside China, because EPC experience is greater in China, and equipment
and materials are all local. The combinations of scale and local supply yield CapEx for
polysilicon production equipment that is at least 15% greater than China in ASEAN, 75% greater
in the United States, and 50% greater in Europe. The CapEx differentials for all new building
and facilities costs in ASEAN and India are at least 10% greater than China and, owing to much
higher EPC expenses, perhaps up to 10 times greater in the United States and Europe (although
recent data are limited owing to the rarity of projects in the United States and Europe since
2014). Higher startup costs outside China are also expected because of longer design and
permitting timelines and higher construction soft costs.

The results of Figure 26 also depend on assumed facility size. The NLR polysilicon cost models
were set to represent the approximate average of the top 10 largest producers in China (200,000
t/yr), the largest ASEAN producer (OCI Malaysia, 35,000 t/yr), the largest U.S. producer
(Hemlock, 35,000 t/yr), and the largest European producer (Wacker Bavaria, Germany, 60,000
t/yr) in 2024. India was not analyzed because its production is much more significant
downstream—beginning with wafers—and in 2024 it had no known production of polysilicon.
The downstream cost model results reflect the more likely scenario in 2024 that PV
manufacturers in India were importing polysilicon to make wafers or would have imported
polysilicon within the imported cells for module assembly (Jacobo 2024d).

 38

2.6.5 Upstream Inputs for Polysilicon
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to the polysilicon production
process. Many other upstream inputs are not discussed here due to limited supply chain
information or relatively low importance.

 • mg-Si: See Section 2.7.
 • Graphite/carbon heating elements: Graphite heating elements and specialty gaskets are
 sourced from a mixture of foreign and domestic suppliers.
 • Hydrogen and hydrochloric acid: These common feedstock chemicals serve many
 research and industrial activities. The United States has a strong supply base for these
 chemicals, and local supply can be used. For example, the Dow Chemical plant in
 Midland, Michigan, is 13 miles from the Hemlock polysilicon facility. Another Dow
 Chemical plant in Knoxville, Tennessee, is 73 miles from the Wacker polysilicon facility.
 • Equipment: The supply chain for U.S. polysilicon equipment is local, because every
 manufacturer has its own unique approach and patents. This specialization is another
 reason for the higher U.S. CapEx.

$/kg. The net silicon utilization (a function of wafer thickness, kerf losses, all downstream yield
losses, and module efficiency) is used to convert $/kg to $/Wdc.

2.7 Metallurgical-Grade Silicon
This section focuses on the mg-Si step in the c-Si PV supply chain, including direct inputs to mg-
silicon production. A simplified supply chain diagram is shown in Figure 27.

 Figure 27. Simplified supply chain diagram for mg-Si

 40

2.7.1 Technology Trends for Metallurgical-Grade Silicon
Most mg-Si is produced through carbothermic reduction of silica sand (silicon dioxide) into mg-
Si and carbon dioxide in a submerged electric arc furnace (EAF). The silica sand is a high-purity,
low-iron, low-boron quartz sand. Advances have been made in the efficiency of the EAFs,
reducing energy demands from over 35 kWh/kg to less than 13 kWh/kg (Chen et al. 2018, 2017,
2016; Mirletz et al. 2024). Alternatives to the EAF include hydrogen reduction and using metals
as alternate reductants, but none of these are commercially viable (Hoover et al. 2024).

Multiple non-PV applications—including silicones, silanes, other semiconductors, and aluminum
alloys—also use mg-Si. Different applications have different purity requirements, and specific
mg-Si manufacturing approaches are not necessarily substitutable to another application. Many
facilities tune their process to a known incoming material stream and impurity profile, increasing
the barrier to substitution.

2.7.2 Domestic Production of Metallurgical-Grade Silicon
In 2024, mg-Si was produced in the United States by three companies: Ferroglobe, Dow
Chemical, and Mississippi Silicon. Dow Chemical and Mississippi Silicon are U.S. companies,
while Ferroglobe is headquartered in the United Kingdom. Table 5 and Figure 28 list U.S.
facilities, including those producing mg-Si for non-PV applications.

In 2024, U.S. mg-Si production capacity for PV polysilicon was about 74 kt/yr, out of 190 kt/yr
of total U.S. capacity. The 74 kt/yr could serve 35.7 GWdc/yr of polysilicon production, 30
significantly exceeding mg-Si demand from domestic polysilicon producers.

2.7.3 Imports/Exports of Metallurgical-Grade Silicon
While China controls more than 80% of mg-Si production (Figure 29), the United States imports
mg-Si primarily from Brazil, Canada, Norway, and Australia (U.S. Geological Survey 2025t).
Dow Chemical also owns assets in Brazil, which form a primary source of low-boron mg-Si for
polysilicon for Dow Chemical and Hemlock production in the United States (Brown 2018). The
U.S. reliance on mg-Si imports is less than 50% of apparent consumption (U.S. Geological
Survey 2025t). Depending on the purity criteria for mg-Si, 92–110 kt were imported in 2024
(U.S. Geological Survey 2025t; U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a).

 42

 Figure 29. Global production of mg-Si by region.
 Source: (U.S. Geological Survey 2025t)

Figure 30 displays mg-Si imports of 2N–4N purity, the level used to produce solar-grade
polysilicon. In 2024, 130 kt was imported, primarily from Brazil, Canada, and Australia. The
average customs value was $3.21/kg, with lower prices from ASEAN ($2.25–$2.75/kg) and
higher prices from leading import countries ($3.25/kg) (Figure 31).

 Figure 30. U.S. imports of 2N–4N mg-Si by country and year.
 Source: (U.S. Geological Survey 2025t; U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a)

 43

 Figure 31. 2024 U.S. imports of 2N–4N mg-Si by country fraction and average customs value.
 USD = U.S. dollars
 Source: (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a) HTS code 2804.69.1000

In 2021, a final affirmative antidumping determination was announced for mg-Si from Bosnia
and Herzegovina, Iceland, and Kazakhstan, applying additional duties of 21.41%, 37.83%, and
160%, respectively, to mg-Si containing 85%–99.99% silicon (and less than 4% iron) by weight
(U.S. International Trade Administration 2021). A new AD/CVD investigation was opened in
May 2025 against Angola, Australia, Laos, Norway, and Thailand for the two lower grades of
mg-Si (U.S. International Trade Administration 2025a). A Section 301 tariff of 50% applies to
mg-Si of greater than 4N purity from China (U.S. Trade Representative 2025). All silica-based
products from China’s Xinjiang region are banned by the UFLPA (U.S. Customs and Border
Protection 2021; U.S. Department of Homeland Security 2022).

2.7.4 Regional Pricing for Metallurgical-Grade Silicon
Ferroglobe reported that the U.S. average spot price for mg-Si was $3.97/kg in 2024 (Figure 32)
(U.S. Geological Survey 2025t). Import customs value totaled $417 million in 2024 for
approximately 130 kt, indicating an average import price of $3.21/kg, and a range of $2.32–
$3.40/kg, depending on country of origin. Chinese mg-Si prices in early 2025 averaged $1.31/kg,
while the European Union average spot price in 2024 was $2.72/kg (Ferroglobe 2025).

 44

 Figure 32. Spot prices of mg-Si in 2024.
 Source: (Ferroglobe 2025)

2.7.5 Upstream Inputs for Metallurgical-Grade Silicon
This section provides limited information on priority mg-Si inputs. Many upstream inputs are not
discussed here due to limited supply chain information or relatively low importance.

 • Quartz(ite): Also known as vein quartz, lump quartz, or quartz gravel. For PV
 applications, mg-Si needs low-impurity quartz (Brown 2018), which is typically mined as
 a rock and then crushed to 20–120 mm sand (Goodin 2025). This is different from the
 low-iron silica sand used in PV glass (see Section 2.8.5). While U.S. mg-Si is made from
 high-purity quartz sand, upgraded industrial-grade sand can be used for mg-Si with
 enough purification steps (Heidari and Anctil 2022). There may be competition for low-
 iron silica between mg-Si and PV glass production, but differing purity requirements and
 low levels of U.S. PV glass production reduce this risk.
 • Coke, coal, wet woodchips: These materials—readily available in the United States—
 provide carbon in the carbothermic reduction process of turning silicon dioxide (silica
 quartz sand) into mg-Si and carbon dioxide. If alternate technologies were pursued, such
 as hydrogen reduction of silica, demand for these materials would decrease.
 • Graphite electrodes: Graphite electrodes—made from needle coke and pitch (coal
 derivatives) then formed and baked into large rods—are consumed in the EAF during
 mg-Si production (GrafTech 2025). Excluding China, the world has about 786 kt of
 graphite electrode manufacturing capacity, which is higher than global demand
 (GrafTech 2025). A graphite electrode facility in Pennsylvania was idled in 2024
 (GrafTech 2025). Steel EAFs are the other major use of graphite electrodes, among other
 ferrous and nonferrous metal refining (GrafTech 2025). These electrodes require
 synthetic (not natural) graphite (Zhang et al. 2023). In 2024, the United States primarily
 imported graphite electrodes from India, China, Mexico, Poland, and Japan.
 • Equipment: Most U.S. mg-Si facilities are many decades old. One or two have had
 upgrades within the past two decades. Nominally, mg-Si production uses established

 45

 technology and processes with similar equipment used to refine other metals. However,
 owing to low equipment demand, expertise and capacity for equipment may be low.

2.8 Glass for Crystalline-Silicon PV
This section focuses on glass for c-Si module assembly, which is typically produced as rolled or
patterned glass and occasionally referred to as cover glass. Direct inputs to glass production are
also discussed in this section. A simplified supply chain diagram is shown in Figure 33.

 Figure 33. Simplified supply chain diagram for rolled glass used in c-Si modules.
 AR = antireflective

2.8.1 Technology Trends for Crystalline-Silicon PV Glass
Solar glass requires a special formulation of low-iron sand to increase light transmission. These
soda-lime-silica glasses contain silicon dioxide (65%–75%), sodium oxide (10%–20%), calcium
oxide (5%–15%), and smaller quantities of aluminum oxide, magnesium oxide, antimony oxide,
and cerium oxide (Thomsen et al. 2009; Chen et al. 2020). Iron oxide impurities must be kept
below 0.1%, preferably less than 0.04% (Thomsen et al. 2009).

Glass-glass modules are becoming the prevalent packaging scheme, capturing 40% market share
in 2023, although glass-backsheet modules are still used in residential and commercial
applications (Fischer et al. 2025). Glass thickness affects module durability. Front and back glass
are typically 2.3 mm thick for glass-glass modules. For glass-backsheet configurations, the front
glass is usually 3.2 mm thick (Fischer et al. 2024). In glass-glass modules, only the front-side
glass has an antireflective coating. Because the diffuse light reaching the rear side is not as
efficiently trapped by the antireflective coating, the coating is typically not worth the additional
cost (Ovaitt et al. 2024).

Traditionally, both front and back glass for c-Si modules are made through the rolled process (as
opposed to the float process used for CdTe front glass) (Fischer et al. 2024; Palmiotti et al.
2025). Rolled glass is also called patterned glass owing to the patterning imparted by the rolls.
Because of reliability testing standards, all glass used in PV is tempered or heat strengthened
regardless of thickness, but some of the thinner glasses may not break like safety glass

 46

(Silverman et al. 2024). There is a trend toward ultra-clear rolled glass for PV applications
(NextGen Energy Markets 2025).

2.8.2 Domestic Production of Crystalline-Silicon PV Glass
At least six U.S. companies produce glass, but in 2024 they did not produce for PV products.
They largely produced glass by the float process for use in automotive, architectural flat glass,
and other markets. If these companies did produce PV glass, they would need to compete with
large suppliers in China and ASEAN who offer pricing below U.S. costs. Float glass can be used
in c-Si modules, but module appearance and light transmission may differ from modules with
rolled glass. The cost and time required to establish greenfield rolled glass lines—or to convert
float glass lines to rolled glass lines—are unknown.

Several companies have announced plans to build U.S. glass facilities. One supply agreement
was announced between the Qcells module assembly factory in Georgia and a new glass line in
an undisclosed U.S. location that would be owned by Canadian Premium Sand. In 2024,
Canadian Premium Sand announced plans to build facilities in Manitoba, Canada, and repurpose
a facility in the southern United States to produce 4 GWdc of PV glass (Canadian Premium Sand
Inc 2025b, 2025a). This U.S. facility, in negotiation to be a joint venture, received 48C funds for
a tax credit allocation of $75 million. Canadian Premium Sand claims to be the only low-iron
rolled glass manufacturer in North America (Canadian Premium Sand Inc 2024). SolarCycle
announced plans for a rolled PV glass manufacturing facility associated with their recycling
facility in Georgia, supplied at least in part by the glass cullet recovered from recycled PV
modules (Pickerel 2024e). SolarCycle conducted a first successful test of this glass in 2025
(Norman 2025c).

Finally, Vitro Glass announced in January 2025 an expansion of its Wichita Falls, Texas, facility
with a new furnace and rolled glass production line, which is receiving a $67.6 million ITC
allocation. The company estimates the expansion will create 290 new jobs. No commissioning
date has been announced, but the facility is estimated to produce 25 million glass sheets annually
(Vitro Architectural Glass 2025; U.S. Department of Energy 2025a).

2.8.3 Imports/Exports of Crystalline-Silicon PV Glass
Because there was no domestic production of rolled glass for PV in 2024, module manufacturers
relied on imports. About 46 million m2 of rolled glass were needed to produce domestically
assembled PV modules in the United States during 2024, 31 including 31 million m2 of front glass
with antireflective coatings and 15 million m2 of rear glass without antireflective coatings. Figure
34 shows U.S. imports of tempered glass 32 from China for all applications, including PV cover
glass.

31
 This includes glass for 7.7 GWdc of c-Si modules. We assume half the market requires rear glass because—while
more than half of U.S. c-Si demand in 2024 was for bifacial modules—U.S. PV module assembly has historically
focused on monofacial modules for the higher-priced residential PV market. We also assume the 6.3 GWdc of CdTe
modules produced in the United States during 2024 used imported rolled glass as the rear cover glass.
32
 The HTS code that includes PV cover glass (7007.19.0000) also includes glass for non-PV applications and is
described as “toughened (tempered) safety glass, other than that of a size and shape suitable for incorporation in
vehicles, aircraft, spacecraft, or vessels.”

 47

The U.S. import data do not directly indicate how much rolled glass was imported for PV, but
some analysis is possible from rate provision codes. 33 Tempered glass with antireflective coating
for PV modules is exempt from Section 301 tariffs if the area is less than 2.5 m2 (U.S. Trade
Representative 2024a). As shown in Figure 34, tempered glass imports from China only
represented about 80% of U.S. demand for rolled glass for c-Si PV modules assembled in the
United States, before accounting for demand from glass applications other than PV. Thus, either
significant amounts of PV front glass were imported from countries other than China, or some
PV front glass was not tempered or heat strengthened sufficiently to be included under HTS
number 7007 (Palmiotti et al. 2025).

 Figure 34. Tempered glass imports from China into the United States during 2024.
                                       Source: (US ITC, n.d.) for 7007.19.0000

Figure 35 shows the total area of U.S. tempered glass imports by country. Total U.S. imports
from China, Malaysia, and Vietnam approach 67 million m2, while the total of all remaining
countries only approached 2 million m2. As mentioned earlier in this section, about 46 million m2
of rolled glass were needed for c-Si module assembly in the United States during 2024. This
indicates that PV applications may account for the majority (69%) of glass reported under HTS
number 7007.19.0000. Malaysia and Vietnam are the only countries other than China that
supplied more than 1 million m2 to the United States in 2024 (29 million m2 in total). This
indicates these countries likely provided a significant amount of front glass needed for 2024
module assembly in the United States, as indicated by the gap between market size and imports
for rate provision code 79 in Figure 34.

33
 Because glass for PV with antireflective coatings is exempt from Section 301 tariffs, imports of PV front glass are
probably reported within the rate provision code “79 - dutiable, no duty reported” segment, along with the value of
the only other Section 301 exemption for this HTS code, which covers screen protectors. Rear glass for PV, which
typically does not have antireflective coatings is probably reported with many other non-PV glass markets in the “69
- dutiable, duty reported” segment.

2.8.4 Regional Costs for Crystalline-Silicon PV Glass
After solar cells, glass is the second most expensive item in a PV module, especially for bifacial
modules that commonly (but not always) use similar front and back glass. In 2022 in China, PV
rolled glass production was growing explosively, reaching a capacity of 84 kt/day across 442
production lines (Shanghai Non ferrous Metals 2023). The average price of PV glass from China
in 2022 was $2.90/m2 and $3.75/m2 for 2 mm and 3.2 mm, respectively (Shanghai Non ferrous
Metals 2023). In early 2024, Infolink reported Chinese spot prices of $2.36/m2 and $3.62/m2 for
2 mm and 3.2 mm solar glass, respectively (InfoLink Consulting Co. 2025).

2.8.5 Upstream Inputs for Crystalline-Silicon PV Glass
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to rolled glass production. Many
upstream inputs are not discussed owing to limited supply chain information or relatively low
importance (e.g., for inputs such as dolomite, alumina, and material needed for antireflective
coatings).

 • Cullet: Cullet is clean, crushed glass fed back into glass manufacturing, offsetting virgin
 materials. Up to 30% cullet can easily be used in standard glass manufacturing, yielding

 49

 energy savings and reduced gaseous reaction products (Hubert 2015). Some
 manufacturers target 50% cullet (Devlin 2025). This cullet is primarily pre-consumer to
 reduce contamination risks. Post-consumer cullet is not yet used in PV glass, but
 SolarCycle is establishing PV recycling in conjunction with a new rolled glass
 manufacturing line, which would bring post-consumer cullet into the supply chain
 (Pickerel 2024e). Also see Section 7.1.
 • Low-iron silica sand: The United States is a leading global silica sand (95% purity)
 producer, producing 130 million t of sand and gravel in 2024, 7% of which went to glass
 making (U.S. Geological Survey 2025j). Not all this material is suitable for PV glass,
 because less than 0.1% iron oxide content is required, either from naturally low
 impurities or use of cleaning processes (Thomsen et al. 2009; Heidari and Anctil 2022).
 The geographic distribution of high-purity SiO2 deposits has resulted in illegal mining,
 mostly in Asia, but the U.S. resource of high-quality sands is advantageous (Heidari and
 Anctil 2022; United Nations Environment Programme 2019). Carefully separated cullet,
 both pre- and post-consumer, can help offset demands on this natural resource.
 • Soda ash and lime: The United States produces approximately 12 million t of soda ash,
 nearly half of which goes toward glass manufacturing, and imports very little (Bolen
 2022). The United States produces approximately 16 million t of lime, with minimal
 imports (U.S. Geological Survey 2025b).
 • Antimony: Antimony (Sb) is used as an additive—at a concentration of less than 0.01%
 (Thomsen et al. 2009)—in rolled glass production to control the oxidation state of iron
 for improved transmission and reduce bubbles (Hubert 2015; Chen et al. 2020; Blieske
 and Stollwerck 2013). Antimony oxide must only be used in rolled glass production and
 cannot be added to float production, because it would react with the tin bath to create tin
 oxide particles (Blieske and Stollwerck 2013; Thomsen et al. 2009). Cerium oxide 34 or
 arsenic oxide can substitute for antimony (Hubert 2015; Thomsen et al. 2009).
 • Equipment: Industry interviews indicated that turnkey rolled glass manufacturing lines
 are available for purchase in the United States.

34
 Cerium also absorbs hard ultraviolet light that would otherwise degrade PV cells and/or encapsulation.

 50

3 Cadmium Telluride Modules
3.1 Overview
Globally, CdTe modules represented approximately 2% of all PV modules produced in 2024 (PV
Tech Research 2025) and approximately 18% of U.S. PV installations (21% of U.S. utility-scale
PV installations) (U.S. Energy Information Administration 2025a). As of 2024, one company
(First Solar) was the only large-scale producer of CdTe modules.

In 2024, there was approximately 22.0 GWdc of CdTe PV manufacturing capacity globally (8.9
GWdc in the United States) and about 15.5 GWdc of production (6.3 GWdc in the United States)
(PV Tech Research 2025). The primary source of unutilized capacity was approximately 5 GWdc
of new capacity ramping in the United States and India. First Solar plans to expand U.S. and
global manufacturing capacity to 14 GWdc (all Series 7) and 25 GWdc, respectively, by 2026, and
sales to between 15.5 GWdc and 19.3 GWdc by 2025 (First Solar 2025a). First Solar indicates that
Series 7 modules produced in the United States have 100% domestic sources for components
listed in the U.S. Treasury domestic content guidance. While First Solar’s Vietnamese and
Malaysian CdTe module manufacturing facilities remain active, U.S. CdTe module imports from
those countries fell in 2024 by 15%. CdTe module imports from India increased in 2024, but
some of this increase may have been due to mislabeled trade data.

First Solar began shifting to its larger-format, more efficient, and frameless Series 7 modules in
2023. In 2024, more than half the modules produced in the United States were Series 7, but the
less efficient Series 6 model (which, unlike Series 7, has a bifacial option) constituted 62% of
global production. This percentage of Series 6 modules is expected to decrease in 2025 as
documented Series 7 facility announcements come online.

At the end of 2024, First Solar reported average module efficiencies of 18.2% for Series 6
modules and 18.9% for Series 7, down 1.7% and 0.4% year over year, respectively, and 2.5
percentage points lower than the average efficiency of c-Si modules installed in the U.S. in 2024
(First Solar 2025a). The company did not explain the lower efficiency, but it could relate to the
scale-up of manufacturing lines in 2024 or manufacturing issues reported in Q3 2024.

In general, balance-of-module components are imported for Series 6 modules, from trade
partners such as Malaysia, while components for Series 7 modules produced in the United States
come from domestic suppliers. There is enough domestic production of cadmium for 6 GWdc of
modules, but no tellurium or selenium refining exists in the United States. First Solar partners
with 5N Plus in Canada to refine U.S. tellurium and produce CdTe powder. Some of the
tellurium-containing residues 5N Plus refines come from Utah. However, Canada primarily
imports tellurium from China, the world’s leading producer, which recently placed export control
on tellurium and CdTe compounds. Most of 5N Plus CdTe powder to First Solar ships from
Canada, but it may come from manufacturing facilities in Germany as well.

3.2 Global Context
Figure 36a displays CdTe PV production and market share over time, showing global share
decreasing from 5% to 2% during 2015–2024 even as production increased. In 2024, 93% of
2024 net sales occurred in the United States, followed by 5% in India and less than 1% in France

 51

and other countries (First Solar 2025a). In the United States, CdTe constituted about 18% of all
U.S. PV installations and 21% of utility-scale systems in 2024 (Figure 36b). Residential and
other nonutility PV systems are not examined owing to their minimal use of CdTe PV. 35

 Figure 36. (a) Global CdTe production and share, 2015–2024; (b) U.S. utility-scale PV installations
 by module technology, 2015–2024.
Sources: (a) SPV Market Research 2016–2025 (Mints 2025); (b) U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA), Form
 860 (U.S. Energy Information Administration 2025a)

3.3 Monolithically Integrated Module Production
This section focuses on the monolithically integrated CdTe module production. Unlike c-Si
module production, which combines individual cells in strings, CdTe modules are composed of
one solid unit of cells (i.e., monolithically integrated) directly deposited on the front glass. This
section only discusses U.S. company First Solar, because it is the only large-scale producer of
CdTe PV. Direct inputs to CdTe module production are discussed, except for CdTe powder and
glass, which are the focus of subsequent sections. Figure 37 shows a simplified supply chain
diagram based on NLR research (Smith, Woodhouse, Horowitz, et al. 2021a; Scarpulla et al.
2023) as well as more recent documentation of module composition (First Solar 2024e).

35
  Given the volume of CdTe modules produced and sold by First Solar over the past several years and the weighting
in revenue toward the United States, EIA may be underrepresenting U.S. CdTe installed capacity. For example, EIA
reported 7.8 GWdc of CdTe installed in 2024, while global First Solar production has exceeded that level since 2021,
reaching 12 GWdc in 2023 and 15 GWdc in 2024. The three most likely explanations are: (1) CdTe modules are
combined with c-Si panels in systems only reported as using c-Si panels. While 600 MWdc of plants reported
combined use in 2024, discussions with some developers indicate this number may be higher. (2) U.S. developers
are stockpiling First Solar modules for use in future systems. Given the previous uncertainty in tax credits,
developers may have purchased 5% of the cost of future PV systems to safe harbor projects over the next 4 years.
(3) First Solar has reported warranty claims, so some production could be module replacements (or repowering of
existing facilities).

 52

 Figure 37. Simplified supply chain diagram for CdTe module production

3.3.1 Technology Trends for CdTe Modules
Because First Solar is the only large-scale producer of CdTe PV, this section focuses on First
Solar’s technology trends. In 2024, 62% of First Solar’s global production was for the Series 6
model, while 38% was for Series 7 (Figure 38). The Series 7 model is larger and more efficient
but does not have a bifacial option, unlike the Series 6 (Table 7). Industry interviews indicate
that the Series 7 platform is intended for monofacial and bifacial products, but monofacial
products were launched first given the rapid capacity expansion. 36 Another primary distinction
between the two products is the frame and mounting format (Table 7). Series 7 modules are
technically frameless but include steel rails for mounting, while the Series 6 has an aluminum
frame that incorporates a mounting mechanism.

At the end of 2024, First Solar reported average module efficiencies of 18.2% for Series 6
modules and 18.9% for Series 7, down 1.7% and 0.4% year over year, respectively, and 2.5
percentage points lower than the average efficiency of c-Si modules installed in the U.S. in 2024
(First Solar 2025a). The company did not explain the lower efficiency, but it could relate to the
scale-up of manufacturing lines in 2024 or manufacturing issues reported in Q3 2024.

See Section 3.5 for CdTe glass trends. Detailed reports on CdTe PV technology development
(Scarpulla et al. 2023) and priority research trajectories (Meagley et al. 2025) are available.

36
  A module’s bifaciality factor is the ratio of power produced from the back side of the module to power produced
from the front side under the same lighting conditions. At 15%, the bifaciality factor for CdTe modules in 2024 was
lower than for c-Si PV modules. PERC-based c-Si modules, which are more common in the United States, have a
bifaciality factor around 70%. TOPCon-based c-Si modules, which hold the largest global market share, have a
bifaciality factor around 80% (Mittal 2025; First Solar 2023c).

3.3.2 Domestic Production of CdTe Modules
Figure 38 shows global production and capacity data for First Solar during 2015–2024 (PV Tech
Research 2025). In 2024, there was approximately 22.0 GWdc of CdTe PV manufacturing
capacity globally but with a historically low utilization rate (for First Solar) of 70%, resulting in
about 15.5 GWdc of module production (PV Tech Research 2025). 37 The primary source of this
underutilization was approximately 5 GWdc of new capacity ramping in the United States and
India. First Solar announced in the first half of 2024 that it will have 14 GWdc/yr of CdTe
module manufacturing capacity in the United States by 2026 (up from 11.2 GWdc at the end of
2024) (PV Tech Research 2025; First Solar 2024a).

37
  From 2015 to 2024, the global c-Si module manufacturing utilization rate was 58%, compared with First Solar’s
utilization rate of 88%.

 54

While most modules produced by First Solar in 2024 were Series 6, more than half the modules
produced in the United States were Series 7. This ratio is expected to increase in 2025 as the
Series 7 facility announcements documented in Figure 38 come online. First Solar indicates that
Series 7 modules produced in the United States have 100% domestic sources for components
listed in the U.S. Treasury domestic content guidance (First Solar 2024e). 38

Thin-film PV technologies produced in the United States qualify for a 45X MPTC with a value
of $170/kWdc for production and shipment of the wafer, cell, and module. Unlike c-Si PV
technologies, these steps occur in a single facility for CdTe PV. Section 3.3.4 presents the
impacts of these credits on manufacturing value. For the U.S. production of 6.3 GWdc CdTe PV
in 2024, this would correspond to a total 45X MPTC value of approximately $1.1 billion.

Based on press releases for the new U.S. facilities, we estimate the labor intensity for CdTe
module production to be approximately 200 full-time jobs per annual GWdc of U.S. production
(Kennedy 2023; First Solar 2022, 2021). This corresponds to approximately 1,300 total U.S. jobs
for 2024 U.S. production levels.

One domestic closure in 2024 was Toledo Solar (Norman 2024), which had purchased older
CdTe PV production equipment with the intention of initially producing 100 MWdc/yr of
smaller-format CdTe modules for residential and nonutility markets (Pickerel 2020).

3.3.3 Imports/Exports of CdTe Modules
Figure 39 shows U.S. PV imports other than c-Si 39 during 2022–2024 for any country supplying
over 50 MWdc of imports in 2024. Most U.S. imports originated from India, Malaysia, and
Vietnam, the three non-U.S. locations where CdTe modules are produced (Figure 38). While
First Solar’s Vietnamese and Malaysian CdTe module manufacturing facilities remain active,
U.S. non-c-Si module imports from those countries fell 15% from 2023 to 2024; over the same
period, imports of non-c-Si modules from India grew from less than 11 MWdc to over 1 GWdc,
which we attributable to the opening of First Solar’s 3 GWdc plant in India in early 2024. The
data in Figure 38 and Figure 39 suggest most of global CdTe production is being imported into
the United States. CdTe PV imports into the United States are not affected by the same tariffs
and restrictions as c-Si PV. CdTe PV is specifically excluded from Section 201 tariffs and is not
as directly affected by AD/CVD rates or UFLPA. There is high probability for demand in India
for Indian-produced CdTe PV, because the country has a large utility-scale PV market supported
by the government. CdTe PV is eligible for India’s Approved List of Models and Manufacturers
for project development and Production-Linked Incentive for PV manufacturing. India also has
tariffs on module and cell imports into India (40% and 25%, respectively) (Feldman, Zuboy,
Dummit, Stright, et al. 2025). Nevertheless, U.S. import data suggest Indian-produced CdTe

38
 The U.S. Treasury guidance lists components but not subcomponents, so, for purposes of the domestic content
bonus credit, it matters where the CdTe is processed to make cells, but not where the CdTe itself comes from.
39
 The HTS code that applies to CdTe modules (8541.43.0080) has been in use since 2022 (Feldman et al. 2024). It
describes “Photovoltaic cells assembled in modules or made up into panels, other,” which refers to PV technologies
other than c-Si of the type described by 8541.43.0010 (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025c). It therefore
includes PV technologies other than CdTe, although these are probably not present in large quantities. Given that
more than 1 GW of non-c-Si PV modules were imported into the United States from countries without a CdTe
facility, it is probable that some of these shipments were c-Si modules. The U.S. International Trade Commission
DataWeb does not report any U.S. exports for this code.

 55

modules are being shipped to the United States, perhaps because they command higher prices
there. Note that U.S. project developers can mix foreign CdTe modules with domestic CdTe
modules and still qualify for the full domestic content bonus credit.

3.3.4 Regional Pricing for CdTe Modules
This section updates the NLR CdTe manufacturing cost analysis published in 2022 (Basore and
Feldman 2022a). Models for CdTe PV production costs at different locations are shown in
Figure 40, and details are reported in Appendix A. Small cost differentials between the non-U.S.
manufacturing locations are primarily due to differences between local utility and labor costs.
General duties are applied to the ASEAN and India models to reflect costs to import into the
United States, while the United States model includes some Section 301 tariffs on materials or
components that are generally not sourced from domestic suppliers.

Overhead, financing, and profit are estimated from First Solar’s 2024 annual report (First Solar
2025a). The overall 44% gross margin reported by First Solar in 2024 included sales of modules
and government grants including 45X. Fleet average 20% gross margins were reported when
45X revenues were excluded. Because First Solar sold $857 million of 45X tax credits generated
during 2024 for $816 million, we calculate net 45X MPTC revenue to be approximately 95% of
the full value. This translates to $162/kWdc for U.S. CdTe module production.

First Solar documents report a global fleet average factory gate module selling price of
$299/kWdc in 2024, compared with an average sales cost of $239/kWdc, other operating expenses
including R&D and SG&A equal to $33/kWdc, and an additional $0.04/Wdc for shipping,
warehousing, and other costs (First Solar 2025b; 2025a).

3.3.5 Upstream Inputs for CdTe Modules
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to CdTe module production. Refined
CdTe powder and glass for CdTe PV are detailed in Sections 3.4 and 3.5. Many other upstream
inputs are not discussed owing to limited supply chain information or relatively low importance
(e.g., bypass diodes, pigtails, nitrogen, argon, photoresist, and packing material). Sources for
some of these inputs were documented in the 2019–2020 timeframe (Smith, Woodhouse,
Feldman, et al. 2021), but sourcing has evolved significantly since then.

 • Refined CdTe powder: See Section 3.4.
 • Glass: See Section 3.5.
 • Module frames or back rails: Aluminum frames are used for Series 6 modules, while
 steel back rails are used for the frameless Series 7 modules. Aluminum frames were
 imported from Malaysia for Series 6 modules produced in the United States (First Solar
 2025a). However, First Solar has a domestic supplier of steel rails for Series 7 modules
 produced in the United States. It was reported in August 2024 that longtime
 manufacturing partner Ice Industries will invest $6 million in a facility producing steel
 back rails for First Solar (Pickerel 2024d).
 • Encapsulant: First Solar modules are understood to use a polyolefin elastomer (POE)
 encapsulant (Wesoff 2020). Dow has been named as one POE supplier to First Solar
 (Semiconductor Today 2020). Other domestic suppliers of POE encapsulant for PV exist,
 such as H.B. Fuller (HB Fuller 2025), but it is unknown whether First Solar uses multiple
 suppliers.
 • Edge seal: Series 7 modules use edge sealant from domestic suppliers to meet 100% of
 the domestic content criteria determined by the U.S. Treasury (U.S. Internal Revenue
 Service 2025b). Table 1 in Section 2.3.5 provides information about the domestic supply
 of edge sealant.
 • Junction boxes: Series 7 modules use junction boxes from domestic suppliers to meet
 100% of the domestic content criteria determined by the U.S. Treasury (U.S. Internal
 Revenue Service 2025b). Table 1 in Section 2.3.5 provides information about the
 domestic supply of junction boxes.

 57

 • Equipment: German manufacturer Von Ardenne, a vacuum coating tool supplier, has a
 longstanding relationship with First Solar (Roselund 2017). While it has manufacturing in
 China, its vacuum coating tools are made in Germany. First Solar states in its 2024
 Annual Report: “Some of our manufacturing equipment is customized and sole sourced.
 If our manufacturing equipment fails or if our equipment suppliers fail to perform under
 their contracts, we could experience production disruptions and be unable to satisfy our
 contractual requirements.” First Solar sources its laser tools from LPKF Laser &
 Electronics, which is headquartered and manufactures solar equipment in Germany.

3.4 Refined CdTe Powder
This section focuses on the refined CdTe powder production step in the CdTe PV supply chain,
including direct inputs. Here, refined CdTe powder includes CdSeTe or CdZnTe compositions
used near the window layer or back surface fields in CdTe PV devices with arsenic or copper
doping (Scarpulla et al. 2023). Figure 41 is a simplified supply chain diagram for refined CdTe
powder production (Audet and Levitcharsky 2011; U.S. Geological Survey 2025e, 2025l,
2025n).

 Figure 41. Simplified supply chain diagram for refined CdTe powder production

3.4.1 Technology Trends for CdTe Powder
The material composition of commercially available CdTe PV has evolved to include CdSeTe
areas and CdZnTe in the absorber within approximately the past decade (Meagley et al. 2025).
Use of ZnTe as the rear contact developed over this time as well. Typical material purities
required for CdTe PV applications are around 5N–6N (Basore and Feldman 2022a). There are
some examples of arsenic doping used in place of copper doping (Meagley et al. 2025).

3.4.2 Domestic Production of CdTe Powder
We believe U.S. CdTe refining for PV does not occur at commercial scale. Most CdTe powder
consumed in the United States is imported from Canada. Therefore, this section summarizes the
domestic availability and production of its constituent materials.

The U.S. Geological Survey (USGS) estimated domestic production of primary refined cadmium
at 0.3 kt in 2024, which could support more than 6 GWdc of CdTe PV production. The amount of
secondary cadmium produced (recovered from nickel-cadmium battery recycling) is withheld,
because only two producing facilities operated during 2024 (U.S. Geological Survey 2025e).

 58

Similarly, USGS withholds data for domestic production of primary tellurium-containing
residues and primary selenium-containing residues, because only two producing facilities
operated during 2024 (U.S. Geological Survey 2025l, 2025n). USGS notes that no tellurium or
selenium refining exists in the United States, so all residues are exported for further processing.

First Solar has a partnership with 5N Plus to refine tellurium sourced from a Utah facility (Rio
Tinto) at the 5N Plus refinery in Canada, which is then reimported within refined CdTe powder
into the United States for use in First Solar manufacturing facilities (5N Plus 2022). Rio Tinto is
not believed to provide all the tellurium-containing residues 5N Plus processes (Pickerel 2025a);
they provide only about 1 GWdc-equivalent. Thus, 5N Plus likely uses tellurium from other
locations, which may include China (the leading global producer of tellurium). Some secondary
tellurium was recovered from recycled CdTe PV in the United States in 2024. However,
quantities are comparatively small, because few CdTe modules have reached their end of life
(U.S. Geological Survey 2025n), and some are landfilled (Meagley et al. 2025). First Solar
claims its modules are designed so more than 90% of the module material can be recovered for
reuse and that the company has 500 MWdc of recycling capacity (First Solar 2025b).

3.4.3 Imports/Exports of CdTe Powder
The United States has imported approximately 7 kt of “salts of inorganic acids” (which includes
CdTe powder) 40 annually in recent years. We estimate First Solar required approximately 0.5 kt
of refined CdTe powder to produce the 6.3 GWdc of modules it made domestically in 2024. The
average customs value of these imports was approximately $3/kg in 2024, which is much lower
than the estimated price for refined CdTe powder discussed in the following section. For these
reasons, we conclude that large volumes of less expensive compounds are reported under this
code, obscuring insights that could be drawn about CdTe powder exclusively (though some of
the discrepancy could be explained by stockpiling refined CdTe powder for future use).

5N Plus (a supplier of CdTe powder to First Solar) has production locations in Canada and
Germany. The company announced a 50% increase in its supply contract with First Solar during
2024 (5N Plus 2024), reaching 3 times the size of its contract in 2022 (Willing 2025). In early
2025, China placed export controls on tellurium and compounds such as CdTe and CdZnTe,
forcing exporters to obtain special approval from China’s Ministry of Commerce and General
Administration of customs (V. Shaw 2025a).

3.4.4 Regional Pricing for CdTe Powder
This section reports on constituent metal pricing, because direct pricing information for CdTe
powder is not readily available. Some metal markets show prices of $100–$200/kg for 5N–6N

40
  Per discussion with USGS staff, the HTS code covering refined CdTe powder was 2842.90.9000 prior to July
2025 (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025b). However, this code was not exclusive to CdTe; it was defined
to include salts of inorganic acids or peroxoacids (excluding double or complex silicates and aluminosilicates,
fulminates, cyanates, and thiocyanates). The U.S. International Trade Commission does not report export data for
this code. Data collected after July 1, 2025, will be split into two codes (U.S. International Trade Commission
2025d). The first, 2842.90.9010, describes selenide or telluride salts of inorganic acids or peroxoacids (excluding
double or complex silicates and aluminosilicates, fulminates, cyanates, and thiocyanates) and will likely enable
greater insight into CdTe powder trade patterns. The second, 2842.90.9050, describes all other salts of inorganic
acids or peroxoacids (excluding double or complex silicates and aluminosilicates, fulminates, cyanates, and
thiocyanates).

 59

cadmium (Shanghai Metal Market 2025e, 2025c), $100–$500/kg for 5N–7N purity tellurium
(Shanghai Metal Market 2025b, 2025f), and $60–$300/kg for 5N–6N purity selenium (Shanghai
Metal Market 2025a, 2025d). Based on industry input, NLR assumes $220/kg for solar-specific
CdTe powder in 2024, which translates to roughly $0.02–$0.03/W. Given the spread in metal
markets pricing, difference in price based on purity, and the 5N Plus 2024 reported adjusted
gross margin of 33%, 41 there may be additional variability within the marketplace. Insight into
pricing by country or region is not easily available, but U.S. producers that purify tellurium to at
least 99% purity or convert it to CdTe are eligible for an MPTC equivalent to 10% of production
costs (U.S. Department of Energy 2022).

3.4.5 Upstream Inputs for CdTe Powder
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to the refined CdTe powder
production process. Many other upstream inputs are not discussed owing to limited supply chain
information or relatively low importance, such as equipment for mining and refining.

 • Cadmium: USGS reports that cadmium is primarily used in the production of NiCd
 batteries, alloys, coatings, and pigments, and to a lesser extent in CdTe PV and CdZnTe
 detectors and imaging applications (U.S. Geological Survey 2025e). United States
 exports and imports of cadmium are reported in Figure 42 for HTS code 8112.69, which
 includes multiple forms of cadmium other than waste or scrap. Amounts and destinations
 of cadmium changed dramatically in 2024. Notably, U.S. imports from China and exports
 to India changed substantially in 2024.
 o Data for code 8112.69 from UNComtrade indicate that Canada only imported
 3,000 kg of cadmium from China and 3,000 kg from the United States in 2024
 (United Nations 2025). In 2023, UNComtrade shows Canada imported 3,000 kg
 of cadmium from the United States, 3,000 kg from Germany, 1,000 kg from Peru,
 and smaller amounts from several other countries. USGS reports Canada
 produced 1.7 kt of refined cadmium in 2024 and 1.8 kt of refined cadmium in
 2023, which may indicate significant Canadian production of primary or
 secondary cadmium (U.S. Geological Survey 2025e).
 • Tellurium: According to USGS, 60% of global tellurium consumed in 2024 was for PV
 applications, followed by thermoelectric devices (20%) and metallurgy (15%) (U.S.
 Geological Survey 2025n). The HTS code for tellurium is 2804.50.0020 (purity not
 specified). Figure 43 shows U.S. export and import data. For each year shown, Canada
 represents most exports and imports, although amounts vary significantly. Exports
 reported under this code may include copper telluride (the intermediate compound
 refined from copper slimes before further processing into purified tellurium) (U.S.
 Geological Survey 2025n).
 o UNComtrade only reports trade data for the HS code 2804.50, which includes
 both boron and tellurium. In 2024, UNComtrade shows Canada imported 0.4 kt
 from China under this code, followed by 0.2 kt from South Korea, and less from
 any other country (United Nations 2025). Similarly, 2023 shows Canada imported

 o The estimated amount of tellurium needed per GWdc of CdTe PV modules is 46 t

 • Selenium: In 2024, 40% of global selenium consumed was for metallurgy, including

 and import data are shown in Figure 44.
 • Zinc: Most zinc in 2024 was used to produce galvanized steel, followed by brass and
 bronze and other zinc-base alloys (U.S. Geological Survey 2025q). The zinc supply chain
 is discussed in Appendix B.

3.5 Glass for CdTe PV
This section focuses on glass production for the CdTe PV supply chain, including direct inputs.
Figure 45 shows a simplified supply chain diagram for CdTe front glass, which differs from c-Si
rolled glass in that it is float glass and has a transparent conductive oxide (TCO) made of
fluorine-doped tin oxide (FTO) in addition to an antireflective coating.

 62

 Figure 45. Simplified supply chain diagram for CdTe FTO coated front glass

3.5.1 Technology Trends for CdTe PV Glass
CdTe PV modules use front and rear glass, which do not have identical specifications.

Front glass: For First Solar products, the front glass acts as a superstrate, which requires a high
degree of flatness as well as a TCO coating on the superstrate side. To achieve the necessary
flatness, CdTe PV front glass is typically produced on a float line (Basore and Feldman 2022a).
The front glass is annealed and has requirements for flatness to accommodate the thin-film
layers, in addition to low iron content (for transparency). Heat strengthening of the glass occurs
after the high-temperature CdTe deposition step (Meagley et al. 2025). Prior to CdTe absorber
layer manufacturing, a thin-film TCO layer composed of FTO, indium tin oxide, and zirconium
oxide is deposited on the smooth side of the float glass (Wikoff et al. 2022; Preeti and Kumar
2025). The front glass also has an antireflective coating (opposite side from the TCO), made of
one or more metal oxides such as tin oxide (Guardian Glass 2021; Buskens et al. 2016; Thomsen
et al. 2009).

Rear glass. The rear glass serves primarily as a cover and does not require the same performance
and characteristics as the front glass superstrate. While there are bifacial CdTe modules, they do
not necessarily use an antireflective coating on the rear glass owing to additional cost (Ovaitt et
al. 2024).

Glass Thickness. Glass thicknesses vary for CdTe modules between front and back and across
series (Table 8). Glass is typically annealed before delivery to the module production facility,
where it undergoes heat strengthening during the CdTe deposition processes, per discussion with
industry. Typically, heat strengthening creates safety glass (Palmiotti et al. 2025). The thinnest
TCO glass offered by NSG, a primary supplier for First Solar, is 2.2 mm.

 63

3.5.2 Domestic Production of CdTe PV Glass
At least four U.S. facilities can or will soon provide front TCO glass for First Solar products
(Table 9). The capacity of each float line is assumed to be about 0.55 kt/day, or 200 kt/yr.

Based on Series 6 glass thickness, module size, and glass density, front and back glass demand
for CdTe PV is estimated to be 71 kt/GWdc, or approximately 10 million m2/GWdc. CdTe PV
production of 12 GWdc/yr is approximately equal to four float lines at 0.5 kt/day capacity. Five
lines are about equal to 14 GWdc/yr, which is First Solar’s 2026 U.S. production target.
