most likely imported (Smith, Woodhouse, Feldman, et al. 2021), though annual reports indicate
some front and rear glass was sourced from Vitro in 2023 (First Solar 2023a). Several Midwest
Vitro facilities were at end of life and slated for decommissioning, but an agreement with First
Solar enabled modernization and expansion of these facilities (Crowell 2023). Japan-
headquartered Nippon Sheet Glass Group is known to supply gigawatt-level volumes of TCO-
coated front glass from a float glass line in Ohio (Jacobo 2025c).

Around 21 float glass facilities are in the United States, of which approximately seven contain
two lines. This corresponds to 30 float glass lines in total (National Glass Association 2025). The
companies operating float lines are Vitro, Cardinal, Guardian, Pilkington NSG, NSG Group, and
AGC (Asahi Glass Company) (National Glass Association 2025). As indicated by the expansion
and investments into the lines in Ohio and Pennsylvania, it is possible to convert existing float
glass lines to TCO glass production if market drivers materialize.

3.5.3 Imports/Exports of CdTe PV Glass
As described above, domestic capacity of TCO float glass is sufficient to meet CdTe
manufacturing demand. The rear glass, which is not TCO-coated and can be rolled or float, may
be imported. See Section 2.8.3 for more information on glass imports and exports.

3.5.4 Pricing for CdTe PV Glass
First Solar has entered into multiyear agreements with NSG and Vitro to provide front glass, but
the price has not been disclosed. Bottom-up modeling estimated that CdTe front and back glass
cost approximately $0.053/Wdc or $9.42/m2 for a Series 6 module from Southeast Asia in 2020
(Smith, Woodhouse, Horowitz, et al. 2021b). This approximately aligns with front TCO glass
costs provided by Roth Capital (Philip Shen 2025) of $7–$9/m2, who also estimated $3–$5/m2
for rear glass. Because of the TCO coating, front glass is always more expensive than rear,
uncoated glass.

For solar applications, float glass is generally understood to be more expensive than rolled glass.
Discussions with U.S. float glass producers in 2021 indicated this is partly because the float line
must be slowed significantly to reduce impurity incorporation for high transmittance of sunlight
(Basore and Feldman 2022b). However, monofacial CdTe modules can tolerate more impurities
in rear glass, which may enable domestic float glass lines to supply rear glass at higher line
speeds and more competitive costs.

3.5.5 Upstream Inputs for CdTe PV Glass
This section provides some limited information on priority inputs for CdTe PV glass production.
Many other upstream inputs are not discussed here due to limited supply chain information or
relatively low importance such as dolomite, lime, pot ash, alumina, and material needed for
antireflective coatings.

 • Cullet: See Section 2.8.5. Cullet containing antimony oxide cannot be used in float glass
 production, but it can be used in rolled glass production.
 • Low-iron silica sand: See Section 2.8.5.
 • FTO: FTO is applied to manufactured float glass using chemical vapor deposition with
 fluorine and tin gases. Fluorine gas is sourced from fluorspar or fluorite, which the

 65

 United States primarily sourced from Mexico in 2024 (U.S. Geological Survey 2025g).
 Tin sourcing is described below. For FTO coatings, tin is introduced to the chamber in a
 chloride gas or organo-metallic gas form (Allendorf et al. 2006).
 • Tin bath: Float glass is produced by pouring molten glass onto a tin bath kept molten
 with electricity, ensuring a smooth surface. The tin bath can be 3–8 m wide, 50–60 m
 long, and 6 cm deep (Leftheriotis and Yianoulis 2012), implying 63–200 t of tin per float
 line. The tin bath must be kept in a reducing environment of nitrogen and hydrogen (3%–
 10%), and it is possible to implement a gas recovery and reuse system (Greg Morris
 2015). Tin consumption for float glass baths constitutes less than 26% of total domestic
 demand. We estimate the tin bath does not require frequent replacement. There is no U.S.
 mining or smelting of tin. Refined tin imports to the United States come primarily from
 Peru, Bolivia, Indonesia, and Brazil (U.S. Geological Survey 2025a).
 • Equipment: Equipment is readily available from several vendors, and new lines or
 upgrades can be implemented in less than 2 years (USGlass Magazine 2023c). Float line
 capacity can range from 50 to 1,200 t/day (Stewart Engineers 2024).

 66

4 Structural Components
4.1 Overview
This section covers supply chains for the structural elements used in PV systems. See Appendix
B for information on raw materials used in many structural products. The United States hosts a
growing base of domestic manufacturing for many PV system structural components,
particularly for utility-scale systems. Some elements—such as torque tubes, piles, and select
tracker assemblies—are widely produced domestically owing to their size and shipping
constraints. Others, such as fasteners and certain rooftop components, still rely heavily on
imports.

Major steel components used in utility-scale PV systems represent only a small fraction of total
U.S. steel consumption, which overall only has a 13% net import reliance. China is a major steel
producer as well as a dominant manufacturer of finished steel PV products. Given China’s scale,
low labor costs, and political strategy to export cheap goods, its products are often sold at a
significant discount in global markets. As a result, both raw and finished steel products from
China are part of some U.S. PV installations. However, the United States maintains upstream
strength through domestic iron ore production, and it has a large domestic capacity of steel
manufacturing as well as high tariffs on imported steel. Much of the steel used in PV
installations has a relatively low value-to-weight ratio, which favors a domestic supply with
shorter shipping distances. It is easier for PV suppliers to source steel tailored to individual
project specifications from domestic suppliers, plus there are incentives for PV systems that
depend on the use of domestically produced steel. We estimate that more than 75% of torque
tubes and driven piles are produced domestically using domestic steel. Rebar demand for PV is
comparatively small, limited to pads or drilled piers, and represents well under 1% of the
national rebar market, most of which is supplied domestically.

However, most generic steel fasteners are imported (more than 50% from Taiwan and China)
owing to their high value-to-weight ratio and the significant scale and cost advantage of foreign
supply chains. Generic fasteners used for PV represent a small part of the larger fastener
fabrication industry, so their market size would not justify the U.S. scale-up required to
overcome a 40% price premium. As a result, U.S. fastener manufacturers focus on niche, high-
specification products such as those produced specifically for trackers that are uniquely eligible
for the 45X MPTC and domestic content bonus credit.

Galvanized steel rails dominate utility-scale systems and are largely supported by U.S. roll-
forming capacity and abundant domestic iron ore. Aluminum rails and flashing/feet are used in
approximately 80% of residential and commercial rooftop PV, constituting roughly 1% of
aluminum consumed domestically. The United States has limited aluminum smelting capacity
and little mining of bauxite, the ore used to make aluminum. Thus, almost half of aluminum-
based products are imported, while half are domestically produced from primary smelters using
imported alumina and secondary smelters using domestically available scrap aluminum products.
We estimate approximately 50% of aluminum mounting components used in the U.S. PV
industry are made domestically.

 67

4.2 Trackers
As part of a PV system, trackers move PV modules throughout the day to increase the amount of
sunlight they receive and thus the amount of electricity they produce. Trackers are composed of
various structural, mechanical, and electronic components—some simple in design and
composition and some complex. Tracker companies specialize in developing IP and managing
the logistics of getting tracker equipment to the PV installation site, where much of the tracker
assembly occurs. Trackers are installed not by the tracker companies but by EPC installers, who
receive training and field services from the tracker companies. Tracker companies may
manufacture some components, but much of the manufacturing is done by third-party suppliers.
Tracker companies balance the cost of tracker component production against the speed with
which they can get components to an installation site, which can result in manufacturing
locations that are more expensive but closer to demand, such as the United States and Mexico
(Basore and Feldman 2022a).

This section provides an overview of single-axis tracker systems and some of their key
components (Figure 46). Subsequent parts this section provide additional detail on the supply
chain for other components: torque tubes, fasteners/clamps, piles and rebar, and racking/rails.

 Figure 46. Simplified supply chain diagram for single-axis trackers

4.2.1 Technology Trends for Trackers
Trackers are available in a variety of configurations (Table 10). The greatest distinction is
between single-axis and dual-axis designs. Single-axis trackers have one rotational axis, which
simplifies the mechanical design and reduces costs but limits the tracker’s ability to optimize
incident sunlight. Dual-axis trackers, in contrast, employ a more complex and expensive
rotational system to keep the PV modules perpendicular to the sun and thus maximize incident

 68

sunlight. As shown in Table 10, single-axis designs constitute almost the entire U.S. and global
tracker markets. This report addresses only single-axis products.

Among single-axis designs, several options are available: single-row, dual-row, and centralized
(Table 10). These options present trade-offs between the flexibility offered by systems with
independent rows and the installation efficiencies and reliability (because fewer actuators are
required per row) offered by systems with linked rows. The comparative popularity of these
options also has implications for the tracker supply chain, because each requires different types
and amounts of materials and components. In 2023, single-row trackers—which are most
flexible in terms of smart tracking and installation at sites with difficult geometries or terrain—
had captured about two-thirds of U.S. and global market shares. Because each tracker company
tends to focus on one or two tracker designs, changes in market share by company are related to
changes in market share by technology. For example, among leading U.S.-headquartered tracker
companies, Nextracker focuses on single-row designs, GameChange Solar on single-row designs
(primarily) and dual-row designs (secondarily), and Array Technologies on centralized designs
(primarily) and dual-row designs (secondarily) (Joe Shangraw 2024d, 2024c; Joe Shangraw and
Shen 2025). Several additional tracker technology trends have supply chain implications as well:

 • Smart tracking requires increasingly sophisticated controller hardware and software to
 combine artificial intelligence algorithms, real-time site data, and weather forecasts for
 applications such as minimizing row-to-row shading, positioning modules optimally
 during cloudy conditions, and stowing modules during wind and hail events (Joe
         Shangraw 2024d).
 • One-in-portrait (1P) module mounting has constituted about 80% of the global market
 recently, with 2P constituting most of the rest 20%. Designs using 1P mounting, which
 feature rows of single modules attached to torque tubes in a portrait orientation, tend to
 reduce steel racking requirements. In contrast, 2P designs, which feature rows of dual
 modules attached to torque tubes in a portrait orientation, reduce the number of piles per
 module string (Joe Shangraw 2024d). Figure 47 illustrates the two options.
 • Regardless of the module mounting configuration, the trend toward using significantly
 larger modules requires more structural steel. The growth of tracker use for elevated
 agrivoltaics systems also has potential to increase steel use (Chunduri and Schmela
 2024).
 • Tracker systems are increasingly being installed on undulating or otherwise complex
 terrain, which—if the land is not graded extensively—requires using either longer piles
 or terrain-following trackers. Longer piles increase material use while terrain-following
 trackers require specialized structural components and tracking algorithms (Chunduri and
         Schmela 2024).
 • Global supply chain challenges have spurred a trend toward localizing tracker component
 production, which reduces transportation costs, delays in manufacturing and installation,
 and environmental impact (Chunduri and Schmela 2024).

 69

4.2.2 Domestic Production of Trackers
As noted in the introduction to Section 4.2, tracker systems are largely assembled on-site using
components shipped from various locations. Tracker companies primarily develop IP and
manage logistics. Most of the tracker systems shipped during 2024 in the United States were
from companies with U.S. headquarters: Nextracker captured 44% of the U.S. market,

 70

GameChange Solar 26%, Array Technologies 19%, Nevados 1%, and the total of several smaller
U.S. companies 3% (Figure 48). Some of these U.S.-headquartered companies are major
international players as well. In 2024, Nextracker captured 26% of the global market (ranked
first in global market share), GameChange Solar 12% (third), and Array Technologies 9% (fifth)
(Joe Shangraw and Shen 2025).

domestically in 2024, and 100% domestic systems became available in 2025 (Array

Shangraw 2024d). However, some interviewed companies reported that few customers requested
100% domestic content in 2024 and that domestically manufactured component prices were
around 30%–50% higher than import prices that year. Table 11 shows information on domestic

4.2.3 Imports/Exports of Trackers
Some tracker systems are classified in trade data as single products. For example, trackers may
be imported under HTS code 8479.89.95.99 (“Electromechanical appliances with self-contained
electric motor”) (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025e). However, this broad
classification can include a wide range of other electromechanical devices used across various
industries, such as manufacturing, agriculture, or industrial automation. In addition, companies
may classify similar products under different HTS subcodes, depending on how their product
descriptions align with the tariff schedule or based on interpretations by customs brokers, which
further complicates efforts to track specific products. Overall, because tracker systems are
assembled on-site from numerous components that are shipped to the project site, tracker imports
and exports are best measured at the component level.

4.2.4 Regional Costs for Trackers
Figure 49 shows tracker system prices in 2024. The modeled data, based on DOE benchmarks
(U.S. Department of Energy 2024d), break down the national average U.S. tracker system price
by component. The total U.S. modeled price is $0.14/Wdc. Torque tubes account for 38% of the
tracker price, followed by driven piles (20%), shipping (14%), and motors (10%). The other
components constitute less than 10% of the system price each.

The DOE model assumes torque tubes and fasteners receive the 45X MPTC, and half this credit
is used by the supplier to reduce the component price, 43 which reduces the torque tube price by
about $0.01/Wdc and the fastener price by about $0.001/Wdc. Except for torque tubes, fasteners,
and driven piles—which are assumed to be produced domestically—all the other components are

43
 In the absence of sufficient market data, we assume the tax credit value was evenly split between lowering the
component price and increasing company profit margins. Companies with higher-profit-margin products without
sufficient competitive forces to push down prices may not reduce prices at all when receiving cash flows from the
45X MPTC. Conversely, manufacturers of extremely competitively priced products may need to lower the price of
their products by the entire value they receive from the 45X MPTC to achieve competitive pricing.

 72

assumed to be U.S. imports from China subject to 25% Section 301 tariffs. These tariffs add
about $0.01/Wdc to the sum of the prices for these components.

The comparison of average 2024 selling prices in major markets in Figure 49 is from Wood
Mackenzie (Joe Shangraw 2024d). Based on these data, the United States had the highest tracker
system price at $0.12/Wdc, and India had the lowest at $0.06/Wdc. Wood Mackenzie attributes the
high U.S. price to domestic component requirements. In contrast, India achieved the lowest
prices through major manufacturers with local supply chains that offer low-cost labor and
materials.

 Figure 49. Tracker system prices in major markets, 2024.
 Sources: (U.S. Department of Energy 2024d; Joe Shangraw 2024d)

4.2.5 Upstream Inputs for Trackers
Priority inputs for tracker production include torque tubes, fasteners/clamps, piles and rebar,
racking rails, actuators, drive systems, dampers, and controllers (Table 12). The first four are
detailed in Sections 4.3–4.6, and notes on the others are provided in Table 12 based on industry
interviews in 2024 and 2025. Other upstream inputs are not discussed here due to limited supply
chain information or relatively low importance, such as power supplies, sensors, and cables.

Overall, industry stakeholders expressed confidence in tracker supply chains. They noted that
trade policies pose supply chain risk but that this risk is mitigated via global diversification,
domestic production, and in-house production. They also noted that advanced electronics in
tracker controllers partially depend on China-related supply chains.

  * From industry interviews, 2024–2025. As of 2024, U.S. imports of components such as electric motors, hydraulic
 power units, gear assemblies, valves, and control instruments generally faced base import duties of 1.5%–4% (U.S.
 International Trade Commission 2025a), depending on the specific product and country of origin. Imports from

 from countries such as China, India, and South Korea.

4.3 Torque Tubes
This section focuses on the production of torque tubes in the PV tracker supply chain, including
direct inputs. Figure 50 shows a simplified supply chain diagram for torque tube production.

 74

Within most single-axis tracker systems, torque tubes are the central rotating shafts that support
and—powered by the system’s actuators—drive the movement of PV modules along a single
axis. The torque tubes can be manufactured by tracker manufacturers, imported, or manufactured
by third-party U.S. roll formers. Torque tubes must meet strict mechanical tolerance
requirements to enable trouble-free bearing rotation and proper system alignment as well as
structural integrity requirements to withstand dynamic and static loads, such as wind and snow,
without mechanical flaws. They are typically made from galvanized steel for durability and
corrosion resistance. Aluminum and polymer-based composites can be used for site-specific
environmental conditions or structural demands, but they are rare and not considered in this
analysis.

 Figure 50. Simplified supply chain diagram for torque tubes.
 BOF = basic oxygen furnace; HDG = hot-dip galvanization

4.3.1 Technology Trends for Torque Tubes
Torque tubes come in various shapes, which represent a balance between ease of manufacturing,
material use, structural integrity, and integration into specific tracker systems (Medium 2024).
For example, Nextracker uses round tubes, Array Technologies uses octagonal tubes, and
GameChange Solar uses square tubes (Array Technologies 2020; GameChange Solar 2025;
Nextracker 2019; Unimacts 2025). These torque tubes are typically made of steel and are
galvanized to protect them from corrosion (National Material Company 2023).

The specifications of torque tubes—such as length, diameter, wall thickness, and steel grade—
are important to assess U.S. manufacturing readiness. Typical round torque tubes are 114–165
mm in diameter, with wall thicknesses of 2.5–5 mm, and weights of 6–16 kg/m, depending on
geometry and steel grade (PandaPipe 2025; Nextracker 2020; TUS Pipe 2022). These
dimensions fall within the production capabilities of domestic roll-forming and tube mill
facilities, which are designed to handle common structural steel sections. This contrasts with

 75

other renewable sectors such as offshore wind, where monopiles and flanges often exceed U.S.
forging and forming capabilities.

Torque tube specifications used in PV system installations are not niche or exotic, and they are
often standard sizes readily produced using hot-rolled steel coils and inline galvanization—
processes widely available in the U.S. steel sector. In fact, leading suppliers, such as Unimacts
and Attala Steel, manufacture torque tubes domestically using locally sourced steel (Attala Steel
2025; Unimacts 2025). This suggests that most torque tubes used in utility-scale PV systems are
both manufacturable and procurable domestically, aligning with 45X and ITC domestic content
compliance.

4.3.2 Domestic Production of Torque Tubes
The data needed to estimate total U.S. torque tube manufacturing capacity are not publicly
available. However, industry announcements and manufacturing partnerships suggest the
equivalent of multiple gigawatts of torque tubes were domestically produced in the United States
in 2024, with additional production capacity planned. Domestic torque tube manufacturers
include Lock Joint Tube, Atkore, OMCO, BCI Steel, JM Steel, MSS Steel Tubes, Priefert Steel,
Nucor, Unimacts, and Zekelman Industries. Many of these companies have partnered with
leading solar tracker companies, including Array Technologies, GameChange Solar, and
Nextracker (U.S. Department of Energy 2025c; Zekelman Industries 2025).

4.3.3 Imports/Exports of Torque Tubes
Torque tubes have a low value-to-weight ratio, making imports less competitive because of high
transport costs. Multiple domestic roll formers and steel processors partnered with the PV sector
in 2024, supported by a robust U.S. steel industry with only about 13% net import reliance
(USGS 2025). While welded steel tube imports serve a wide range of industrial applications,
only a small fraction can be attributed to PV. Based on these factors, we estimate that more than
75% of torque tubes deployed in the U.S. PV industry are domestically manufactured. Appendix
B provides detailed analysis and assumptions.

Torque tubes used in PV tracker systems are probably classified under multiple HTS codes
depending on their material, shape, and manufacturing process (see Appendix C). Between 2015
and 2024, the United States imported $0.5–$1.2 billion of welded steel tubes every year; Canada
and Mexico together consistently accounted for more than half of total import value each year.
Because U.S. welded steel tube imports serve a wide range of non-solar industrial applications,
we cannot determine from the trade data whether these trends are consistent with PV torque tube
imports. In 2024, estimated PV torque tube demand was around 930,000 t of steel (Appendix B),
while U.S. welded tube imports totaled roughly $889 million, equivalent to about 680,000 t. 44
Given that PV torque tube demand in 2024 alone exceeded all welded tube imports serving
multiple industries, and the U.S. steel sector meets more than 75% of national consumption from
domestic sources, it is reasonable to conclude that most torque tubes are manufactured
domestically.

44
  U.S. International Trade Commission trade data for welded tube (2024): customs value ~$889.1 million, first unit
quantity 680.36 million kg (~680,000 t), roughly translating to $1.3/kg ($1,300/t) of import custom value.

 76

Imports from USMCA partners such as Canada and Mexico are duty-free. Base import duties
from other countries—such as Taiwan, South Korea, and India—with Most Favored Nation
status are also zero for the relevant HTS codes. However, they may still be subject to additional
tariffs, such as Section 232 steel tariffs, or AD/CVD measures depending on product type and
country of origin.

4.3.4 Regional Pricing for Torque Tubes
The domestically manufactured torque tube cost in 2024 was about $0.05/Wdc when used in a
100-MWdc single-axis tracking system. This assumes manufacturers pass through roughly half of
the 45X MPTC to buyers, 45 equivalent to a reduction of about $0.01/Wdc (U.S. Department of
Energy 2024d). Data from industry interviews suggested that domestically produced torque tube
prices were about 30% higher than the prices of imported counterparts in 2024; however,
domestically produced torque tubes are cost-competitive because they can help PV projects
receive an ITC domestic content bonus.

Globally, the price of torque tubes can be as low as $0.025/Wdc in certain regions, particularly in
markets with lower raw material, fabrication, galvanizing, and manufacturing expenses driven by
lower labor or overhead costs (Business Analytiq 2025). However, steel prices are a major driver
of price differences. In 2023, for example, U.S. steel cost twice as much as Chinese steel and
about 25% higher than Northern European Steel (Chopra and Shangraw 2023). Beyond regional
price variations, costs also fluctuate owing to site-specific conditions, such as wind load
requirements, soil conditions affecting foundation design, and corrosion protection needs (e.g.,
galvanization or special coatings for humid or coastal areas).

4.3.5 Upstream Inputs for Torque Tubes

4.4 Fasteners/Clamps
This section outlines the supply chain of generic fasteners and clamps used in PV mounting
structures, along with their primary input materials, manufacturing processes, and domestic
production vs import reliance (Figure 51 and Figure 52). Fasteners and clamps are small but
critical BOS components that mechanically secure modules, rails, and structural assemblies in
PV systems. They can be categorized as follows:

By product type:
 • Clamps (mid clamps, end clamps, and rail connectors)
 • Fasteners (bolts, nuts, screws, washers, rivets, and lockbolts).
By material:
 • Steel (commonly used for structural fasteners and utility-scale tracker clamps)
 • Aluminum (commonly used for module-level clamps in residential/commercial racking).

45
 In the absence of sufficient market data, we assume the tax credit value was evenly split between lowering the
component price and increasing company profit margins. Companies with higher-profit-margin products without
sufficient competitive forces to push down prices may not reduce prices at all when receiving cash flows from the
45X MPTC. Conversely, manufacturers of extremely competitively priced products may need to lower the price of
their products by the entire value they receive from the 45X MPTC to achieve competitive pricing.

 77

By application/market:
 • Structural/PV tracker-specific (large, high-specification fasteners such lockbolts, often
 integrated into proprietary tracker designs)
 • Generic/commodity (small fasteners such as regular bolts, screws, washers, and mass-
 produced aluminum clamps, widely traded across industries).

 Figure 51. Simplified supply chain diagram for steel fasteners/clamps

 Figure 52. Simplified supply chain diagram for aluminum clamps.
 CNC = computer numerical control

 78

4.4.1 Technology Trends for Fasteners/Clamps
Most PV clamps and generic fasteners are made from extruded, anodized aluminum for
corrosion resistance, mechanical strength, and compatibility with aluminum rails, particularly in
residential and commercial PV systems. However, in utility-scale PV installations, where steel
rails are more common, galvanized steel hardware is typically used where all contacting
materials share similar electrochemical properties. In mixed material assemblies, such as
aluminum clamps mounted on steel rails, insulating washers or polymer spacers are often used to
electrically isolate the dissimilar metals and prevent galvanic coupling.

PV fasteners range widely in size, with M6 to M16 sizes commonly used for module mounting
(commodity fasteners) and M27 to M76 used in structural applications such as tracker
assemblies. Smaller M6–M16 fasteners are mass-produced for numerous industries and readily
available from domestic and global suppliers. Larger fasteners—especially those conforming to
high-strength standards such as EN 14399 HV, Grade 10.9—are used in more structurally
demanding PV applications and may require qualified vendors or specialized production lines.

From a supply chain standpoint, generic fasteners are often imported owing to their high value-
to-weight ratio and low unit cost. However, high-specification fasteners, such as lockbolts used
in trackers, are more likely to be produced domestically or by regional suppliers because of their
tighter tolerances, higher material costs, and eligibility for the 45X MPTC. For example,
Nextracker has transitioned to lockbolt systems to improve installation efficiency and vibration
resistance design choices that necessitate closer integration with specialized fastener
manufacturers (Chavesbao 2024; Ness 2020; Raftery 2024).

Clamps, often made from extruded aluminum, also vary by design. Mid clamps, which secure
adjacent modules, are slightly taller and heavier than end clamps. These products must be tightly
dimensioned to support electrical bonding and withstand snow and wind loads. Like steel-based
fasteners, aluminum clamps are also assumed to be largely imported, with limited reshoring
owing to economic constraints discussed earlier. Domestic production feasibility for tracker-
specific fasteners is high, but commodity fasteners and clamps face structural cost disadvantages
that limit U.S. competitiveness in the high-volume market (Eurotec 2025; SPC 2018a, 2018b).

4.4.2 Domestic Production of Fasteners/Clamps
Tracker companies in the United States partner with manufacturers such as Unimacts and BCI
Steel to produce proprietary fasteners and other tracker components domestically. Array
Technologies and GameChange Solar also have their own production facilities to manufacture
locally (GameChange Solar 2024b; Fischer 2024; Pickerel 2024a). Nonproprietary fasteners used
in PV systems are manufactured by a few domestic companies (Atkore 2025; All-Pro Fasteners
2025; Mudge Fasteners 2024; Fastenal 2025).

Many companies have announced tracker component manufacturing, including Alpha Steel,
Atkore (Nextracker), BCI Steel (Nextracker), Extruded Aluminum Corporation (Array
Technologies), JM Steel (Nextracker), Lock Joint Tube (Array Technologies), MSS Steel Tubes
(Nextracker), OMCO Solar, Priefert Steel (Nevados), and Unimacts (Nextracker). However,
information about domestic production of fasteners within these facilities is not readily available.

 79

Most nonproprietary commodity fasteners continue to be imported. Their small size and high
value-to-weight ratio make them cost-effective to source internationally, particularly from
countries with established mass-production capabilities. Consequently, U.S. fastener
manufacturers tend to focus on high-specification or niche applications such as structural or
proprietary fasteners, limiting domestic competitiveness in the high-volume fastener segment.

4.4.3 Imports/Exports of Fasteners/Clamps
Generic fasteners and clamps used in PV systems are probably classified under different HTS 46
headings (see Appendix C). Between 2015 and 2024, the United States imported a substantial
volume of generic fasteners, with Taiwan and China contributing to more than 50% of imported
customs value. It is reasonable to assume that generic fasteners used in PV follow similar
sourcing patterns, given their indistinguishability from fasteners used in other sectors. In 2024,
the United States imported roughly $4 billion of generic fasteners, and PV fastener demand was
only about 50,000 t (see Appendix B). Even if all PV fasteners were assumed to be imported,
they would only constitute 4% 47 of U.S. imports of generic fasteners (U.S. Department of Energy
2024d). Based on industry interviews, high-specification tracker fasteners are more likely to be
supplied domestically owing to niche production requirements and eligibility for IRA 45X
incentives.

A wide range of iron or steel fasteners generally face a base tariff of 0%–8.5% (U.S.
International Trade Commission 2025a), depending on the product type. While China faces a
25% Section 301 tariff on top of the base duty, Canada benefits from 0% duty under USMCA.
Other countries—such as Taiwan, Japan, South Korea, Germany, and India—face standard base
duties by product type and may also face additional duties under Section 232 steel tariffs unless
exempted through country-specific exclusions or quota arrangements.

4.4.4 Regional Pricing for Fasteners/Clamps
The combined cost of domestically manufactured structural and module-level fasteners used in a
100-MWdc single-axis tracking system was about $0.002/Wdc in 2024 (Ramasamy et al. 2025).
This cost assumes the supplier applies half of the 45X MPTC to lower prices, 48 equivalent to a
reduction of about $0.001/Wdc (U.S. Department of Energy 2024d). Data from industry
interviews suggests that domestically produced fastener prices were about 40% higher than the
prices of their imported counterparts in 2024.

46
 Fasteners used in PV systems are generally classified in a way that enables relatively detailed tracking of steel
fastener imports (see Appendix C). In contrast, clamps used to secure PV modules to rails are typically classified
under broader headings depending on their material: 7326 for miscellaneous steel articles and 7616 for
miscellaneous aluminum articles. These imprecise classifications may cover a wide range of unrelated hardware,
making it difficult to isolate solar-specific clamp imports as noted in some of the U.S. Customs Rulings (U.S.
Customs and Border Protection 2024a, 2019).
47
 Using the steel fastener estimate of $3.3/kg from (U.S. Department of Energy 2024d), this equates to ~1.2 million
t of imported fasteners. PV demand for fasteners was ~50,000 t in 2024 (Appendix B), representing ~4% of total
imports.
48
 In the absence of sufficient market data, we assume the tax credit value was evenly split between lowering the
component price and increasing company profit margins. Companies with higher-profit-margin products without
sufficient competitive forces to push down prices may not reduce prices at all when receiving cash flows from the
45X MPTC. Conversely, manufacturers of extremely competitively priced products may need to lower the price of
their products by the entire value they receive from the 45X MPTC to achieve competitive pricing.

 80

Globally, the price of PV fasteners can be as low as $0.001/Wdc in certain regions, particularly in
markets with lower raw material costs and non-material-related expenses (Business Analytiq
2025). Tariffs and trade regulations, such as Section 232 tariffs on steel and aluminum imports,
affect the prices of imported and domestically manufactured fasteners (The Adviser Magazine
2025). These tariffs have led to higher producer prices in the metals industry, which are
eventually passed on to consumers.

In the U.S. commercial sector, fastener costs are modestly higher owing to smaller volumes,
averaging around $0.003/Wdc. In the residential sector, clamp-related fastener costs alone can
reach approximately $0.02/Wdc (U.S. Department of Energy 2024d). However, no
comprehensive global estimates are available for fastener or clamp costs across commercial and
residential PV markets because of wide variability in system design, labor practices, and supply
chain localization.

4.4.5 Upstream Inputs for Fasteners/Clamps

4.5 Piles and Rebar
This section focuses on the supply chain of piles and rebar used in ground-mount PV systems,
including direct inputs (Figure 53 and Figure 54). Piles are vertical support posts driven into the
soil to anchor the racking system, transferring loads from the tracker structure to the ground.
They eliminate the need for concrete foundations, making installation faster and less labor-
intensive. Rebar is often embedded in the concrete foundations of specialized tracker systems to
reinforce mechanical stability. Driven piles are most common in soft soils that allow for direct
embedment. Foundations with rebar are typically used where pile driving is problematic.

 Figure 53. Simplified supply chain diagram for driven piles

 81

 .

 Figure 54. Simplified supply chain diagram for rebar

4.5.1 Technology Trends for Piles and Rebar
Driven round steel piles 49 are most common. They are typically made from structural steel, most
commonly ASTM A500 grade B or ASTM A252 grade 2 or 3 steel (Nextracker 2023c; Pickerel
2017a). Cylindrical piles are straightforward to manufacture, cost-effective, and widely
adaptable to different tracker systems. The shaft type (I-beam or H-pile) is selected depending on
soil condition and other environmental factors. Driven helical piles using steel shafts with
welded helix plates are increasingly used where soil conditions are weak or non-cohesive,
allowing for screw-like installation that provides end-bearing and frictional resistance. Larger
piles provide the highest load-bearing and lateral strength, while smaller ones serve lighter-duty
needs. Hot-dip galvanization in accordance with ASTM A123/AM-123 is the common coating
for piles used in the United States (ASTM 2024b). In some cases, piles may be coated with
epoxy-based protective layers instead of hot-dip galvanizing, especially for corrosive
environments.

The most widely used rebars are deformed carbon steel bars, typically manufactured according to
ASTM A615 (Grades 40, 60, or 75), where Grade 60 (yield strength of 60 ksi) is most common
owing to its balance of strength and cost. Standard rebar sizes range from #3 (3/8 in.) to #11 (1-
3/8 in.), with #4 to #6 bars being most common in PV applications. 50 Rebar provides tensile
reinforcement and increases the structural integrity of concrete under various loading scenarios,
including lateral loads from wind or tracker torque. In corrosive environments (e.g., coastal
sites), epoxy-coated rebars (ASTM A775) or stainless steel rebars may be used, although at
higher cost (ASTM 2024a).

4.5.2 Domestic Production of Piles and Rebar
While the specifications for piles and rebar vary by industrial application, both are primarily
supported by the domestic steel supply chain. The United States consumes a significant portion

49
 Utility-scale PV projects typically use piles with diameters of 3–6.63 in., wall gauges of 0.125–0.25 in., and
lengths of 6–16 ft. Pile torque capacities range from 6,000 to 35,000 ft-lbs (Magnum Piering 2020).
50
 In utility-scale PV plants, rebar sizes typically range from #4 (0.5-in. diameter, 0.2-in.² area) to #6 (0.75-in.
diameter, 0.4-in.² area). These sizes are used for foundation reinforcement, providing sufficient tensile strength. The
#5 rebar (0.625-in. diameter, 0.3-in.² area) is also common. Larger sizes like #8 (1-in. diameter, 0.8-in.² area) may
be used for heavier-duty foundations (BigRentz 2021).

 82

of the iron ore it produces, supporting the integrated steel mills that supply hot-rolled coils and
plate for pile manufacturing.

The data needed for precise estimation of U.S. tracker pile manufacturing capacity are
unavailable. However, consistent with assumptions in Section 4.1, we assume more than 75% of
driven piles used in U.S. PV installations are domestically manufactured. Steel tube
manufacturers co-produce torque tubes and pile shafts using similar rolling and galvanizing
infrastructure, often located near major solar markets to reduce freight costs and lead times.
Leading domestic steel suppliers and fabricators—such as Unimacts, Zekelman Industries, JM
Steel, Atkore, Alpha Steel, and BCI Steel—are key players supporting the production of piles for
utility-scale tracker foundations. These companies often partner with tracker manufacturers,
including Nextracker, Array Technologies, and GameChange Solar. Nextracker, for instance,
partnered with Unimacts and MSS Steel to supply domestically fabricated piles and torque tubes
from its U.S. mills in Pennsylvania and Arkansas, helping meet domestic content requirements
(Nextracker 2023a; Fischer 2024; Nextracker 2024b). Similarly, JM Steel and BCI Steel have
enhanced their manufacturing footprints to accommodate growing tracker pile demand, offering
custom roll-formed or welded piles cut to tracker specifications.

Rebars are widely produced across the United States through EAF mini-mills, which primarily
use scrap steel as input. The United States has significant domestic capacity for plain and
deformed carbon steel rebars, with much of the output complying with ASTM A615, A706, and
A996 standards. Most of the rebar production is concentrated in the central and southern United
States, close to scrap steel sources and key construction markets, including solar projects.

Nucor Corporation is the largest rebar producer in North America, with several EAF mini-mills
in South Carolina, Arkansas, and Texas (Nucor 2025). Nucor produces ASTM-grade rebar
including products compliant with A615 and A706. Commercial Metals Company operates mills
in Texas, Oklahoma, and South Carolina. It is known for rebar fabrication and EAF-based
sustainable steelmaking, and it produces epoxy-coated and fabricated rebar (CMC 2025). Gerdau
Long Steel North America operates mills in Florida, Tennessee, and Missouri, supplying
deformed and plain rebars per the A615 and A706 standards. The company’s Jacksonville,
Florida, mill serves Southeast U.S. solar projects (Gerdau 2025). Steel Dynamics, Inc. produces
rebar in Indiana and Arkansas, with growing capacity through new investments (Steel Dynamics
2025). Byer Steel Group is a Cincinnati-based mini-mill specializing in rebar made from 100%
recycled steel, commonly used in local infrastructure and regional solar projects (Byer Steel
2025).

4.5.3 Imports/Exports of Piles and Rebar
Based on pile-related HTS codes (see Appendix C), between 2015 and 2024 Canada consistently
dominated the import market, followed by Mexico (Figure 55). The low value-to-weight ratio of
piles makes short-distance transportation from Canada and Mexico attractive. In 2024, PV pile
demand was about 400,000 t (Appendix B), while U.S. imports of structural steel columns and
piles totaled about 254,000 t ($761 million). Given that PV pile demand alone exceeded imports,
and the U.S. steel sector met more than 75% of national consumption from domestic sources, we
assume domestic mills and roll formers must supply most piles used in PV projects.

 83

 Figure 55. Steel column and pile import customs value, 2015–2024
The United States has sufficient domestic rebar production capacity to meet demand, but some
rebar has been imported (Figure 56). Turkey was the leading supplier through 2018. By 2023–
2024, however, Turkey’s share had diminished—mainly due to imposition of AD/CVD duties—
with Mexico, Algeria, Spain, and Egypt filling much of the gap. In 2022, rebar imports peaked,
reflecting a supply shortfall from U.S. manufacturing facilities coupled with a surge in post-
COVID construction demand.

In 2024, the U.S. imported roughly $581 million of rebar, equivalent to 915,000 t, representing
only about 5% of a domestic market valued at over $10 billion (Grand View Research 2024;
MEPS International 2024). The PV share of this market is only on the order of tens of thousands
of tonnes annually (see Appendix B).

Any U.S. imports of these steel products enter duty-free (U.S. International Trade Commission
2025a) from Canada and Mexico under USMCA and from most European Union countries with
Most Favored Nation status. However, U.S. imports from China are subject to standard base
duties and Section 301 tariffs of up to 25% (U.S. Trade Representative 2018), significantly
increasing total U.S. import costs. While imports from Algeria face no base tariff, they may be
subject to a 25% Section 232 steel tariff unless exempted. Imports from Turkey also face a zero
base tariff but are subject to AD/CVD duties (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025c).

 84

 Figure 56. Rebar import customs value, 2015–2024

4.5.4 Regional Pricing for Piles and Rebar
In 2024, driven piles cost approximately $0.03/Wdc in a 100-MWdc U.S. PV plant. For projects
that use reinforced concrete foundations instead of piles, the cost of rebar alone would be
roughly $0.007/Wdc, which excludes the cost of concrete and form work. Depending on site-
specific geotechnical conditions and structural requirements, some projects may require a
combination of driven piles and rebar. Globally, the cost of these materials could be as low as
$0.015/Wdc for driven piles and $0.003/Wdc for rebar (U.S. Department of Energy 2024d).

4.5.5 Upstream Inputs for Piles and Rebar

4.6 Racking Rails
This section focuses on the supply chain of PV racking rails, including direct inputs (Figure 57
and Figure 58). Racking rails are the primary interface between PV modules and support
structures. In rooftop systems, mounting rails are almost exclusively made of aluminum to
minimize weight and are secured to the roof using flashing/feet, L-feet, or ballast systems
depending on the roof type. In ground-mount applications, galvanized steel rails are attached
directly to the longitudinal torque tubes. Modules are fastened to these rails using mid clamps,
end clamps, or other certified mounting hardware.

 85

 Figure 57. Simplified supply chain diagram for aluminum rails

 Figure 58. Simplified supply chain diagram for steel rails

4.6.1 Technology Trends for Racking Rails
Utility-scale PV systems primarily use galvanized steel rails owing to their structural strength,
cost-effectiveness, and compatibility with steel torque tubes and piles. These rails are typically
short C-channel profiles, roll-formed from hot-dip galvanized steel, with lengths of 4–10 ft,
widths of 30–60 mm, and wall thicknesses of 1.5–3 mm. In recent years, tracker manufacturers
such as Nextracker, GameChange Solar, and Array Technologies have increasingly favored rail-
less designs or short-rail systems to minimize material use and reduce installation complexity. In
rail-less configurations, PV modules are mounted directly onto the torque tube with specialized
clamps or brackets. Some systems use short-rail clips for support.

Rooftop systems, by contrast, almost exclusively use aluminum rails to reduce weight and
prevent corrosion. These rails, often extruded from 6005-T5 aluminum and anodized for
durability, share similar dimensions but are significantly lighter, weighing about 1.5–3 kg/m vs.
6–9 kg/m for steel.

 86

4.6.2 Domestic Production of Racking Rails
The domestic shares for steel and aluminum rails are estimated based on the broader material
sourcing assumptions presented in Appendix B. Because steel rails would rely on the same
domestic steel coil and roll-forming infrastructure as torque tubes and piles, we assume more
than 75% of steel rails used in utility-scale PV installations are domestically produced. In
contrast, we estimate about 50% of aluminum rails used in the U.S. PV industry are made
domestically (see Appendix B). Several domestic manufacturers indicate their products are
manufactured to comply with federal domestic content requirements for tax credits (Table 13).

 Table 13. U.S. Rail Manufacturers

  Company Product Manufactured
  OMCO Solar Roll-formed steel racking and rail components for mounting systems
  GameChange Steel torque tubes, purlins (rails), clamps (SpeedClamp™), other tracker
  Solar components
  IronRidge Extruded aluminum rails (XR10, XR100), mounting systems
  K2 Systems CrossRail and MiniRail Comp aluminum racking systems
  PowerField Energy PowerRack (steel frame, non-penetrating)
  Unirac Racking rails, mounting systems, attachments, clamps

 (Paramanandam 2024)

4.6.3 Imports/Exports of Racking/Rails
The HTS codes most relevant for aluminum PV mounting rails are for aluminum alloy profiles
(see Appendix C), and Figure 59 shows these aluminum imports. However, steel rails are not
isolated in trade data, so their volumes cannot be separately tracked.

In 2024, U.S. use of aluminum PV rails required about 60,000 t of aluminum (see Appendix B),
while U.S. aluminum profile imports totaled 268,000 t. We assume half of PV aluminum rails
are imported as finished products, and the remainder are domestically extruded from a mix of
imported and domestic billet feedstock. Thus, PV’s share of aluminum profile imports is around
30,000 t, or 11% of total U.S. aluminum profile imports.

Canada and Mexico have been the dominant suppliers of aluminum profiles, reflecting favorable
logistics and trade arrangements (Kini et al. 2025). In 2024, aluminum alloy extrusions generally
faced a base U.S. import tariff of 1.5% to 5%, depending on the specific alloy and product
profile, though these were waived for trade partners such as Canada, Mexico, Vietnam,
Indonesia, and Germany (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a). However, most of these
countries—except Canada and Mexico—remained subject to a 10% Section 232 tariff on
aluminum products in 2024 (Office of the President 2018).

 87

 Figure 59. Aluminum profile import customs value, 2015–2024

4.6.4 Regional Pricing for Racking/Rails
Racking rails used in utility-scale PV systems cost roughly $0.01/Wdc, and those used in rooftop
systems cost around $0.01/Wdc (commercial) to $0.09/Wdc (residential) as of 2024 (U.S.
Department of Energy 2024d). North American prices are typically higher than prices in other
regions.

4.6.5 Upstream Inputs for Racking/Rails

4.7 Flashing/Feet
This section focuses on the production of flashing/feet for rooftop PV mounting structures,
including direct inputs (Figure 60). Flashing/feet are critical rooftop mounting components
primarily used in residential PV installations. They anchor the racking structure to the roof while
ensuring a watertight seal. Typically installed beneath roof shingles or tiles, flashing/feet consist
of a base plate (flashing), an L-foot or standoff, and sealing materials designed to prevent water
intrusion at penetration points.

 88

 Figure 60. Simplified supply chain diagram for flashing/feet

4.7.1 Technology Trends for Flashing/Feet
Flashing/feet have evolved to include material innovations and design improvements to enhance
durability and installation efficiency in residential PV systems. Traditional 51 aluminum flashing
remains prevalent owing to its lightweight and corrosion-resistant properties. However,
innovations have aimed to improve installation efficiency, reduce material use, and enhance the
longevity of PV installations. Galvanized steel flashing is stiffer, making it particularly well
suited for high-wind areas where structural resilience is critical. Ethylene propylene diene
monomer (EPDM) rubber flashing enhances leak prevention by combining traditional aluminum
flashing with a flexible rubber bushing, creating a watertight seal around roof penetrations.
Elevated flashing introduces a raised section to divert water away from the roof penetration. One
of the more recent innovations is microflashing, which uses a small stainless steel compression
washer with an EPDM rubber seal. This design simplifies installation by eliminating the need to
lift shingles, reducing labor time and minimizing the risk of roof damage (Pickerel 2017b;
QuickBOLT 2017).

4.7.2 Domestic Production of Flashing/Feet
Several U.S. manufacturers produce PV flashing/feet and roof attachments. IronRidge, part of
Enstall, offers 100% U.S.-made components (Enstall 2024). Unirac manufactures clearly labeled
domestic products that meet ITC and Build America Buy America Act standards (Unirac 2024),
while Pegasus Solar offers waterproof flashing products manufactured in California (Pegasus
Solar 2023).

Data on U.S. flashing/feet production are unavailable. However, consistent with the aluminum
material analysis in Appendix B, we assume roughly half of flashing/feet are domestically
produced, while the remainder are imported.

51
  Common dimensions are roughly 0.037–0.065 m² for the footplate area, with heights of 0.06–0.12 m and weights
of 0.23–0.91 kg each (IronRidge 2025b, 2015).

 89

Rubber components such as EPDM grommets are typically imported from overseas suppliers.
However, for domestically supplied flashing/feet, the final extrusion, forming, punching, drilling,
and assembly processes are handled domestically, ensuring compliance with domestic content
requirements, despite reliance on imported subcomponents.

4.7.3 Imports/Exports of Flashing/Feet
No HTS code specifically and exclusively identifies flashing/feet, and classification for such
roofing products depends on the material and structural function (see Appendix C). We assume
the general roofing industry classifications represent flashing/feet used for PV, because these
products use similar materials and serve the same functional role of providing waterproof
structural attachment points. This approach may overstate total volumes attributable to PV, but it
provides the most appropriate proxy given current trade data limitations.

China has been the largest single supplier of roof metal mountings on a value basis (Figure 61).
Other contributors have included Canada, Vietnam, Mexico, Taiwan, South Korea, India, New
Zealand, Italy, and Germany. In conjunction with U.S. manufacturing (see Section 4.7.2), this
diversification possibly reduces supply chain risk for flashing/feet, assuming the PV market
sources flashing/feet from the same supply chain channels as the broader roofing industry.

In 2024, the PV industry required about 7,000 t of aluminum flashing/feet (see Appendix B).
Based on our assumption that half of flashing/feet demand is met through imports, only about
8% of the broader $235 million (about 45,000 t) market is for roof mount and flashing products.

 Figure 61. Roof mounting import customs value by country, 2015–2024
In 2024, U.S. imports of flashing/feet were duty-free from Canada and Mexico under USMCA
(U.S. Trade Representative 2020). Imports from Taiwan and Vietnam were subject to a base rate
of 0% or 3.5%, depending on the product type. Imports from China faced the same base rates but
were also subject to a 25% Section 301 tariff.

 90

4.7.4 Regional Pricing for Flashing/Feet
In 2024, the cost of domestically produced aluminum flashing/feet was about $0.101/Wdc in an
8-kWdc residential rooftop PV system (U.S. Department of Energy 2024d). Industry interviews
indicate that the cost of imported flashing/feet can be up to 5 times less expensive. As with other
BOS components, U.S. prices tend to be higher than those in other regions, reflecting higher
local labor and manufacturing costs. However, we assume domestic flashing/feet are primarily
used in projects where policy incentives or procurement requirements justify the price premium.

4.7.5 Upstream Inputs for Flashing/Feet

 91

5 Electrical Components
5.1 Overview
This section covers supply chains for the electrical parts of PV systems other than PV modules,
including inverters, transformers, and a variety of other electrical components. Inverters convert
dc power from PV modules to ac and are rated in watts ac of output. Utility-scale PV in the
United States has historically used central inverters, but a trend toward string inverters is
emerging due to advantages related to modularity. Commercial systems generally use three-
phase string inverters, while residential systems use either string inverters or module-level power
electronics (MLPE), including microinverters and dc optimizers.

At the end of 2024, the United States had an MLPE assembly capacity of around 7 GWac, utility
inverter capacity of 10 GWac, central inverter capacity of 9 GWac, and commercial string inverter
capacity of 3 GWac. 52 Announced domestic inverter assembly for central and utility inverters
would allow the United States to produce up to 40 GWac of utility-scale inverters per year. Most
U.S. inverter facilities are owned by companies with headquarters outside of the United States,
including Europe, Japan, and Israel. In 2024, most central inverters were imported from Europe
and China, representing 78% of 2024 U.S. shipments (Joseph Shangraw 2024; Joseph Shangraw
and Shen 2025). Like other PV components, U.S.-made PV inverters cost more to produce and
have higher prices compared with inverters made abroad, owing to higher material and labor
costs. Some incentives are aimed at offsetting these costs.

Printed circuit board assemblies (PCBAs) are the primary inverter controls. Some PCBA
manufacturers operate in the United States, enabling domestic manufacturers Enphase,
SolarEdge, and Yaskawa Solectria Solar to provide inverters that partially meet the criteria for
the ITC domestic content bonus. PCBAs and transistors are both specified components within
the IRS safe harbor table for the ITC domestic content bonus, which has helped drive demand for
domestic PCBA suppliers.

Transistors are key components within PV inverter PCBAs. Most PV inverters still use silicon
metal-oxide-semiconductor field-effect transistors (MOSFETs) and insulated-gate bipolar
transistors (IGBTs), but more advanced transistor technologies, such as SiC and GaN, have
gained modest market shares. Central inverters are trending toward higher switching frequencies,
improved power-to-volume and power-to-weight ratios, and higher voltages, which is driving a
slight shift toward SiC MOSFETs instead of silicon MOSFETs or IGBTs. Additionally, some
U.S. inverter manufacturers are offering GaN-based MLPE inverters. Power electronics
manufacturers in the United States generally specialize in SiC or GaN transistor technologies,
but some domestic PV inverter manufacturers have supply agreements that, given the
manufacturing locations of their suppliers, have a high probability of sourcing transistors from
Germany and Malaysia.

Medium-voltage transformers (defined here as having high-side voltages of 1–69 kV) are critical
for large-scale PV applications, stepping up the ac voltage from the inverter to be fed onto the
grid. As of 2024, there is a global supply shortage of all transformers due to growing demand

 Definitions of inverter size in this report generally follow the categories created for the 45X MPTC (U.S.
52

Department of Energy 2022).

 92

from renewable energy, electric vehicles (EVs), and data centers combined with a slow build-out
of supply. The shortage has lengthened product lead times and increased prices. In 2024,
developers were expected to wait 1–2 years for smaller step-up transformers and up to 3–5 years
for larger step-up transformers, and delays could increase if projected demands outpace
expanded supply. We estimate around half of medium-voltage transformers are made
domestically, and they cost approximately 10%–25% more than imports. The United States
would import from China at a significant discount if not for the Section 301 tariffs. Over half of
large medium-voltage transformers are imported from Mexico, though imports from South Korea
and Columbia grew significantly in 2024. China represented approximately 5% of imports. The
most critical and potentially constrained inputs to transformer manufacturing include grain-
oriented electric steel (GOES) and continuously transposed conductor (CTC) copper wire, both
of which have limited U.S. production capacities but are produced in large quantities in several
countries, including China and Russia. In addition, foreign transformer manufacturers can
purchase foreign GOES at a lower price than domestic transformer manufacturers, owing to U.S.
tariffs on imported steel, and then import their products into the United States.

There are a variety of other electrical components within a PV system, particularly for large-
scale applications, such as high-voltage transformers, combiner boxes, switchgear, circuit
breakers, substation components, and transmission-related equipment. Many come from various
domestic and international manufacturing locations, including Mexico, Canada, and China.
Because of recent increases in U.S. deployment of PV, wind turbines, and EVs—which rely on
many of these components—companies have made recent investments to expand domestic
manufacturing.

5.2 Inverters
This section focuses on production of inverters for PV applications, including direct inputs other
than transistors, which are discussed in Section 5.3. A simplified supply chain diagram is
provided in Figure 62.

 93

 Figure 62. Simplified supply chain diagram for PV inverter production

5.2.1 Technology Trends for Inverters
Different sizes of inverters are used to convert dc power from PV systems to ac compatible with
the electric grid.

Residential PV systems in the United States typically use MLPE. In these configurations, a
microinverter is used for each module, or a dc optimizer is used for each module with
connections to a string inverter. Our analysis indicates that MLPE are most likely to use
advanced technology power electronics such as GaN transistors (see Section 5.3). MLPE were
used for 87% of U.S. residential systems installed during 2024, where 56% of systems used
microinverters and 31% used dc optimizers (Barbose et al. 2024, 2025).

Nonresidential systems smaller than 5 MWac in 2024 used MLPE less frequently than residential
systems, and they more often used dc optimizers. Nonresidential systems smaller than 5 MWac
frequently used string inverters. In this configuration, modules are connected in a string and
connected to a single-phase or three-phase inverter.

Utility-scale systems in the United States typically use central inverters (rated for 1 MWac or
more), which convert power from multiple module strings (an array) simultaneously. In the
United States, central inverters represented almost 80% of PV inverter shipments and 90% of
utility inverter shipments in 2024 (Joseph Shangraw and Shen 2025). Globally, however, the use
of utility-scale string inverters has been increasing because of improved levelized cost of energy,
reliability, and repairability as well as lower production losses (Joe Shangraw 2024a, 2024b;
Montague 2023). Both types of inverters for utility applications are trending toward higher
voltages and modular systems because of increasing PV module power (Yole Intelligence 2023,
2024, 2025).

The U.S. market favors central inverters because EPC contractors prefer the low CapEx and
installation costs (Joe Shangraw 2024b, 2024a; Friedman et al. 2024). Discussion with industry

 94

experts indicated that U.S. electrical codes for ac wiring are more robust and challenging to meet
than in other countries. This makes central inverter designs more appealing owing to the higher
amount of dc cabling compared with ac connections and collection cables. Central inverters have
the lowest up-front capital costs for large projects, though utility string inverters are becoming
more cost-competitive (Joe Shangraw 2024b; Vogel 2016). Some high-voltage string inverters
(primarily manufactured by Huawei) are not available in the U.S. market (Joe Shangraw 2024a).

Utility-scale string inverters have been used in the United States to compensate for challenging
terrain, shading conditions, or other power generation imbalances across a utility array. These
same properties of modularity also offer resilience against inverter failures and unreliability.

Inverter choices are also influenced by inverter loading ratios (ILRs), which are calculated as the
PV module capacity in dc divided by the inverter capacity in ac. Systems with ILRs above 1.0
can increase revenue and reduce the variability of solar energy while affecting the quantity and
size of inverters needed per module. In 2024, utility-scale PV systems had a median ILR of 1.33
vs. 1.24 for distributed PV (Figure 63). Generally, ILRs in all sectors have been trending upward
over the past 15 years, with utility-scale ILRs higher than residential or commercial ILRs
(Barbose et al. 2024; Seel and Kemp 2024; Barbose et al. 2025).

                                  Figure 63. ILR by system type, 2010–2024.
 Source: (Barbose et al. 2024; Seel and Kemp 2024; Barbose et al. 2025)

Like all grid infrastructure with telecommunication capabilities, inverters can be targets for
cyberattacks. In 2024, several cyberattacks took place, notably in Lithuania and Japan, which
leveraged vulnerabilities in solar inverter or solar monitoring software (Casey 2025). During
2025 in the United States there were unsubstantiated reports of undocumented communication
devices found in Chinese-made PV inverters and batteries, potentially posing a security risk
(Mcfarlane 2025). According to DER Security Corp, inverters account for 17% of solar
cybersecurity risks (Johnson 2025). Standards and guidelines for cybersecurity of PV and smart
inverters have been published by the National Institute of Standards and Technology and DOE
(Johnson 2017; McCarthy 2024). These guidelines recommend leveraging supply chain
cybersecurity best practices from other industries, including military and medical devices, and
identify supply chain risks such as “third party service providers or vendors with physical or

 95

virtual access to information systems or software, poor information security by lower-tier
suppliers, and compromised software or hardware purchased from suppliers” (Johnson 2017).

5.2.2 Domestic Production of Inverters
Figure 64 shows U.S. inverter manufacturing capacity by country of ownership, categorized by
inverter size. 53 Approximately 30 GWac of total U.S. inverter assembly capacity was established
by the end of 2024 (including TMEIC capacity, which completed construction in November
2024). Most U.S. inverter manufacturing facilities are owned by companies with headquarters
outside of the United States, including Europe, Japan, and Israel.

During 2023, Power Electronics (headquartered in Spain) announced plans to build 20 GWac of
annual capacity to produce central inverters, but land was not reported to have been purchased
until January 2025 (Pedrotti 2025). In 2023, SMA announced plans to establish 3.5 GWac of
annual capacity to produce string inverters. However, in September 2025, SMA reported signing
an agreement with a contract manufacturer to produce an unspecified amount of inverters,
beginning in Q1 2026 (Metea 2025b). An announcement from GE Vernova in early 2025 (GE
Vernova 2025) regarding plans for an unspecified amount of production of central inverters at an
existing GE Vernova facility in Pennsylvania is not included in the chart.

Based on the labor intensity reported in a few press releases (see Section 8.3), we estimate
facilities that produce MLPE create about 200–300 jobs per GWac of annual production, while
facilities assembling larger inverters create about 30–80 jobs per GWac of annual production. If
we assume facilities were effectively operating at half capacity in 2024, this would correspond to
about 1,900 jobs, with about half originating from MLPE facilities.

While 2024 production levels for each facility are not known, we can estimate 45X MPTC use if
all facilities were operating at half capacity. Based on inverter size, we estimate the maximum
45X MPTC total in 2024 was approximately $0.5 billion. However, the actual number probably
varies significantly owing to ramping of new facilities over the year as well as output variation
from a given facility for different inverter products that have differing 45X MPTC values. See
Section 8.2 for more detail.

53
 The location of company or parent company headquarters is organized as “U.S. HQ,” “China HQ,” or “Other HQ”

acquired any assets from a company headquartered in a different country, whether most of the company’s operations
are in a different country from the headquarters of record, or the nationalities of the company’s owners.

5.2.3 Imports/Exports of Inverters
Figure 65 shows the total number of inverters imported into the United States for all industrial
applications, including PV. Other industrial uses include EVs, BESS, consumer electronics, wind
turbines, rail applications, and so forth. Thus, conclusions drawn from these data apply to the
U.S. inverter market broadly and may not represent the PV inverter supply chain precisely.

Overall, 2024 saw a significant drop in inverter imports. The share of imports from China
decreased from 50% to 30% between 2020 and 2024. While Mexico was a primary source of
imports through 2023, their share plummeted abruptly in 2024. In 2024, MLPE manufacturers
Enphase and SolarEdge announced they had ceased manufacturing in Mexico (Jowett 2024;
Misbrener 2025) owing to a downturn in the U.S. residential PV market and increased U.S.
manufacturing capacity. The 2024 decrease in imports from Mexico may or may not be due to
these MLPE manufacturing changes, because these data include more than just PV inverters.

The number of imports from Japan increased from 2020 to 2024, achieving a 15% share of U.S.
imports in 2024. However, most Japanese inverter companies servicing the U.S. PV market
(such as TMEIC and Yaskawa) have set up U.S. manufacturing facilities instead of importing
products from Japan.

The United States exported only 0.5 million inverters across all sectors in 2024, significantly
fewer than it imported. From the data in Figure 64, we can estimate that approximately 4 million
PV inverters were produced in the United States during 2024 and the majority were MLPE
(assuming facilities were operating at approximately half capacity). This would imply at least
80% of domestically produced PV inverters were consumed domestically rather than exported,
and it is possible that this percentage may be even higher. Since the data from Figure 65 do not

 97

specify the end-use industries of the inverters, it is possible that many exported inverters may be
types that are used for non-PV applications.

 Figure 65. U.S. imports of inverters for all industrial applications (not limited to PV sector) by
 country and quantity; U.S. exports by quantity.
 Source: (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a), HTS code 8504.40.9570, which includes any inverter device
 that converts between ac and dc, for PV, BESS, EVs, other consumer electronics, etc.

Figure 66 shows the total customs value of inverter imports. Again, the figure shows data for all
end-use applications, not just PV. If we assume U.S. PV inverter production facilities in Figure
64 were effectively operating at half capacity during 2024, we can estimate the market value of
domestic PV inverters was approximately $2 million, based on inverter cost by type reported in
Section 5.2.4. We can then estimate the 2024 value of PV inverter imports by subtracting $2
million of domestic inverters from the $4 million value of all inverter shipments in the United
States estimated in this work (i.e., domestic manufacturing and imports). 54 This nets a value of
total PV inverter imports of almost $2 million, which is roughly 55% of all inverter imports.

Figure 66 also shows the annual market value of PV inverters installed in the United States, 55
which is slightly lower than the annual market value of U.S. PV inverter shipments. In some
years, the market value of PV inverter installations and shipments both exceed the total customs
value for all inverter imports, primarily because the installations and shipments include domestic
PV inverters. While the exact annual market value of domestic PV inverters is not precisely
known, smaller inverters for residential and commercial applications are both more expensive
per unit and more likely to be produced domestically. Thus, we estimate PV inverters represent a
large amount of the customs value for inverter imports, but a significant amount is attributable to
non-PV applications.

supplied, though could also relate to differences in performance or quality of products. Central
inverters tend to come from Europe, and these tend to have a higher customs value per unit.

Companies headquartered in the United States have a presence in both microinverter and central
inverter shipments. Microinverters were largely supplied by Enphase, which primarily
manufactured outside of the United States (in China, India, Mexico) prior to 2024 (Jacobo

to $157.5 million in 45X MPTCs or roughly 1.4 GWac (Enphase 2025a).

produced in the United States prior to 2025 (plans to do so were announced in early 2025, see
Section 5.2.2). Multiple companies headquartered in Japan or the European Union already have
U.S. inverter assembly capacity (Figure 64), with several more announcing plans in 2024 to
establish or expand such facilities.

make up the largest component portion of the price in all regions. The U.S. and European Union
labor costs are higher than in other regions. Section 301 tariffs apply to certain components of
the inverter assembly in the United States, which add $1.8/kWac.
