5.2.5 Upstream Inputs for Inverters
This section provides limited information on priority inputs for inverter production. Many other
upstream inputs are not discussed here owing to limited supply chain information or relatively
low importance, such as inverter enclosures, cabling, concrete pads, and auxiliary transformers.

 • Power-filtering components (inductors and capacitors): These are required parts of all
 inverters. Figure 71 shows the 2024 quantity and average customs value of imports of
 power capacitors with a rating above 0.5 kilovolt-ampere reactive (kVAR). In 2024,
 China was the largest source of imported power capacitors (43%), followed by Japan
 (14%), Indonesia (12%), and India (12%). This HTS code is not PV-specific, so the data
 probably represent the broader power capacitor market. The appropriate HTS code for
 inductors for PV or power inverter applications is unknown. There are U.S. capacitor and
 inductor manufacturers, but market shares for PV applications are unknown.

 Figure 71. 2024 imports and average customs value of power capacitors, by country and import
 market share.
  Source: (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a), HTS code 8532.10.00, which represents power capacitors
 with a rating above 0.5 kVAR. These are not explicitly used only for solar applications and therefore likely represent
 the larger market for power capacitors.

 • Fuses and breakers 56: These common electrical components are manufactured and
 traded in many places globally, including by U.S.-owned and operating companies (Fuses
 Unlimited 2014; Electricals Informed, n.d.). See Section 5.5.3 for more information.
 • Copper bus 57: The copper bus is formed from high-purity copper, such as
 electrolytically refined tough pitch copper, C11000 (>99.9% purity) (Busbar Machine
 2025; Connor 2020; Metoree 2025a). This high-purity copper is shaped into busbars
 through hot and cold rolling, bending, and drilling methods, and potentially coated with

56
 The trade category includes multiple types of switchgear (fuses, contactors, motor starters, surge suppressors),
where HTS code 8535 is for products greater than 1,000 V, and HTS code 8536 is for those less than 1,000 V.
57
 Copper consumption in the United States during 2024 was 1,800 kt. Of this, 23% was used for electrical and
electronic applications (U.S. Geological Survey 2025f; Copper Development Association Inc. 2024). Copper is
mined (1,100 kt/yr), refined (850 kt/yr), and recycled (150 kt/yr) in the United States. The United States imports as
much refined copper as it produces (810 kt/yr), primarily from Chile (65%) and Canada (17%) (U.S. Geological
Survey 2025f).

 103

 tin, silver, or nickel, depending on the final application (Koprex 2025). A mixture of U.S.
 and foreign-owned companies manufacture copper busbars domestically (Data Bridge
 Market Research 2025; MarkWide Research 2025).
 • Thermal management systems (TMS; heat sinks and fans): Different thermal
 management solutions are required for the different sizes of inverters. Large central
 inverters use fans containing permanent ferrite magnets, heat sinks, and air, water, or oil
 circulation systems. String inverters and microinverters use small heat sinks, fans, PCBA-
 integrated thermal dissipation, and thermal potting compounds to conduct heat away in
 the smaller space (NFION 2025; Smith et al. 2024).
 • PCBAs: Inverters typically use multiple PCBAs, which can vary depending on the size
 of the inverter. Microinverters contain both a dc-dc PCBA as well as a dc-ac PCBA, and
 string and central inverters contain several PCBAs including those for power modules
 and other purposes such as communications and monitoring (US Dep. Treas. 2025).
 Trends in inverter technologies direct the trends in the component technologies, including
 moving to higher voltages, decreasing size, reducing losses, and improving lifetimes and
 reliability (Yole Intelligence 2025). A simplified supply chain diagram is provided in
 Figure 72. From industry interviews, U.S. costs for finished, surface-mount PCBAs
 typically run 25%–40% higher than their overseas counterparts, owing to higher utility,
 labor, rent, and overhead costs. In the IRS safe harbor tables, the PCBA constitutes about
 10% of the inverter cost, up to 30% if it includes the main power switching devices (US
 Dep. Treas. 2025). A report from 2020 indicated that the U.S. share of global PCBA
 production had decreased to 4% (from 30% historically), with four of the top 20
 electronics manufacturing services companies headquartered in the United States (Altium
 2022). However, a variety of PCBA manufacturers operate in the United States, which
 has enabled at least Enphase, SolarEdge, and Yaskawa Solectria Solar to provide
 domestic-content-eligible inverters (SolarEdge 2024; Enphase 2025b). Often, PCBA
 manufacturers are essentially contract manufacturers, able to switch and adapt available
 products, so PCBA manufacturers in the United States could potentially switch to
 providing PV inverter products.

Figure 72. Simplified supply chain diagram of PCBA production for PV inverter applications

 104

 • PCBA equipment: Automated machines for manufacturing PCBAs were imported from
 a variety of countries in 2024, including China, Thailand, Taiwan, Germany, and Mexico
 (HTS code 8479.89.9200) (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a). PCBA pick-
 and-place machines are available from many vendors from numerous countries, including
 many in the United States (PCB Directory 2025). Given the flexibility and availability of
 these tools, they are not considered a supply chain bottleneck.
 • Fiberglass boards: Printed circuit boards (PCBs), which are populated with devices to
 produce PCBAs, are made of layers of glass fiber fabric dip-coated in a flame-retardant
 epoxy resin, known as FR4. Then, a copper foil is laminated to sheets of FR4 (Von Roll
 Composites 2024). Bare PCBs fall under HTS code 8534.00.0020, a multilayer copper
 hard board, under which code U.S. imports from China are subject to a 25% tariff under
 Section 301. Despite this, 50% of U.S. imports of blank PCBs were from China in 2024,
 at an average price of $1.74/board (Figure 73). Unlike finished PCBAs, bare PCBs are
 difficult to source within the United States and are typically priced 300%–500% higher
 than their Asian-produced counterparts, according to industry interviews. The epoxy resin
 coating the glass fiber fabric is a halogenated resin like brominated bisphenol A, typically
 tetrabromobisphenol A diglycidyl ether (TBBPA-DE), using the flame-retardant
 chemical tetrabromobisphenol A (TBBPA). TBBPA is produced by only one or two
 producers in the United States. It is imported from Jordan and Israel, with small
 quantities from China and Japan, and over 90% of production is used for PCBs (U.S.
 HTS code 2908.19.25). However, many electronics companies have been phasing out the
 use of brominated epoxies (The International Bromine Council 2020; Bromine Science
 and Environmental Forum 2012; Material Research L3C 2019).

 Figure 73. Imports of blank PCBs into the United States by country and average unit price.
 Source: HTS code 8534.00.0020 (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a)

 • Solder: There are two primary types of solder, lead-based (usually SnPb 60/40) and lead-
 free (typically a tin-silver-copper “SAC” alloy at >96% Sn, or SnBi). Solder is readily
 available from a variety of vendors.

 105

5.3 Transistors
This section focuses on the production of transistors for the PV inverter supply chain, including
direct inputs to transistor production. Figure 74 shows a simplified supply chain diagram.

5.3.1 Technology Trends for Transistors
Different transistor materials and designs are relevant for different PV inverter applications, as
summarized in Table 15.

Because of the higher switching frequencies, improved power-to-volume and power-to-weight
ratios, and increasing availability at higher voltages, there is some trend in PV inverters toward
SiC MOSFETs instead of silicon MOSFETs or IGBTs (Chiu et al. 2025). SiC is expected to
provide a 5%–10% cost reduction for the system (Joe Shangraw 2024b).

GaN still plays a small role in the device market, although some manufacturers are selling GaN-
based PV microinverters (EPC 2025; Navitas 2021; Jacobo 2024b). The discrete transistor
device market is approximately evenly split between silicon IGBT, silicon MOSFET, and SiC
MOSFET, with SiC rapidly gaining market share since 2020 (Yole Intelligence 2024; Chiu et al.
2025). SiC is primarily used in higher-voltage inverter applications, such as central and string
inverters.

 106

5.3.2 Domestic Production of Transistors
Manufacturing of transistors in the United States is heavily focused on SiC and GaN
technologies, while silicon technologies are split between domestic and foreign (particularly
Chinese) production (Yole Intelligence 2024, 2023). Domestic manufacturers include Onsemi,
LittelFuse, Wolfspeed, Microchip, Vishay, TI, xfab, and others (Yole Intelligence 2024).
Foreign-headquartered companies—including TSMC, Bosch, and Soitec—are investing in
manufacturing in the United States (Yole Intelligence 2024).

Onsemi is one of the three top global manufacturers of power devices, along with Infineon and
STMicroelectronics (Yole Intelligence 2024). Onsemi creates devices in both silicon and SiC,
and it has manufacturing facilities abroad and in New York, New Hampshire, and Pennsylvania.
Its Idaho and Maine facilities were divested to LA Semiconductor and Diodes Inc., respectively,
with wafer supply agreements (Onsemi 2024). Wolfspeed is the only company making
exclusively SiC devices, with U.S.-based facilities in North Carolina, New York, and Arkansas
(Yole Intelligence 2024; Wolfspeed 2024). Microchip also works in SiC and has facilities in
Colorado and Arizona with both PV and BESS applications (Microchip Technology 2025a,
2025b).

There had been a downward trend in U.S.-based semiconductor manufacturing capacity within
certain market segments, reaching 10% to 50% of global market share in 2022 (Thadani and
Allen 2023). However, recent announcements have altered this trend (Morra 2024). Since 2020,
Microchip has planned expansion of silicon and SiC capacities at its Colorado site, Texas
Instruments expanded production into Utah, and Wolfspeed has invested in a North Carolina
manufacturing facility (Yole Intelligence 2024). Foreign-headquartered companies are also
expanding U.S. fabrication capabilities (Yole Intelligence 2024). Many announcements of
expansions domestically and abroad are underpinned by governments developing incentives and
encouraging investment into the semiconductor industry (Semiconductor Industry Association
2024). In the United States, the CHIPS and Science Act (CHIPS) has enabled more than 90
projects across 28 states since 2020 (Semiconductor Industry Association 2024). More than six
new facilities were planned to open between 2024 and 2025 from Intel, Texas Instruments, and
Micron (knometa Research 2023).

5.3.3 Imports/Exports of Transistors
Figure 75 shows imports of power electronic transistors 58 in millions of units by country, plus
average import value. In 2024, a large portion of imports were from Asia: China, the Philippines,
Malaysia, Thailand, Taiwan, Japan, and South Korea. It is unknown what fraction of these total
U.S. imports were used in PV applications.

Figure 76 shows the global market share value in million USD by transistor technology type and
company headquarters. Together, the global market share of U.S. companies is about 34% for
silicon MOSFETs, 10% for IGBTs, 37% for SiC, and 47% for other technologies (including
GaN). The largest power electronics company globally, Infineon, is headquartered in the
Netherlands and holds a market share of about 30% in both silicon MOSFET and IGBT markets
(Yole Intelligence 2024). Six Japanese companies control 40% of the IGBT market and about

 U.S. International Trade Commission data for HTS code 8541.29.0095 represents power transistors that are
58

mounted, with a dissipation rate >1W and an operating frequency <30 MHz, not including photosensitive transistors.

 107

20% of each of the MOSFET and other technologies markets. STMicroelectronics in the
European Union holds about 34% of the share of SiC transistors. These are market values as
opposed to manufacturing capacity. The U.S. semiconductor industry may cumulatively hold
50% of the 2023 global semiconductor market share (Semiconductor Industry Association 2024).

 Figure 76. Global transistor market value in million USD by country headquarters, company, and

Globally, incentives like CHIPS have encouraged investment, enabling manufacturing capacity
expansions. Since 2022, announced or under-construction capacity expansions—in terms of
wafer starts per month—increased capacity 203% in the United States, 124% in the European
Union, and 86% in Japan (Semiconductor Industry Association 2024).

These graphs summarize the global market for transistors, but transistors for U.S. PV inverters
might be better traced through company supply agreements. For example, SolarEdge and
Enphase have supply agreements for silicon MOSFETs with Infineon, which manufactures in
Dresden (Germany) and Kulim (Malaysia) among 15 locations (Infineon Technologies 2023a,
2024, 2023b, 2025; S&P Capital IQ 2019; Infineon Technologies AG 2025). Manufacturers such
as Infineon, Mitsubishi, and Fuji Electric historically contributed over 80% of the global
production capacity of IGBTs. However, Chinese manufacturers, such as BYD, have begun to
significantly expand, and localized production can affect market shares (Fichtner 2023).

5.3.4 Regional Pricing for Transistors
The typical cost of manufacturing a silicon MOSFET includes about 8% for the silicon wafer,
30% for consumables, 20% for labor, 30% for equipment and facilities, and 8% for testing, with
additional costs due to yield losses (Yole Intelligence 2023). News sources indicate that SiC
modules for the EV industry are 20%–30% lower cost from Chinese manufacturers vs. other

 109

global options (Honour Electronic 2025). More research is needed to understand the regional
costs of power transistors.

5.3.5 Upstream Inputs for Transistors
This section provides limited information on priority inputs for transistor production. Many other
upstream inputs are not discussed here owing to limited supply chain information or relatively
low importance, such as the equipment needed to produce the transistors.

 • Wafers: Many announced U.S. manufacturing expansions are related to wafer fabricators
 to support power electronics manufacturing (Semiconductor Industry Association 2024;
 Yole Intelligence 2024). See Section 2.5.
 • Dopants: Silicon transistors are typically doped with boron for p-type and phosphorus,
 arsenic, or antimony for n-type, and they are configured in either an NPN or PNP
 conformation. SiC is doped with beryllium, boron, aluminum, or gallium for p-type and
 nitrogen or phosphorus for n-type. GaN is doped with magnesium for p-type and silicon,
 germanium, or carbon for n-type (Wittmann 2007). Boron is available in the United
 States, but most of the world’s boron production comes from Turkey, and primary uses of
 boron are in ceramics, glasses, detergents, and fertilizers (U.S. Geological Survey 2025s).
 Antimony, gallium, and germanium—which are overwhelmingly produced in China
 (U.S. Geological Survey 2025d, 2025h, 2025i)—have been the subject of trade tensions.
 In December 2024, a Chinese Commerce Ministry directive banned the export of
 antimony, gallium, and germanium to the United States (Lv and Munroe 2024).
 • Contacts: Aluminum was the old standard for metal contacts, but modern transistors are
 more likely to use a combination of copper and tungsten with barrier layers including
 titanium nitride (MKS Inc. 2025). See Appendix B for aluminum and copper information.
 Tungsten can be refined in the United States, including from scrap, but is dominated by
 the Chinese market, which is subject to 30% tariffs, leading to diverse sources of imports
 (U.S. Geological Survey 2025p). Titanium metal is produced by one company in the
 United States, with two more idled facilities, and in 2024 was primarily (>80%) imported
 from Japan (U.S. Geological Survey 2025o).
 • Packaging: Transistor packages can be made of plastic, glass, ceramic, or metal, and
 they can be in surface-mount or through-hole configurations. Power transistors are
 frequently designed to attach to heat sinks, with the drain of the transistor connected to
 the grounded metal enclosure.

5.4 Medium-Voltage Transformers
This section focuses on production of medium-voltage transformers, including direct inputs to
transformer production (Figure 77).

 110

 Figure 77. Supply chain diagram for transformers.
 Based on (Nguyen et al. 2022).

Invented in the 1880s, transformers are relatively simple devices that increase or decrease the
voltage of ac electricity and can provide other services such as isolating circuits. Typically, a
transformer contains an iron or steel core in which two sets of wrapped copper wires (called
windings) inductively transfer electrical current between each other. The number of wire turns on
each side of the core determines the voltage change achieved. Transformers come in multiple
sizes and serve multiple purposes. For example, a large power transformer (LPT) can convert the
thousands of volts coming from a large power plant to the hundreds of thousands of volts needed
for efficient long-distance transmission. The electricity from those transmission lines can be
decreased to tens of thousands of volts via substation transformers for local distribution. Then,
distribution transformers can decrease the voltage to hundreds of volts for supplying individual
homes and businesses (Moseman 2024; Patel 2024b).

This section focuses on medium-voltage transformers while Section 5.5 includes a discussion of
high-voltage transformers. No universal criteria define the boundaries between low-, medium-,
and high-voltage transformers. In this report, medium-voltage transformers are considered to
have high-side voltages of 1–69 kV. Medium-voltage transformers can step voltage down and
up. For example, under one definition, distribution transformers receive input voltage at 34.5 kV
or less and provide output voltage at 600 V or less (U.S. Department of Energy 2025b).
Additionally, transformers designed to step up electricity from solar power plants for connection
to the grid (Figure 78) may receive electricity at around 1 kV or less and output electricity at up
to 35 kV, or even higher (Brunstock 2024; Meta Power Solutions 2025; TTES 2025; Virginia-
Georgia Transformer 2023).

Transformers are also categorized by power ratings. For example, the trade data analyzed in
Section 5.4.3 includes transformers with power ratings of 650–10,000 kVA. However, voltage
class and power ratings do not necessarily correlate. For example, under one definition,
distribution transformers have power capacities of 10–2,500 kVA, while solar step-up
transformers can have higher power capacities, such as 10,000 kVA (Brunstock 2024; U.S.
Department of Energy 2025b; Meta Power Solutions 2025; TTES 2025; Virginia-Georgia

 111

Transformer 2023). In this report, transformers are described by voltage or power ratings,
depending on the data source, and are labeled accordingly.

Multiple aspects of the supply chain are shared across transformer sizes and applications. Step-
up transformers are particularly similar to distribution transformers in terms of supply chains and
manufacturers (McKenna et al. 2024). Despite their basic similarities with off-the-shelf
distribution transformers, PV step-up transformers must be customized for specific systems
based on factors such as voltage, power, climate efficiency, network topology, and noise levels
(Virginia Transformer 2023; Siemens AG 2015; Electrical India Magazine 2020). Similarly, PV-
plus-storage systems have unique transformer needs (Dailymag 2025). This need for
customization exacerbates the challenges to step-up transformer supply posed by the global
shortage of transformers.

 Figure 78. Schematic showing roles of transformers in supplying solar energy.
 ISU = inverter step-up (transformer); HV = high voltage; MV = medium voltage

 112

After a relatively balanced transformer market was achieved in 2019, there was a global
transformer shortage caused by rising demand and slow expansion of supply (Christoffersen
2024). Demand drivers include the increasing need to replace aging and damaged transformers,
surging renewable energy generation, increased electrification, and the proliferation of EVs and
data centers. Important materials used in transformers, such as grain-oriented electrical steel
(GOES), are also constrained by competition from growing markets for electric motors and EV
chargers. At the same time, transformer manufacturers have not expanded their capital-intensive
operations adequately to meet the growing needs, in part because of workforce constraints
(Moseman 2024; McKenna et al. 2024). Disruptions caused by the COVID-19 pandemic also
produced supply chain imbalances (Jacobs et al. 2024). The resulting transformer shortage has
lengthened product lead times. In 2024, developers were expected to wait 1–2 years for smaller
step-up transformers and up to 3–5 years for larger step-up transformers, and the delays could
increase if projected demands outpace expanded supply (Carroll 2024; Chopra et al. 2024).

Transformer demand is expected to continue growing. NLR estimated in February 2024 a U.S.
stock of 60–80 million distribution transformers, equivalent to about 3 TW of installed capacity,
and projects an increase in stock of 160%–260% by 2050. In the same timeframe, increased
renewable energy generation is expected to increase demand by up to 2 TW for step-up
transformers in the 0.5–5 MVA range (McKenna et al. 2024).

5.4.1 Technology Trends for Medium-Voltage Transformers
A variety of technologies could affect the transformer supply chain if they become widespread in
the market. Using nontraditional materials or otherwise reducing traditional material use could
ease supply chain challenges, enhance performance, or both. Building medium-voltage
transformer cores with amorphous metal can reduce core losses and increase efficiency while
reducing reliance on GOES (Dailymag 2025; Patel 2024b). Amorphous metal accounts for about
5% of transformer cores in the United States (the rest use GOES), and the United States has one
manufacturer of amorphous metal cores: Metglas in South Carolina (Dabbs 2023). The United
States also imports amorphous metal products from China (Patel 2024a). Amorphous metal
transformers are more widely used elsewhere—including Canada, India, China, the European
Union, and the United Kingdom—and they are considered a mature technology (McCoy et al.
2020). In 2023, DOE proposed a rule requiring almost 90% of all covered distribution
transformers to use amorphous metal cores by 2027, but the rule was revised in 2024 to only
“support a 10% to 25% increase in the availability” of amorphous metal distribution
transformers. Opponents of the initial rule worried that making the manufacturing and supply
chain changes necessary to largely replace GOES cores with amorphous metal cores would
exacerbate transformer shortages (Patel 2024b; Walton 2024; Energy Conservation Program:
Energy Conservation Standards for Distribution Transformers 2024).

Emerging technologies could affect the transformer supply chain in the future. For transformer
cores, innovations include nanocrystalline cores, three-dimensional printing of hollow cores, and
use of high-temperature superconductors. Alternative transformer insulation approaches could
improve longevity, address environmental concerns, and improve fire safety. Solid-state, “smart”
transformers employ semiconductors and power electronics to invert dc power to ac power at the
same time they change the voltage—and to communicate with grid operators—so they can
effectively manage power flow in grids that include PV and wind. Artificial intelligence-driven
digital twins simulate transformer operations and stresses to enhance design and maintenance

 113

while improving grid reliability, which could be particularly helpful for improving the longevity
of transformers subject to intermittent PV generation. Finally, several advancements aim to make
transformers more flexible and mitigate the constraints inherent with needing specialized
transformers (Dailymag 2025; Moseman 2024; Patel 2024b).

5.4.2 Domestic Production of Medium-Voltage Transformers
Within the broader U.S. transformer market, greater shares of relatively small transformers are
produced domestically compared with the share of domestically manufactured large
transformers. About 20% of high-voltage transformers are supplied domestically vs. 50%–60%
of distribution transformers (Martucci 2025). Although domestic production statistics specific to
medium-voltage solar step-up transformers are unavailable, they are likely similar to the
statistics for distribution transformers owing to the similarities between these two medium-
voltage product types (McKenna et al. 2024). Table 16 lists major transformer manufacturers
with U.S. production facilities.

Domestic manufacturers have been slow to expand as demand has grown. Transformer
manufacturing is capital-intensive and can require decades of production to pay back initial
investments, which has discouraged new investments. Manufacturers have also pointed to
constraints on production from shortages of materials, equipment, and skilled labor (Jacobs et al.
2024). Additional barriers to domestic expansion include the nonstandard nature of transformer
designs—which hinders efficient mass production—and uncertainty about the cost and
regulation of electrical steel (National Infrastructure Advisory Council 2024). At the same time,
government funding for domestic transformer manufacturing has not materialized to offset the
cost advantage of manufacturing overseas (see Section 5.4.4) (Jacobs et al. 2024).

Although the expansion of transformer manufacturing has been slow for the past several years, it
is starting to ramp up in the United States. In 2024 and 2025, for example, Siemens announced
expansion of a transformer factory in North Carolina; Hitachi announced expansions in Virginia,
Missouri, and Mississippi; Eaton announced expansion in South Carolina; and Power and Data
Management opened a factory in Utah. Some of these companies and others have announced
expansions outside the United States (Skidmore 2024; Patel 2024b; Walton 2025). Sunbelt
Solomon has established facilities to repair and recycle transformers (Jacobs et al. 2024).
However, these efforts “won’t ease the logjam soon,” according to the Institute of Electrical and
Electronics Engineers (IEEE) (Moseman 2024). At the end of 2024, Rystad noted that most
major manufacturing expansions will be commissioned in 1–2 years and that refurbishment and
life cycle extension strategies are limited by technical complexity (Christoffersen 2024).

5.4.3 Imports/Exports of Medium-Voltage Transformers
Between 2015 and 2024, U.S. imports of medium-voltage three-phase transformers (650–10,000
kVA 59) increased significantly by value and volume, with Mexico typically the largest supplier
by both metrics (Figure 79, Figure 80). Available data are insufficient to identify all companies
manufacturing medium-voltage transformers in Mexico. However, Prolec GE and Virginia
Transformer reported manufacturing medium-voltage transformers in Mexico for the North
American market (Prolec Energy 2025; Virginia-Georgia Transformer 2025). The total value of
the North American medium-voltage transformer market was approximately $2.19 billion in
2024 (Future Market Insights 2024). Based on these figures, imports accounted for roughly 65%
of total market value in that year. While this does not isolate utility-scale PV step-up
transformers, it reflects the broader U.S. dependence on imported medium-voltage transformers,
particularly from Mexico. In addition to Mexico, the United States imports a significant value of
medium-voltage transformers from South Korea, Canada, Colombia, and China.

The transformer products under these HTS codes are not subject to base import duties in the
United States. Imports from free trade agreement partner countries such as Canada, Mexico,
South Korea, and others also qualify for zero tariffs, but they may still be subject to Section 301
tariffs or other trade enforcement measures from certain countries, such as China, not reflected in
the base schedule (U.S. Trade Representative 2018, 2024b).

59
 Definitions of medium-voltage transformers vary across sources. While the U.S. import data cited here (HTS
codes 8504.22.0040 and 8504.22.0080) include transformers rated between 650 and 10,000 kVA, other sources
define medium-voltage transformers based on kV output (typically 1–69 kV), or focus on narrower kVA bands such
as 1,000–2,500 kVA. As such, comparisons between trade data, market value reports, and application-specific
classifications (e.g., PV step-up transformers) should be interpreted with caution.

5.4.4 Regional Costs for Medium-Voltage Transformers
The cost of domestically manufactured 2.8-MVA transformers in 2024 were estimated at about
$0.03/Wdc when used in a 100-MWdc single-axis tracking system (U.S. Department of Energy
2024d). Regional cost differences for step-up transformers are difficult to ascertain. One estimate
of differences for LPTs suggests that U.S.-made transformers are about 10% more expensive
than those made in Mexico, Canada, and Europe, while LPTs manufactured in Asia-Pacific
countries, such as China and South Korea, could sell at a discount of 25% or more to U.S.
products, without considering tariffs (Nguyen et al. 2022). Section 301 tariffs on transformers
imported from China (25% in 2024) increased the cost of those products compared with the cost
of products from other countries.

 116

Transformer prices increased by 60%–80% between 2020 and 2024, including an 80% increase
for distribution transformers (Chopra et al. 2024). Although price data specific to medium-
voltage solar step-up transformers are unavailable, they are likely similar to the price data for
distribution transformers owing to the similarities between these two medium-voltage product
types (McKenna et al. 2024). During the 2020–2024 period, GOES prices almost doubled and
copper prices increased by around 40%, as manufacturers struggled to ramp back up adequately
after COVID-era production cuts (Chopra et al. 2024).

Figure 81 shows an estimated cost breakdown for an “average” transformer. Soft costs constitute
55% of the cost and materials the other 45%. High demand has supported the relatively high
margin (Chopra et al. 2024).

5.4.5 Upstream Inputs for Medium-Voltage Transformers
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to the production of medium-voltage
transformers. Many other upstream inputs are not discussed here due to limited supply chain
information or relatively low importance, such as enclosures, cables, and concrete pads. Power
converters, electronic equipment, and semiconductors are also important to the transformer
industry (Patel 2024b).

The most critical and potentially constrained inputs to transformer manufacturing include GOES
and CTC copper wire, both of which have limited production capacities in the United States
(Table 17). Only one location of one U.S. manufacturer—the Zanesville, Ohio, plant owned by
Cleveland-Cliffs—produces GOES. Cleveland-Cliffs also manufactures non-oriented electrical
steel (NOES), as does U.S. Steel in Arkansas. NOES is used in various electrical machines,
including EV motors, but cannot be used in place of GOES in transformers. Facilities that
produce NOES can be upgraded to produce GOES, although no plans to do so among U.S.

 117

facilities were known as of 2024 (Putsche et al. 2024). In fact, the global trend toward increasing
NOES production for EV applications may constrain GOES supplies, because the same types of
manufacturing facilities can be modified to make either material (Nguyen et al. 2022).

In 2024, domestically manufactured GOES was sufficient to supply the equivalent of only about
20% of U.S. transformer demand. Amorphous metal cores (see Section 5.4.1) hold potential to
reduce dependence on GOES (Chopra et al. 2024).

Transformer manufacturers have expressed concern that U.S. GOES production may disappear
because of competition from lower-cost imports (Putsche et al. 2024). Recent producers of
GOES have included Brazil, China, Czech Republic, France, Germany, India, Japan, Poland,
Russia, and South Korea (Nguyen et al. 2022). In addition, GOES manufactured anywhere in the
world can be imported into other countries, such as Canada and Mexico—who do not produce
GOES themselves—where it is used to make transformer cores that are exported to the United
States without paying U.S. tariffs on steel (Martucci 2025). This approach also threatens the
viability of U.S. GOES manufacturing (Putsche et al. 2024). GOES manufacturing is highly
technical, and the U.S. market for GOES is limited, which reduces the attractiveness of
establishing additional manufacturing capacity in the absence of economic incentives (Martucci
2025). In any case, some transformer manufacturers diversify their GOES supply, using both
domestic and international sources (Nguyen et al. 2022).

CTC copper wire is used in transformer coil winding to minimize losses and hot-spot
temperatures and to enable compact winding with improved short-circuit performance. We
estimate only two U.S. companies can produce it (Table 17), and LPT manufacturers have
identified it as a supply concern. The greatest concern stems from the lack of substitute material
for use in transformer manufacturing. The cost-competitiveness of U.S. versus global CTC
copper wire manufacturers is not well understood (Nguyen et al. 2022).

In addition to GOES and CTC copper, transformer components such as tap changers, bushings,
and insulating material are potential sources of supply chain bottlenecks (Nguyen et al. 2022;
Putsche et al. 2024). Raw copper and aluminum are also important to transformer production, as
they are important to multiple other components covered in this report (see Appendix B). In
2024, U.S. imports of aluminum were subject to 10% Section 232 tariffs, with exceptions, while
U.S. copper imports were not subject to Section 232 tariffs. 60

60
  Section 232 tariffs were implemented for copper (50%) in July 2025 and increased for aluminum (50%) in June
2025 (Hammond and Kitamura 2025; Kitamura 2025).

5.5 Other Electrical Components
This section focuses on the supply chain of major electrical components primarily used in the
utility-scale sector, including high-voltage transformers, combiner boxes, switchgear, circuit
breakers, substation components, and transmission components. Direct inputs into the production
of these components are also discussed.

High-voltage transformers enable step-up or step-down voltage transformation between
generation, transmission, and distribution systems. In utility-scale PV plants, high-voltage
transformers are primarily used to step up voltage from medium voltage, typically 34.5 kV or
lower, to transmission-level voltages greater than 69 kV. These transformers are usually located
at the plant’s point of interconnection substation and are designed to handle large power flows.

Combiner boxes are used in commercial and utility-scale PV systems to aggregate outputs from
several module strings into one main circuit before sending it to the inverter. They help reduce
wiring complexity and cost by minimizing the number of conductors running to the inverter.
Each string feeds into the combiner box through a fuse for overcurrent protection, and most
modern boxes include surge protection devices, string-level monitoring, and disconnect switches
to simplify maintenance and improve safety. These boxes are typically mounted near the PV
array and, for ground-mount applications, built with weather-resistant, noncorrosive materials to
handle outdoor conditions (CHINT Group 2023; Vickerman and Farhat 2025; VIOX Electric
2024).

 119

In utility-scale solar and storage systems, electrical BOS components such as switchgear, circuit
breakers, substation components, and transmission infrastructure play a central role in managing
power quality, ensuring grid compliance, and enabling system protection and control. Switchgear
includes assemblies that isolate and protect downstream equipment through disconnect switches,
fuses, and circuit breakers, often deployed on both ac and dc sides of the system depending on
project configuration. Circuit breakers, a subset of switchgear, serve as automatic protective
devices that interrupt current flow during faults, and their rating and response time directly
impact system reliability and arc flash safety (Eaton 2025; Schneider Electric USA 2025).

Substation components include voltage and current transformers, busbars, protection relays,
surge arresters, and grounding, engineered to manage voltage transformation and protection and
to serve as interconnection points with the broader grid (Figure 82). In a PV substation, busbars
link collector system feeders from inverters to main breakers and transformers that step-up
voltage to the transmission level. Protection relays monitor electrical parameters (voltage,
current, frequency, etc.) and initiate breaker trips during abnormal or fault conditions (e.g., short
circuits, overcurrent, undervoltage). They provide selective coordination to isolate only the
faulted section, preventing damage to equipment and maintaining safety and grid stability. Surge
arresters protect substation equipment from high-voltage transients caused by lightning strikes,
switching surges, or faults. They absorb and divert excess energy to ground, preventing
insulation failure in critical components such as transformers and breakers (Electrical
Engineering Portal 2025; Electrical Tech 2022).

At the transmission level, components such as high-voltage conductors, transmission towers,
insulators, and grounding systems facilitate bulk power delivery across long distances, with
designs informed by NEC and IEEE standards. Transmission line conductors are typically made
of aluminum over a steel core for strength and are left uninsulated, relying on air for electrical
insulation. To prevent the energized conductors from contacting grounded towers, the conductors
are suspended from towers using insulators. These insulators are either horizontal post-type or
suspension-type and must withstand both the continuous operating voltage and occasional surges
from switching or lightning. Traditional insulators are porcelain or glass, which require periodic
maintenance to avoid flashover, while newer polymer or silicon types are lighter and more
durable. A standard ac transmission circuit consists of three phases, with each phase using one
conductor below 200 kV and bundled conductors (two or more per phase) above 200 kV to
increase current capacity and reduce losses. Ground wires (shield wires) run atop towers to
protect from lightning and may include fiber optic cables for communication. Reliable
communications and automated controls are essential for safe transmission system operations
and outage management (Aspen Environmental Group 2014).

 120

 Figure 82. Schematic of utility-scale PV system connected to step-up substation

5.5.1 Technology Trends for Other Electrical Components
High-voltage transformers: High-voltage transformers are typically rated above 69 kV and are
engineered to handle moderate to large power flows (e.g., >10,000 kVA). These transformers are
usually core-type, oil-filled units with oil natural air natural or oil natural air forced cooling
systems, built to handle high thermal and dielectric stresses over decades of operation (Siemens
Energy 2024).

Combiner boxes: Modern combiner box designs often include remote monitoring for voltage,
current, and surge protector status, improving fault detection and uptime. Enclosures are
typically made of powder-coated steel or fiberglass-reinforced plastic for durability in harsh
outdoor conditions. Recent trends include smart monitoring with Internet-of-Things functionality
and even early integrations with energy storage systems to support hybrid configurations. These
advancements are aimed at improving reliability, reducing downtime, and streamlining utility-
scale deployment (Weidmüller 2025; Yaskawa – Solectria Solar 2025).

Switchgear: For a 100-MWdc PV plant, switchgear is typically medium voltage (15–35 kV
class) on the ac collection side. Metal-enclosed or metal-clad configurations are preferred for arc
flash safety, compactness, and serviceability. Indoor-rated units (National Electrical
Manufacturers Association [NEMA] 1) are used in inverter stations, while outdoor-rated (NEMA
3R or 4X) variants are deployed at main substations. Vacuum-interrupting technology dominates

 121

owing to low maintenance and reliable arc quenching. Air-insulated switchgear is common for
cost-sensitive applications, but gas-insulated switchgear is gaining traction for footprint-limited
sites or areas with high pollution levels (Eaton 2025).

Circuit breakers: Medium-voltage vacuum circuit breakers rated for 15–38 kV and 1,200–3,000
A continuous current are standard in PV collector systems. For substation-level interconnection
(e.g., 115 or 230 kV), SF₆ gas-insulated or vacuum-interruption high-voltage breakers are used.
Trends include digital monitoring for contact wear, predictive maintenance via partial discharge
sensors, and breaker-integrated protection relays (ABB 2025c; IEEE Standards Association
2021; Siemens Energy 2025a, 2025c).

Busbars: Busbars in switchgear and substations are typically made of tin- or silver-plated copper
or aluminum and sized based on current ratings (e.g., 2,000–5,000 A). In outdoor substations,
tubular aluminum busbars are preferred for their corrosion resistance, mechanical strength, and
thermal expansion properties. Insulated busbars (bus duct or busway) are used in compact
medium-voltage switchgear (Siemens Energy 2025b).

Protection relays: Digital or numerical relays are standard across PV substations for
overcurrent, differential, distance, and undervoltage protection. Relays integrate supervisory
control and data acquisition, phasor measurements, and International Electrotechnical
Commission (IEC) 61850 protocols. Key trends include all-in-one multifunction relays, settings
based on human-machine interface, and cybersecurity hardening (ABB 2025a; Schweitzer
Engineering Laboratories 2025).

Surge arresters: Metal-oxide varistor surge arresters with polymer housings are standard at
inverters, switchgear, and substations. Discharge ratings range from 10 kA (medium voltage) to
100 kA (high voltage). Polymeric designs outperform porcelain in weight, handling, and
pollution resistance (Hitachi Energy 2025; IEEE Standards Association 2020; Siemens Energy
2025d).

Grounding: Grounding includes copper-clad steel rods, grid mesh, and bonding to equipment
frames, with design driven by IEEE Standard 80 and National Electrical Code (NEC) 250.
Proper design is critical for step and touch voltage limits. Exothermic welding and corrosion-
resistant conductors are standard. Digital tools and fall-of-potential testing verify grounding
integrity (IEEE Standards Association 2015; NFPA 2025; nVent ERICO 2025).

Transmission conductors: Common conductors for 115–230-kV transmission from 100-MWdc
PV sites include aluminum conductor steel reinforced (e.g., 477 MCM Hawk or 795 MCM
Drake). High-temperature low-sag options such as aluminum conductor composite core and
aluminum conductor steel-supported are gaining traction for thermal upgrades or long-distance
routing. Bare aluminum conductors are standard, relying on air insulation and ground clearance
(CTC Global 2025; Southwire 2025).

Transmission towers/poles: Lattice steel towers and tubular monopoles are used based on
distance, aesthetics, and right-of-way considerations. For short transmission (<10 miles),
galvanized monopoles are typical. Modular bolted designs simplify logistics, and drones or lidar

 122

are increasingly used in planning and inspection (Power Line Systems 2025; Sabre Industries
2025; Valmont Utility 2024).

Electrical insulator: Silicone rubber polymer insulators dominate owing to their light weight,
resistance to vandalism, and better performance in contaminated environments. Post-type
insulators are used in substations, while suspension/tension types are used on lines. Composite
designs reduce flashover risk and simplify handling (Hubbell Power Systems 2025; IEEE
Standards Association 2002; Lapp Insulators US 2025).

5.5.2 Domestic Production of Other Electrical Components
While several manufacturers have existing and announced U.S.-based facilities producing
various electrical components, comprehensive estimates of U.S. domestic manufacturing
capacity or supply sufficiency are very limited. The following summary notes leading domestic
manufacturers in select areas.

High-voltage transformers: Extensive research on LPTs helps in understanding high-voltage
transformer supply chains more broadly. Domestic manufacturers have a maximum annual
capacity of around 343 LPT units, operating at approximately 40% utilization. This limited
capacity has led to extended lead times, often exceeding 2 years (U.S. Department of Energy
2024b). Initiatives have been undertaken to bolster domestic transformer manufacturing.
Siemens Energy announced plans to establish its first U.S. LPT manufacturing facility in
Charlotte, North Carolina (AP News 2024). Westrafo, an Italian firm, is constructing a medium-
and high-voltage transformer plant in Trotwood, Ohio, with production expected to commence in
July 2025 (Anselmo 2024). HD Hyundai Power Transformers USA invested over $14 million to
expand its Montgomery, Alabama, facility, aiming to increase productivity by 10%. Eaton has
invested $340 million in a new transformer manufacturing facility in South Carolina, focusing on
three-phase transformers. However, it is unclear how much of these reported new capacities will
directly support the specific power and voltage class of high-voltage transformers used in PV
plants (≥10 MVA, ≥69 kV). More granular data on domestic high-voltage transformer
production lines distinct from LPTs are unavailable.

Combiner boxes: Olson Motor & Control Co. produces UL 1741-rated combiner boxes from its
facilities in Hillsborough, New Jersey, and Colorado Springs, Colorado (Assembly Magazine
2011). RAND Manufacturing Group operates out of Lakemoor, Illinois, offering customizable,
UL-listed combiner boxes in NEMA 4X enclosures, with string counts ranging from 10 to 24
(Stahlin 2024). ATI Electrical Supply, a U.S.-based supplier, offers a range of 1,000-V and
1,500-V PV combiner boxes with load-break-rated disconnect switches and watertight designs,
though manufacturing locations are not public (ATI Electrical Supply 2025). Weidmuller
announced manufacturing of PV combiner boxes at a new production facility in Virginia
(Pickerel 2025e). Additional major U.S. suppliers include Bentek, Shoals Technologies Group,
and Terrasmart, all of which offer UL-listed PV combiner boxes for utility-scale and commercial
PV markets.

Switchgear: Major domestic manufacturers of switchgear include General Electric, Schneider
Electric, and ABB. Mitsubishi Electric Power Products is constructing an $86 million switchgear
factory in Beaver County, Pennsylvania, expected to be operational by summer 2026 (Saqib and
Karman 2024; Suttles 2025).

 123

Circuit breakers: Schneider Electric is investing $46 million to expand circuit breaker
production at its plants in Lexington, Kentucky, and Lincoln, Nebraska. The company also
operates a large facility in El Paso, Texas, which is its fourth in the city and now its largest U.S.
manufacturing operation with about 1,500 employees (Schneider Electric 2022, 2023).
Additional manufacturers with U.S. circuit breaker production include Eaton, ABB, and Hitachi
Energy, with the latter recently doubling high-voltage equipment output at its facility in Mount
Pleasant, Pennsylvania.

Busbars: Manufacturers such as Mersen and Vertiv produce laminated busbars domestically.
Vertiv has expanded its production capacity with facilities in Anderson, South Carolina (Mersen
2025; Spherical Insights 2023; Vertiv 2024).

Protection relays: Key U.S.-based manufacturers of relays include Schweitzer Engineering
Laboratories, Beckwith Electric Co., and NOARK Electric North America. Schweitzer leads
with five domestic facilities in Washington, Idaho, Illinois, and Indiana. Beckwith Electric
designs and manufactures all its protective relays at its Largo, Florida, facility. NOARK Electric
operates a distribution center in Pomona, California, though its manufacturing is primarily
international (Metoree 2025b; Mordor Intelligence 2024).

Surge arresters. The North American surge arrester market is moderately consolidated, with
major players like ABB, Eaton, Emerson Electric, Schneider Electric, and Siemens operating
domestically. These companies manufacture a range of surge protection devices suitable for
various applications (Thomas 2025b).

Transmission conductors: The U.S. transmission conductor industry includes several
established firms—such as Southwire, Prysmian, and CTC Global—along with newer entrants
developing advanced technologies. For instance, TS Conductor announced a $134 million
investment in a new manufacturing facility in South Carolina to produce high-capacity power
lines (Spartanburg News 2025; T&D World 2025).

Electrical insulators: Leading U.S. manufacturers of electrical insulators include TE
Connectivity, Hubbell Power Systems, and Victor Insulators. The U.S. electric towers and poles
market is supported by manufacturers such as AECOM, Burns & McDonnell, and Kiewit
Corporation, who are involved in the installation, repair, and replacement of electrical
infrastructure. These companies contribute to the domestic production and maintenance of
transmission towers (Global Market Insights Inc. 2024; Powertelcom 2021). ABB’s Blackburn
brand offers grounding systems, including compression, mechanical, and exothermic connectors,
manufactured in the United States. Companies such as Special Technical Services, Inc. produce
grounding cables and related accessories domestically (ABB 2025b; Thomas 2025a).

5.5.3 Imports/Exports of Other Electrical Components
Based on available data for LPTs, the United States has relied on imports for about 80% of high-
voltage transformers in recent years (U.S. Government Accountability Office 2023; U.S.
Department of Energy 2024a), suggesting that most high-voltage transformers used in U.S. PV
systems are also imported. As shown in Figure 83, key suppliers of high-voltage transformers
(>10,000 kVA) include Mexico, South Korea, and Brazil. Imports surged in 2023 and 2024,
reflecting domestic shortfalls, increased demand, and lower costs of imports.

 124

Trade data specific to other PV-related electrical components were unavailable. Thus, no trade
analysis is provided here.

 Figure 83. High-voltage transformer (>10,000 kVA) import customs value by country, 2015–2024
                                        (HTS 8504.23.00)
According to Chapter 85 of the U.S. International Trade Commission’s Harmonized Tariff
Schedule (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a), imports under HTS 8504.23.00
generally face a base Most Favored Nation duty rate of 1.5%, though actual applied tariffs may
vary based on origin. Transformers from free trade agreement countries (e.g., Mexico, South
Korea, Canada) often enter duty-free, while imports from China are subject to Section 301 tariffs
of up to 25%. Additional AD/CVD or Section 232 tariffs may apply to specific countries or
transformer types, based on trade rulings. For instance, LPTs from South Korea remained under
a continuing AD duty as of 2024 (U.S. International Trade Administration 2024).

Based on the HTS chapter documents 73, 74, 76, and 85 (U.S. International Trade Commission
2025c), U.S. imports of all the other electrical components used in a PV system generally face
base Most Favored Nation tariff rates of 0%–5%. For example, combiner boxes and many
switchgear and circuit breaker components covered under HTS 8536 and 8535 series often fall
within the 2.7%–3.7% range while protection relays and surge arresters can range up to 5%.
Aluminum and copper busbars (HTS 7604, 7407) typically have base rates of 1.5%–5%,
depending on alloy type and shape. Conductors under HTS 7614 and structural components such
as steel poles and grounding equipment (HTS 730820, 7326908605) often carry a 0% base tariff
but may be subject to additional Section 301 or 232 tariffs depending on country of origin.
Countries under a free trade agreement with the United States—such as Canada, Mexico, and
South Korea—benefited from duty-free access for most of these components in 2024. However,
imports from countries like China may be subject to additional 301 tariffs of up to 25% and, in
some cases, additional tariffs related to AD/CVD or Section 232.

5.5.4 Regional Pricing for Other Electrical Components
Detailed regional pricing comparisons for the other electrical components covered here were
unavailable. However, U.S. national benchmark costs of certain electrical components were
available from DOE analysis (Ramasamy et al. 2025): $12/kWac for combiner boxes; $8/kWac for
switchgear; $19/kWac for circuit breakers; $26/kWac for transmission poles, towers, and cables;
$21/kWac for dc and ac conductors; and $9.5/kWac for grounding equipment. Based on 2024
import data using HTS codes, the customs value 61 of high-voltage transformers averaged
approximately $10/kVA. Cost data for other electrical components—such as protection relays,
surge arresters, and insulators—remain limited.

information or relatively low importance, such as cables and other conductors. Supply chains for
upstream inputs such as steel, aluminum, zinc, and copper are detailed in Appendix B. The
diverse range of other primary input materials used in electrical components (Table 18) requires
further analysis.

6 Energy Storage
6.1 Overview
This section focuses on BESS, which is used in stationary applications. Most LIB capacity
manufactured globally and in the United States goes to EVs, but stationary energy storage is
growing. BESS represented around 6% of global and 12% of U.S. battery sales in 2020 (Volta
Foundation 2025), growing to 14% of global and 22% of U.S. battery sales in 2024 (Rho Motion
2025; BNEF 2025a).

In 2024, U.S. installed utility-scale BESS capacity grew by 66%, and 28% of new residential PV
systems were paired with BESS (Solar Energy Industries Association 2025, 2023; U.S. Energy
Information Administration 2025b). In 2024, 41 GWh and 13 GWac of stand-alone and PV-
coupled BESS were installed in the United States, representing 24% of global BESS installations
(Volta Foundation 2025). About 90% of U.S. and global BESS installations in 2024 were utility-
scale projects (Allison Feeney et al. 2025).

Outside the BESS market, manufactured batteries were used in EVs, portable consumer
electronics, and other markets. The primary demand specifications for each market—highest
energy density and fastest charging times for EVs, good energy density and low cost for BESS—
drive the principal differences between battery chemistry and size. Within the battery cell itself,
the 2024 U.S. EV market primarily used lithium/nickel/manganese/cobalt (NMC) battery
cathodes or lithium/nickel/cobalt/aluminum (NCA) cathodes, while BESS favored lithium/iron
(Fe)/phosphate (LFP) battery cathodes. EV battery capacities averaged around 70 kWh (EV
Database 2025), while typical utility-scale BESS had storage capacities of 3–6 MWh.

Figure 84 shows the primary components within a utility-scale BESS. Smaller, distributed BESS
uses much of the same nomenclature but has fewer subcomponents. The first component, the
battery cell, stores electrical power as a dc chemical potential and includes an anode (typically
graphite), a cathode (typically LFP for BESS), and electrolyte materials, in addition to packaging
and electrode foils and separation films. Cells are stacked and packaged into modules/packs that
contain charge-monitoring and protection equipment; module-level thermal, electrical, and fire-
protective systems, and secondary battery management system (BMS) and TMS. Numerous
modules/packs are integrated within a container, which includes a primary BMS (to coordinate
protection equipment and distribution of current) and a primary TMS (a convective air system or,
less commonly, a system of water/glycol liquid cooling that circulates across individual
modules).

Increasingly, BESS include their own inverter, which helps provide direct connection to an ac
source/load. Utility-scale BESS (and commercial or residential BESS) with a built-in inverter are
typically called ac block BESS, while those without an inverter inside are typically called dc
block BESS. A dc block BESS couples to an inverter in a separate container. Benefits of ac
coupling include a more extensive safety record, simpler code requirements, product
interchangeability, and reduced susceptibility to electro-corrosion.

BESS products are specified by rated storage capacity and rated output power, the ratio of which
is the discharge duration. The discharge duration represents how long a fully charged battery can

 128

be reliably called upon to discharge continuously at its rated output power. Although storage
capacity is primarily determined by the size (mass) of the battery, it is also affected by the
discharge rate. To determine the rated storage capacity, battery manufacturers first choose the
rated output power—an engineering optimization based on many factors, balancing energy
efficiency, longevity, and cost (which favor lower power) against product value to the customer
(which favors higher power). When an inverter is included, it is sized to deliver the desired
output power. This optimization typically results in duration times of 1–5 hours, with 4 hours
being predominant for utility-scale BESS. Once the output power rating and discharge duration
are selected, the rated storage capacity is their product. When an inverter is included, its power
rating determines the rated output power for the BESS, making the rated storage capacity an ac
quantity (MWhac). When an inverter is not included, the rated output power is determined by the
battery itself, making the rated storage capacity a dc quantity (MWhdc). When inverters may or
may not be included, the rated storage capacity can be expressed generically (MWhcap).

 Figure 84. Taxonomy of utility-scale BESS cost modeling including lists of component parts
Since the first commercial introduction of LIBs in 1991, development of batteries for EVs
opened new supply chains, lowered costs by economies of scale, and found extension to BESS.
The first LIB cells in 1991 sold for around $9,500/kWhdc 62 in 2024 dollars (Ziegler and Trancik
2021). As the annual market size grew by a factor of 10,000 between 1992 and 2020, the real
price for LIB cells declined 20% for every doubling of production—compounding 13% relative
annual cost declines every year for 24 years (Ziegler and Trancik 2021). The LIB cell costs
learning curve continued from 2020 to 2024, and LFP cell prices in China reached an average
price less than $60/kWhdc (Jayanthan 2025). Full dc blocks in China sold for around $100/kWhdc
in 2024 (Jayanthan 2025). In 2024, U.S. LFP cell prices were $80/kWhdc, and utility-sized LFP
dc block BESS averaged $148/kWhdc, an almost 50% reduction from the 2022 average U.S.

62
  In this report, “kWhdc” units are used for a battery cell, battery module, or BESS without an inverter, “kWhac”
units are used for a BESS with an inverter, and “kWhcap” units are used for a BESS which may or may not include
an inverter.

 129

price of $270/kWdc (Murray 2024). Because most components used in U.S. BESS come from
China, U.S. shipping costs are higher than those in China. High U.S. costs are exacerbated by
25% Section 301 tariffs for the relevant materials, parts, and manufacturing equipment as well as
labor costs that are 3–5 times higher than in other battery manufacturing regions.

After a rapid buildup, approximately 61 GWh of U.S. utility-scale BESS manufacturing capacity
existed at the end of 2024. In the same year, 22 GWh of BESS were imported into the United
States, mainly from China. Companies headquartered in the United States own over half of U.S.
utility-scale BESS manufacturing capacity, while Chinese-headquartered companies own over
25%. Residential BESS pack manufacturing also continues to scale, with an additional more than
5 GWh of manufacturing capacity at the end of 2024, largely from Tesla. While U.S. non-BESS
battery cell production in 2024 was about 109 GWh, U.S. BESS cell production was
insignificant. Multiple companies started cell production in the first half of 2025. Little U.S.
anode and cathode manufacturing capacity exists, all of which came online after 2022.
Significant new U.S. upstream materials capacity is under construction or has been announced.

The following sections provide supply chain information for utility-scale and residential BESS,
BESS cells, and BESS inverters/converters.

6.2 Utility-Scale BESS
This section focuses on the supply chain and production of utility-scale dc block BESS. Figure
85 shows components that make up a BESS and some of the upstream components needed to
produce systems within the BESS. The inclusion of an inverter in the BESS is not required but is
increasingly popular in systems sold in the United States.

 Figure 85. Abridged supply chain diagram for utility-scale BESS

 130

6.2.1 Technology Trends for Utility-Scale BESS
BESS products are specified by duration: how long a fully charged battery can discharge at its
output power rating. Multiplying duration (hours) by output power (typically kilowatts or
megawatts) defines battery storage capacity. One representative utility-scale BESS product in
2024 could provide around 2 MWac rated power for 2 hours, or 1 MWac power for 4 hours—
either way offering up to 4 MWhac capacity (Tesla 2025a). Other 20-foot utility BESS containers
available to U.S. project developers in 2024 provided different power ratings and durations and
overall rated capacities as high as 6.3 MWhac and as low as 3.5 MWhac (Volta Foundation 2025).
Up to 9 MWhac per container may become available in 2025 (CATL 2025), but dc block sizes
are limited by weight, which determines the size of vehicles and cranes required to transport
systems. Cell-to-block technology advances have primarily come through developments in BMS
and TMS capabilities: new hardware and software to control more battery modules and
engineering solutions around the problem of having more thermal stressors within a smaller
space. Through more rigorous product testing and qualification, BESS reliability has also
improved. A typical industry warranty period was 20 years in 2024.

Recent utility-scale BESS enclosures are typically 20 ft long by 8 ft wide by 8.5 ft high.
Containers as long as 40 ft were observed prior to 2022, which allowed for walk-through
inspections and maintenance within the cabinet, but larger cabinets do not align with project
developer needs to minimize land use. Twenty-foot containers are more amenable to standard
crane operations and semi-truck shipping based on dimensions and, more importantly, weight
limitations, above which additional permitting is needed for handling. As BESS products
increase energy storage density through higher storage capacity and smaller container size (while
managing thermal stressors and safety), there is a weight limit of 36–39 t for more standard
cranes that can safely and routinely load containers onto semi-trucks and unload them at BESS
project sites. Shipping and installing larger and/or heavier containers is possible but incurs higher
costs.

6.2.2 Domestic Production of Utility-Scale BESS
NLR estimates, based on company websites and industry interviews, that U.S. utility-scale BESS
manufacturing capacity was around 61 GWhcap at the end of 2024, with another 17 GWhcap under
construction (Figure 86). 63 The rapid build-out of new BESS manufacturing capacity in 2024
was sufficient to serve projected near-term domestic demand (assuming cell supply is available),
even as Tesla exported an estimated 5–8 GWhcap to Australia. Companies headquartered in the
United States own over half of U.S. utility-scale BESS manufacturing capacity, while Chinese-
headquartered companies own over 25%. Manufacturing capacity grew from 2024 to the first
half of 2025 as 11 GWhcap of capacity came online from the manufacturer LG (with a remaining
6 GWhcap still under construction) and 1 GWhcap from Powin was closed (Hall 2025).

63
  The location of company or parent company headquarters is organized as “U.S. HQ,” “China HQ,” or “Other HQ”

acquired any assets from a company headquartered in a different country, whether most of the company’s operations
are in a different country from the headquarters of record, or the nationalities of the company’s owners. NLR
estimates for manufacturing capacity operating or under construction as of Q2 2025 represent values to the best of
our knowledge, as some companies do not publicize their activity.

 131

 Figure 86. 2024 U.S. utility-scale BESS manufacturing capacities

6.2.3 Imports/Exports of Utility-Scale BESS
BESS products (utility-scale and distributed) are included in a larger HTS code categorization
(LIB battery packs 64), and therefore we cannot estimate import levels from the trade data. From
industry interviews, most BESS imported to the United States came from China, estimated at 22
GWhcap or around $3 billion in 2024.

BESS exports represent an unknown fraction of all LIB pack exports from the United States, but
based on industry interviews Tesla’s shipments of utility-scale BESS to Australia represent a
significant fraction of U.S. BESS exports. While over half of U.S. LIB battery pack shipments in
2024 were to Mexico and Canada (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a), BESS-specific
exports probably were not a large fraction of these shipments. Most BESS produced in the
United States are also installed in the United States.

6.2.4 Regional Costs for Utility-Scale BESS
Figure 87 compares the modeled cost of U.S. utility-scale dc block BESS assembly (excluding
the cost of the battery cells) with imports, showing modeled costs and price buildups for five
scenarios in 2024. The tariff on utility-scale BESS and their components (i.e., battery modules
and packs) from China is different from the tariff for battery cells. BESS, packs, and modules
receive a tariff of 7.5% versus the 25% tariff on cells levied under Section 301, although both

64
  The HTS classifies LIB packs under statistical reporting number 8507.60.0020, cells under 8507.90.8000, and
vehicle battery packs under 8507.60.0010. BESS-specific containers, packs, and cells do not have a unique HTS
code (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a).

 132

tariffs are scheduled to match at 25% starting on January 1, 2026 (U.S. Office of the President
2024). Tariffs on BESS, packs, and modules are applied based on the country of origin of the
cells within those products, such that a pack made in Mexico using cells manufactured in China
is subject to the tariff, while packs produced in China with cells produced in South Korea are not
subject to the 7.5% tariff (Anderson 2025).

In Figure 87, the most significant differences between countries come from the cost of materials.
Chinese BESS manufacturers benefit from having all-local supply chains already producing at
the largest scale in the world. For all other countries, the cost of materials is higher, likely owing
to additional delivery expenses (if sourcing from China) and/or a smaller local supply base.

More than 98% of global operational LFP cell manufacturing capacity was based in mainland
China in 2024 (BNEF 2025a), so we estimate BESS cell costs are higher for countries outside
China, including for U.S. BESS assembly firms, which primarily source LFP cells from China.
At least one analysis of supply chains showed more diversification in U.S. imports of non-cell
components, including sourcing from Mexico for BMS and TMS (Finn-Foley 2025).

Figure 87. Direct costs, overhead, and profit for 4-MWh utility-scale dc block assembly (excluding
 battery cells).
LFP cells are modeled separately in Figure 94, Figure 95, and Figure 96 and added to full utility-scale BESS costs in
 Figure 88. The modeled dc block utility-scale BESS is in a 20-ft container and does not include an inverter.

Labor costs are the second largest driver of price differences. Labor costs are a function of the
labor intensity (FTE per GWhdc of annual production) and labor rates (burdened annual salaries).
For all locations shown in Figure 87, a normalized assumption for 10 GWhdc of annual
production was 900 total direct operators and 250 total supervisors and engineers, working in
shifts to provide 95% uptime for manufacturing. This labor intensity is in the middle of the range
of reported jobs numbers divided by announced manufacturing capacities. The assumptions of
labor rates are the same for the NLR PV module, inverter, and BESS cost models.

 133

The depreciation expenses are averaged from company press announcements that specified
CapEx to achieve the stated manufacturing capacities, and from NLR industry interviews.
Excluding materials and labor, the remaining direct operating expenses—utilities, maintenance,
and depreciation of equipment and buildings—are calculated to be less than 3% of the cost of
goods sold for U.S. manufacturers. This low CapEx advantage is similar to PV module and
inverter assembly. When moving upstream, however, CapEx and utilities become much more
significant (see Section 6.4).

For the U.S. manufacturing scenarios, an assessment was made about which materials and
equipment would be tariffed, and 2024 duty rates were applied (see Table 19). The value of the
45X MPTC, which ramps down beginning in 2030 and goes to zero after 2032, was derived
using a discounted cash flow tool developed by NLR. 65 Using the scenario of a U.S. dc block
with 2024 materials, the modeled firm achieves the same internal rate of return with a factory
gate (excluding shipping) average selling price equal to $165/kWhdc without 45X MPTC or
$162/kWhdc with 5 years of 45X MPTC. The $3.5/kWhdc calculated difference is less than the
statutory $10/kWhdc.

 The NLR discounted cash flow calculator assumed three equipment CapEx investments and one facility

6.2.5 Upstream Inputs for Utility-Scale BESS
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to utility-scale BESS production.
Many other upstream inputs are not discussed here owing to limited supply chain information or
relatively low importance.

 • Cells: See Section 6.4.
 • Module packaging: At least two firms executing BESS assembly in the United States
 buy cells from an external supplier(s) and assemble the cells into modules using custom
 equipment. This entails stringing cells together and fitting them inside a housing that also
 includes module-level monitoring and protection equipment that connects to the larger
 BMS and TMS.
 • BMS: This product is available from domestic suppliers, although the lead times and cost
 may be higher than for imports. From industry interviews in early 2025, one
 manufacturing firm estimated that 25%–50% of all utility BESS manufacturing projects
 in the United States fit the requirement within the IRS guidance for domestic BMS and
 TMS, and one BMS supplier estimated there was enough domestic capacity (including
 competitors) to serve 25%–50% of manufacturers in the United States in 2025. Some
 BESS assembly firms design and fabricate their own BMS with domestic parts. The
 PCBA for the BMS is the most challenging part to find domestically.
 • TMS: Available from domestic suppliers. From industry interviews in early 2025, one
 manufacturing firm estimated that 25%–50% of all utility BESS manufacturing projects
 in the United States fit the requirement within the IRS guidance for domestic BMS and
 TMS.
 • Fire management: Fire management equipment includes alarms and switches for rapid
 shutdown. Less is known about the domestic availability of this component, because it is
 not listed separately in the IRS domestic content tables. Fire safety standards for BESS
 have been developed by the private U.S.-based company UL Solutions. Safety standard
 UL9540A establishes cell-, module-, and cabinet-level testing procedures including for
 fires (UL Solutions 2025). National Fire Protection Association (NFPA) codes 68, 69,
 and 72 outline protocols for explosion protection and prevention including ventilation,
 alarms, and fire suppression systems.
 • Battery racks and enclosure: From industry interviews in early 2025, one
 manufacturing firm estimated that 15%–20% of all U.S. utility-scale BESS
 manufacturing projects fit the requirement within the IRS guidance for domestic
 enclosure. Enclosures also come from China, Spain, and Turkey. A utility-scale BESS
 enclosure is much more than a standard shipping container—it is uniquely designed for
 every BESS manufacturer according to storage capacity and dc block design. Battery
 racks/racking materials that provide structural support to module strings are a negligible
 cost and are typically prefabricated within the enclosure.
 • Manufacturing equipment: As defined here, the equipment and facilities for utility-
 scale BESS assembly are divided into cell interconnection and module packaging (first
 process) and then final assembly of modules onto racks with BMS, TMS, fire
 suppression, inverter (if ac block), and enclosure (second process). The second process
 sometimes occurs in the same facility as the first process. Based on industry interviews,
 all the companies shown in Figure 86 are carrying out the second process, but their status
 regarding the first process is unknown. Both processes typically use a significant amount

 135

 of robotic automation together with human monitoring, product inspection, and testing.
 IP for utility-scale and residential-scale pack and BESS assembly has been developed and
 demonstrated in the United States. However, the manufacturing equipment that can be
 sourced domestically vs. equipment that must be imported is currently unknown.
Table 19 outlines the surveyed costs for materials and equipment for utility-scale BESS
assembly in the United States and Asia, including estimated use of domestic suppliers in 2024.
The data were collected by NLR through interviews of utility-scale BESS assembly firms and
materials and equipment suppliers. Many other upstream inputs, such as cables or coatings, are
not discussed here.

6.3 Residential BESS Packs
This section focuses on residential BESS pack production. BESS packs can refer to either the
largest unit of a residential system or a subcomponent of a BESS, as described in the previous
section. This section focuses on BESS packs for residential use, which—like utility-scale
BESS—can be either an ac or dc block, depending on whether the pack contains an inverter.

 136

Direct inputs needed for residential BESS pack production are discussed here, except for BESS
cells, which are discussed in Section 6.4.

Figure 89 shows the inputs for manufacturing a residential BESS pack. The battery cell supply
chain is further broken down in Section 6.4.5. Pack construction requires a specialized enclosure
fit to the product design. An inverter is sometimes contained within a pack and sometimes sold
as a separate product that must be connected to the pack. Most residential packs include an
inverter allowing direct connection to the building’s electrical circuits.

 Figure 89. Abridged supply chain diagram for residential BESS packs

6.3.1 Technology Trends for Residential BESS Packs
BESS packs are divided into dc-coupled and ac-coupled products, and a large number of U.S.
residential systems used ac-coupled packs in 2024. For example, Tesla’s Powerwall 2 product,
which is ac-coupled, had significant market share in 2024 (Kennedy 2025b). Tesla also ramped
up sales of Powerwall 3 in 2024, which is a dc-coupled hybrid inverter system. Residential BESS
packs are also installed with a microgrid interconnection device, which allows the battery (or
PV-plus-battery) system to operate in both grid-connected and islanded modes, helping
distributed battery systems satisfy NEC standards and interconnection processes. Enphase,
SolarEdge, Tesla, and others include a microgrid interconnection device as part of their standard
product packages; Tesla refers to theirs as the Gateway.

6.3.2 Domestic Production of Residential BESS Packs
Residential BESS packs installed in the United States are dominated by four manufacturers:
Tesla (40%–50% of market), Enphase (20%–30%), SolarEdge (10%–15%), and LG (4%–10%)
(Wood Mackenzie Power & Renewables and Solar Energy Industries Association 2025a).
Smaller players in U.S. residential BESS in 2024 included EG4, Franklin Wh, Lunar Energy, and
Generac.

 137

We estimate Tesla produced approximately 2.5 GWhac of domestic residential BESS packs in
2024, with a year-end manufacturing capacity of 5.0 GWhac and another 5.0 GWhac under
construction (Tesla 2025b; GuruFocus News 2024). 66 Enphase began producing domestic BESS
packs in Texas in the first half of 2025 (Enphase Energy 2025a), with a domestic manufacturing
capacity of 250 MWhac, 67 though most of its battery manufacturing still occurs in China
(Enphase Energy 2025b). SolarEdge, which has historically outsourced most of its
manufacturing, began manufacturing residential BESS packs in Utah in June 2025 (SolarEdge
2025b). LG has not reported where its residential BESS packs are manufactured, but the
company has extensive manufacturing in Michigan, Arizona, and Tennessee (as well as South
Korea).

6.3.3 Imports/Exports of Residential BESS Packs
As discussed in Section 6.2.3, BESS-specific trade data do not exist. However, a significant
portion of imported BESS packs, including residential BESS packs, likely came from China in
2024, given that the pack country of import is based on the location of cell manufacturing, not
pack manufacturing (Anderson 2025).

6.3.4 Regional Costs for Residential BESS Packs
NLR cost and price analysis of residential BESS packs is given in Figure 90. The results include
residential size (150—200 Ah) LFP cells. There were no known U.S. suppliers of LFP cells for
residential packs in 2024 and 2025—only for utility-scale BESS. Owing to a heavy U.S. reliance
on LFP cells from China, a 25% Section 301 tariff is applied to the China cell price of
$62/kWhdc, which appears within the U.S. materials cost.

66
 Tesla announced in November 2024 that it had produced a record 1,000 Powerwalls in a single day (~5 GWh
annualized), with a previous record of 500 (2.5 GWh annualized) (GuruFocus News 2024).
67
 Enphase can produce 50,000 IQ batteries every year with a size of 5 kWh each (Good Energy Solutions 2025).
