6.3.5 Upstream Inputs for Residential BESS Packs
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to residential BESS pack production.
Many other upstream inputs are not discussed here due to limited supply chain information or
relatively low importance.

 • Cells: See Section 6.4.
 • Module Packaging: Domestically available, but at roughly double the cost of imports for
 residential systems.
 • BMS: Available from domestic suppliers.
 • TMS: Available from domestic suppliers.
 • Fire management: Fire management controls and device shutdown are typically built
 within the BMS of a residential BESS. UL 1973 outlines the codes and standards. A
 separate fire alarm system (perhaps an extension of any existing home fire alarm system)
 is recommended for residential BESS cabinets (Tesla 2024b).
 • Battery cabinet: Battery cabinets are uniquely designed for each product. Their domestic
 availability is currently unknown.
 • Manufacturing equipment: Manufacturing equipment largely consists of robotics,
 depending on the level of automation. Its domestic availability is currently unknown.

6.4 BESS Cells
Figure 91 displays the upstream supply chain for LFP battery cells for BESS. Of the BESS
components, cells have the most involved assembly process, with various steps and sets of
equipment needed to safely package the devices. Cathode precursor materials are the most
unique of the upstream materials and have the lowest levels of production in the United States,
which produces other lithium cathode chemistries for battery cells for the automotive industry.

 140

 Figure 91. Abridged supply chain diagram for LFP BESS cells.
 CMC = carboxymethyl cellulose; NMP = N-methyl-2-pyrrolidone; PE = polyethylene; PP = polypropylene; SBR =
 styrene-butadiene rubber

Globally, battery cell production provides roughly 175 permanent jobs/GWh, significantly more
than the 120 jobs/GWh used to manufacture BESS (Volta Foundation 2025). While non-BESS
battery cell production in the United States in 2024 was estimated to be 109 GWh (BNEF
2025a), accounting for 19,000 jobs, U.S. BESS cell production was insignificant at the end of
2024. However, multiple companies started production in the first half of 2025.

6.4.1 Technology Trends for BESS Cells
Historically, cells used for stationary storage relied on LIBs made for EVs. NMC and NCA
cathodes represent the leading cell chemistries in EVs, because they give up to 800–810 Wh/kg
gravimetric energy density at the cathode level (Li et al. 2020) and 250–300 Wh/kg at the pack
level (Volta Foundation 2025). 68 The specific energy of these cells—and the EV driving range—
increases as the combined nickel and cobalt content is loaded as high as 95% (Li et al. 2020). 69

LIB cells can be prismatic, cylindrical, pouch, or blade in shape, and all four designs have been
used in BESS. Prismatic cells are most popular for BESS, led globally by CATL’s dominance
and preference for prismatic cells. Cylindrical cells are the second choice owing to their
versatility across applications. Pouch cells are less common, and blade cells have only been used
in BESS in niche applications (Volta Foundation 2025).

While the EV industry remains focused on higher-cost cells that improve charging times and
driving range, the BESS industry recently transitioned to LFP cells. Cathode costs account for
more than half of LIB cell materials costs (Knehr et al. 2022; Ahmed et al. 2021), and the
68
 This report discusses the cost-performance trade-offs between different cell technologies for BESS. The
development of LIBs for EVs is the starting point for BESS cells as well.
69
 This discovery led to a transition from lithium manganese oxide batteries to NMC and NCA batteries from 2010
to 2020.

 141

transition to more abundant and lower-cost precursors is a principal reason LFP cells were priced
$10–$15/kWhdc lower than NMC or NCA cells in 2024 (Colthorpe 2023). 70 LFP cells can
achieve a high enough specific energy for stationary storage: 550–560 Wh/kg at the cathode
level and 160–230 Wh/kg at the pack level. The lower cost outweighs the lower energy density
for BESS applications (Volta Foundation 2025). Safety is another advantage of LFP cells in
BESS, because NMC and NCA cathodes are more susceptible to thermal runaway. LFP cells can
also withstand 2–3 times as many charge/discharge cycles before they must be replaced (Volta
Foundation 2025; Yan et al. 2018). LFP took over as the majority cell technology for stationary
storage—greater than 50% market share—beginning in 2021 (Volta Foundation 2025). Even in
distributed applications, where energy density has a higher premium, LFP is being used in larger
volumes. For example, Tesla’s Powerwall 3, which was released in late 2023, switched to LFP
from the previously used NMC in the Powerwall 2, leading to a higher power output and larger
system. The Powerwall 2 rated output is 5 kWac whereas the Powerwall 3 output is 10 kWac,
despite both products having the same rated capacity of 13.5 kWhac.

Some companies work on increasing energy density at the anode. Although graphite is by far the
most common anode material used in battery cells, silicon and lithium grew as potential
replacements in early commercial developments.

Non-lithium chemistries are being sought to bridge gaps where longer-duration (more than 8
hours) storage is needed than lithium technologies can provide cost-effectively today. While
advancements have been made in commercializing sodium ion, vanadium flow, and other battery
types for stationary applications, LFP made up the vast majority of U.S. deployments in 2024.

6.4.2 Domestic Production of BESS Cells
Several companies shown in Figure 86 have overseas LFP cell production (including Hithium,
Gotion, and LG) that served some portion of U.S. utility-scale BESS assembly in 2024. The rest
of capacity in Figure 86 primarily relied on imported cells from external parties. As
demonstrated in Figure 92, at the end of 2024 there was approximately 0.4 GWhcap of operating
domestic LFP cell production capacity targeted for BESS applications and another 36 GWhcap
under construction. 71 While there were many announcements in 2024 around new domestic LFP
cell capacity, and a few companies bought equipment and started construction, only one
company actually produced and sold LFP BESS cells in the United States in 2024. From industry
interviews in early 2025, around 400 MWhdc of LFP cells made in the United States (around 1%
of total domestic BESS installations in 2024) are accounted for in shipments between AESC

nickel and cobalt precursors for LIBs were around 3 times higher than for the lithium precursors.

 There was an additional 35 GWh of operational and 75 GWh under construction domestic LFP cell manufacturing

nationalities of the company’s owners. AESC, headquartered in Japan, is classified as “China HQ” because its

(80% owned by Chinese-headquartered Envision) and U.S.-headquartered firms Fluence and
Powin. No other U.S.-made LFP BESS cells have been recorded prior to this second half of 2024
activity. By Q2 2025, 11 GWhcap of the LG LFP cell manufacturing plant was operational.

 Figure 92. U.S. LFP cell manufacturing capacity for BESS in 2024.
 Source: NLR industry interviews, BNEF

Domestic BESS cell production could increase significantly in 2025 and 2026, as five companies
began constructing new LFP for BESS cell lines or began to retool prior assets. LG could be the
largest U.S. LFP BESS cell producer in 2025 and 2026, with two production lines working and
another line under construction (Murray 2025; Pickerel 2025c). A possible fivefold increase in
domestic capacity by AESC to supply Fluence also began in 2024, even as prior partner Powin
ceased operations (Murray 2023). Up to 4 GWhcap by Chinese-headquartered Gotion and 3
GWhcap by e-Storage (a subsidiary of Canadian Solar) also began to be built in 2024. Tesla
reportedly purchased 10 GWhcap of battery cell equipment for its Nevada Gigafactory from
Chinese-headquartered CATL in 2024 (Shanghai Metal Market 2024) and is confirmed in 2025
to still be developing those assets and hiring staff (Shanghai Metal Market 2024; Coppola and
Ludlow 2024). Approximately 21 GWhcap of BESS cell manufacturing plans were canceled
between 2024 and 2025 (Freyr, Kore Power, and IM3NY), and another 17 GWhcap of projects
appear to be stalled (American Battery Factory, Microvast, Pomega/Kontrolmatic).

6.4.3 Imports/Exports of BESS Cells
Battery cell import data are from U.S. International Trade Commission datasets. Figure 93 shows
imports by mass. Most LFP cells imported for BESS come from China, South Korea, Japan,
Malaysia, and Hungary. Battery cell imports from these countries increased steadily from 2020

 143

to 2023, with a notable drop from all but Hungary in 2024. This drop corresponds to an increase
in imports of BESS/packs from China specifically and potentially other countries. Few cells (less
than 400 MWh) were produced domestically for BESS in 2024, but BESS deployment continued
to grow by relying on BESS/packs produced from imported cells as well as imported
BESS/packs.

 Figure 93. LFP cell imports into the United States from select countries, 2020–2024.
 Source: (U.S. International Trade Commission 2025a) (HTS code 8507.90.8000) 72

6.4.4 Regional Costs for BESS Cells
Figure 94 shows the results from NLR’s detailed cost model for LFP cell production in the
United States. The calculated 2024 U.S. LFP total cell direct cost equals $76/kWhdc for utility-
scale systems. 73 The contributions of cathode, anode, assembly, and finishing are represented as
percentages of this total. 74 Cathode production makes up 10% of total cell direct costs, and
cathode materials constitute 29%. Anode production accounts for 7%, and anode materials
account for 12%. Materials in general (cathode, anode, electrolyte, separator, packaging)
constitute around 60% of U.S. LFP cell direct manufacturing costs. Aluminum and copper carrier
foils are included in electrode costs. Total U.S. labor costs across all steps are around 20% of
total cell costs across cathode, anode, assembly, and finishing. Finishing is the most labor-
intensive category. To account for heavy reliance on imports, a 25% Section 301 tariff on half of
all materials costs is assumed.

72
 HTS code 8507.90.8000 is for “other parts” of “electric storage batteries, including separators therefor, whether or
not rectangular (including square).” U.S. International Trade Commission filings suggest battery cells for BESS are
included under this header. Confirmation of countries from which cells are imported suggested that cells for BESS
are a significant fraction of this heading and not a significant fraction of HTS code 8507.60.0020, other (non-EV)
LIBs.
73
 Cells for distributed systems vary more in size (50–150 Ah) than those for utility-scale systems (300+ Ah) and are
generally smaller with higher costs per kWh.

 This figure is based on a prismatic cell design with 500-Whdc cell capacity and a factory capacity of 10 GWhdc/yr.

  Figure 94. Results from the NLR utility-scale LFP cell cost model for the United States in 2024.

Figure 95 shows modeled factory gate prices for BESS cells produced in China, ASEAN, India,
South Korea, the United States, and Europe. The United States has the highest factory gate price
($96/kWhcap), followed by Europe. Much of the higher U.S. cost comes from tariffs on materials,
which are assumed to be imported from China. The United States also has significantly higher
labor costs than any of the other regions modeled. The lowest modeled price is $58/kWhcap for
production within China. The lowest labor cost is for India—around 20% lower than ASEAN—
while companies in China have access to the lowest materials costs. Europe has significantly
higher utility costs than other regions.

 145

Figure 95. Factory gate modeled prices for LFP cell production for utility-scale BESS applications.
 The results shown do not include shipping or tariffs on finished cells (though tariffs are included on some materials).

Figure 96 compares U.S. LFP cell manufacturing with imports and the modeled costs and price
buildup for five scenarios in 2024. For the U.S. manufacturing scenarios, an assessment was
made on which materials and equipment would probably be tariffed in 2024 and duty rates were
applied. The value of the 45X MPTC, which ramps down beginning in 2030 and goes to zero
after 2032, was derived using a discounted cash flow tool developed by NLR. 75 Using the all-
U.S. cell scenario as an example, the model firm achieves the same internal rate of return with a
delivered price equal to $122/kWhdc without the 45X MPTC or $109/kWhdc with the 45X MPTC
for BESS cell production. This $13/kWhdc calculated value is less than the statutory $35/kWhdc.
An all-U.S. BESS cell could also qualify for the critical materials and electrode active materials
45X MPTC available in 2024. Each of these credits apply to 10% of production costs (U.S.
Internal Revenue Service 2025b). Assuming this structure is meant to include the cathode and
anode, the combined 20% credits could apply to around $44/kWhdc of total materials and
production costs for cathode and anode (Figure 94), or a statutory value around $8.8/kWhdc. We
applied this value in full in Figure 96, recognizing a partial incentive may be more realizable.

75
  The NLR discounted cash flow calculator assumes three equipment CapEx investments and one facility
investment for a 15-year project, remaining production costs and overhead shown in Figure 95, a 7.5% rate of return
on investment, and 45X MPTCs declining from 90% of full value for the first 4 years (i.e., $35/kWac from 2025 to
2029) and incrementally down to zero 3 years after (i.e., 45X MPTC is scheduled to have 75% of value in 2030,
50% in 2031, and 25% in the last eligible year 2032).

 146

 Figure 96. Modeled prices for LFP cells for U.S. utility-scale BESS assembly.
 10 GWhdc annual production volume for all facilities.

6.4.5 Upstream Inputs for BESS Cells
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to BESS cell production. Many other
upstream inputs are not discussed here owing to limited supply chain information or relatively
low importance.

 • Cathode materials: Figure 97 shows global and U.S. cathode material capacity
 operating and under construction in 2024. Approximately 9.1 GWh cathode
 manufacturing capacity existed in the United States in 2024, all of which came online
 after 2022. 76 An additional 13.6 GWh of cathode manufacturing capacity was under
 construction at the end of 2024. In addition, 1.3 million t/yr of global capacity was
 announced in 2024, including 40,000 t/yr in the United States (BNEF 2025b; NAATBatt
 International 2025).
 • Anode materials: Figure 97 shows global and U.S. anode material capacity operating
 and under construction in 2024. Approximately 10.6 GWh of anode manufacturing
 capacity existed in the United States in 2024, all of which came online after 2022. An
 additional 22.0 GWh of anode manufacturing capacity was under construction at the end
 of 2024. In addition, 1.3 million t/yr of global capacity was announced in 2024 (BNEF
 2025b; NAATBatt International 2025).
 • Separator: Figure 97 shows global and U.S. separator material capacity operating and
 under construction in 2024. In addition, 1,000 m2/yr of global capacity was announced in
 2024, including 50 m2/yr in the United States (BNEF 2025b).

76
 We use a conversion factor of 1,034 t/GWh for anode materials and 2,202 t/GWh for cathode materials (Gohlke et

BESS.

 • Electrolyte: Figure 97 shows global and U.S. electrolyte material capacity operating and
 under construction in 2024. In addition, 790,000 t/yr of global capacity was announced in
         2024, including 465,000 t/yr of U.S. capacity (BNEF 2025b).
 • Manufacturing equipment: We identified 14 U.S. battery cell manufacturing equipment
 suppliers (Volta Foundation 2025), none of which provide equipment for every cell
 production step. The primary steps in cell production are mixing electrode materials
 (three suppliers); coating, calendering, and slitting electrode materials (five suppliers);
 stacking and winding (11 suppliers); packaging (12 suppliers); filling (nine suppliers);
 formation (five suppliers); degassing (eight suppliers); aging (four suppliers); and testing
 (five suppliers). Most battery cell manufacturing equipment has been designed for EV
 specifications.

6.5 BESS Inverters and Converters
This section focuses on the production of inverters and converters needed for the utility-scale
BESS supply chain. Figure 62 in Section 5.2 presents a simplified supply chain diagram of PV
inverters, which also generally represents the supply chain for BESS inverters and converters,
except for charge controllers (converters), which are specific to dc-coupled PV and BESS
systems as described in the following section.

6.5.1 Technology Trends for BESS Inverters/Converters
BESS products have either an integrated inverter within the packaging (typically in an ac-
coupled system) or an external inverter separate from the BESS unit. All BESS inverters require

 149

bidirectionality to charge and discharge the battery. Inverters for ac-coupled systems typically
only have two input/output terminals connecting the dc battery to an existing ac system, such as
a residence or the grid, and require communication with that connected system (Figure 98). An
inverter in a dc-coupled system can be more complex and offer more installation options. For
example, an inverter in a dc-coupled system may have a dc input for a PV array with a charge
controller or other dc energy generation, a dc input/output for the battery and an ac input/output
for the load (residence, grid, or other), which could also be used to charge the battery. However,
dc-coupled systems require each component to be designed to work with the others, which limits
the options for interoperability and component replacement in the future.

  Figure 98. Schematic of dc-coupled and ac-coupled configurations for PV and BESS systems.
 Source: Reprinted from (Ramasamy et al. 2022)

The PCBAs containing the power devices are a critical component of the BESS inverter,
constituting approximately 30% of the cost (Cole et al. 2025). Of the many types of power
devices, silicon IGBTs and MOSFETs are most commonly used, with SiC devices used in high-
voltage applications. Table 22 shows the power devices relevant to BESS inverter applications.
Markets for the different devices and power modules indicate that silicon IGBTs are the primary
technology used for BESS. GaN is not used for BESS inverters (Yole Intelligence 2024).

 Table 22. BESS Inverter Technology by Associated Voltage
                                    60–100        150–400         500–600        250–900         1.2–1.7
 V V V V kV
 Silicon IGBT
 Silicon MOSFET
 SiC

 150

6.5.2 Domestic Production of BESS Inverters/Converters
Companies based in the United States that explicitly call out manufacturing of PV-plus-BESS
inverters include Tesla, Sol-Ark, EPC Power Corp, CE+T Power, Dyna Power, La Marche, and
EG4 Electronics. Some of these companies target off-grid markets, and in many cases domestic
manufacturing capacity is unknown.

From Table 22, the most commonly used power devices are silicon IGBTs and MOSFETs across
a range of voltages. Companies 77 that produce power modules for these inverters typically
operate across either a technology or a voltage range application. Infineon is the largest global
supplier of silicon MOSFETs and IGBTs, followed by Onsemi and STMicroelectronics. See
Section 5.3 for more information on the transistor supply chain.

6.5.3 Imports/Exports of BESS Inverters/Converters
The HTS code for inverters covers products related to PV, BESS, and EVs as well as any static
inverters that convert between ac and dc power. Thus, the data for imports and exports of BESS
inverters are mixed with those in Section 5.2.3.

6.5.4 Regional Costs for BESS Inverters/Converters
A bottom-up cost model for BESS indicates that, in 2024, the U.S. inverter/converter component
cost was $26/kWhac of battery, or 8% of the overall cost for utility-scale systems (Cole et al.
2025). While BESS pricing frequently is on a per-kilowatt-hour-ac basis, inverter/converter
components are rated on a power (kilowatt) basis, and this is reflected within the model. For the
model and the IRS domestic content bonus tax credit safe harbor table, inverter cost percentages
are 30% PCBAs, 10% electrical parts, 10% climate control, 10% enclosure, and 40% production
costs.

6.5.5 Upstream Inputs for BESS Inverters/Converters
This section provides limited information on priority inputs to the production of BESS inverters
and converters. Many other upstream inputs are not discussed here owing to limited supply chain
information or relatively low importance, such as charge controllers, inverter enclosures, cabling,
concrete pads, and auxiliary transformers.

 • Power-filtering components (inductors and capacitors): See Section 5.2.5.
 • Fuses and breakers: See Section 5.2.5.
 • Copper bus: See Section 5.2.5.
 • Thermal management systems (heat sinks and fans): See Section 5.2.5.
 • Transistors: See Section 5.3.
 • PCBAs: See Section 5.2.5.

  For the inverter power devices, the companies operating in these material-voltage-device spaces are
STMicroelectronics, Onsemi, StarPower, Nexperia, Semikron Danfoss, Infineon, Fuji Electric, CRRC, Hitachi,

 151

7 Prospective Supply Chains
Some parts of the PV supply chain are still developing, including PV recycling, which is covered
here. Because this report focuses on PV technologies manufactured at gigawatt-scale, it does not
discuss technologies other than c-Si and CdTe (e.g., III-Vs, perovskites, copper-indium-gallium-
diselenide [CIGS], and organic PV).

7.1 PV Recycling
A brief overview of the PV recycling supply chain is provided here, with the expectation that
greater insight will be possible as the industry continues to develop and scale. Figure 99 shows a
simplified supply chain diagram for PV recycling.

 Figure 99. Simplified supply chain diagram for recycling c-Si and CdTe modules

7.1.1 Technology Trends for PV Recycling
The most basic PV recycling relies on mechanical methods via recycling equipment used in other
established waste flows, such as crushers, grinders, and electrostatic separators. The most basic
recycling removes the junction box and sends it to electronic waste recycling, removes the frame
and sends it to aluminum recycling, and sends the remaining laminate (glass-EVA-silicon cells
and wires-EVA-backsheet/glass) to the landfill. 78 The laminate can also be processed
mechanically for recycling, which involves either crushing the laminate and applying
electrostatic separations (produces glass cullet, mixed metals, and plastic waste) or using a hot
knife machine to separate the front glass (NPC Group 2025) and shred the remaining laminate
(mixed metals, plastic waste) which is then sent on for metal recovery.

After the various mechanical separations, further processing for material recovery can be
employed. Typically, the separated and ground laminate components are sent to glass recyclers
and metal recyclers (silicon, silver, copper, aluminum) for purification through methods like
electrowinning, smelting, and standard refining techniques. Plastic waste is either landfilled or

78
 EVA = ethylene-vinyl acetate.

 152

burned for energy recovery. The companies that disassemble the module are not usually the same
companies that conduct material recovery.

Research on PV module recycling or design for recycling primarily targets delamination and
extraction for high-value, high-purity material recovery, including aluminum, silver, copper and
silicon (Fischer 2022). The encapsulants and backsheets—the plastics in the module laminate—
pose a recycling challenge. They cannot be recycled, they are hard to delaminate (since they are
designed to remain intact for decades during harsh conditions), and they contaminate other high-
value materials.

7.1.2 Domestic Production of PV Recycling
During 2024 in the United States, 240 kt/yr of PV recycling capacity were operational (solid and
dotted colors in Figure 100), with 53 kt/yr more announced (hashed colors), and around 50 kt/yr
of capacity at an unknown operational status (gray bars) for c-Si and CdTe technologies. Of this
capacity, only around 50 kt/yr were processed. The supply of PV modules for recycling is
limited owing to long module lifetimes; less than 1% of modules experience early failures, and
more than 70% of the U.S. PV fleet is less than 7 years old (Jordan et al. 2022). All or most PV
recycling companies operating in the United States are also U.S. owned, including a mixture of
PV-dedicated facilities and many more metal, glass, and electronic waste recyclers who accept
modules. Material recovery varies by company. Of the total 240 kt/yr operational capacity, about
half the 2024 capacity was basic recycling (dotted capacity on the figure), reclaiming the
aluminum frames, glass, and junction boxes, and half targeted more complete material recovery
(solid capacity on the figure)—including silver, silicon, and copper—in addition to the frames,
glass, and junction box. Capacity for complete material recovery is expected to grow.

 153

 Figure 100. U.S. PV c-Si and CdTe recycling capacity by company, status, and location, 2024.
 Estimated annual processing in 2024 is significantly smaller than estimated capacity. An estimation of material
recovery is provided: solid indicates complete material recovery, dotted indicates basic material recovery. “Unknown”
 refers to projects identified during internal research for which NLR does not know the operational status.
 Source: Original research from websites, news articles, and interviews.

 154

7.1.3 Imports/Exports of PV Recycling
At decommissioning, some states and localities require PV recycling, and some companies
recycle on principle or to maintain social license to operate. In other cases, a contractor conducts
the decommissioning, and in some cases this can obfuscate the disposition. Some gray market
recycling/decommissioning services export old PV modules ostensibly for reuse and/or recycling
in other countries. While reuse of modules is better from an energy and material balance, the lack
of recertification standards may pose a safety hazard, and care should be taken to avoid waste
shifting. Currently, there is only anecdotal knowledge of these gray markets, which have little to
no regulation and are cost-competitive with domestic recycling, especially comprehensive
material recovery recycling. Importation and exportation of used or scrap modules cannot be
quantified using available trade data.

7.1.4 Regional Costs for PV Recycling
Costs for PV recycling vary widely across the United States and with the extent of material
recovery. Quoted ranges from industry are $10–$30/module (Libby and Shaw 2019) or $250–
$500/t of modules (Dias et al. 2022; Deng et al. 2019). Costs are high owing to the nascent
industry; there is a small end-of-life PV module stream, meaning economies of scale are not
achieved. Most PV recycling is done by glass, electronic waste, or metal recyclers who wait until
enough modules have accumulated to make it worth processing a batch.

Landfilling is the primary competitor for PV recycling. In many states, it is possible to landfill
PV modules at a cost of $26–$89/t (Libby and Shaw 2019), or about $1/module. If the batch of
modules is deemed hazardous waste, the cost increases to $175/t (Libby and Shaw 2019).

7.1.5 Upstream Inputs for PV Recycling
End-of-life modules are the primary input to PV recycling. Little to no data exist on the rate of
decommissioning (extreme weather event replacements, repowering, etc.) and size of the U.S.
end-of-life module stream. One analyst estimated the scale of global module replacement—due
to age, failure, or damage—to be 18 GWdc in 2024 (Mints 2025). Probabilistic models estimate
that, in the United States, about 5.6 kt/yr of c-Si modules and 0.1 kt/yr of CdTe modules reached
end of life in 2024 (approximately 100 MWdc). This is estimated to grow to around 90 kt/yr of c-
Si modules and 20 kt/yr of CdTe modules in the 2030s (approximately 1.5 GW/yr) (Mirletz and
Ovaitt 2024). Better end-of-life data would enable model refinement and validation and
proactive planning, including planning when and where to scale up PV recycling industries.

Equipment for disassembling modules is also critical for PV recycling. A few manufacturers
create this type of industrial equipment, and interest in such tools is growing. NPC in Japan sells
PV recycling equipment, including deframers and the hot knife tool (NPC Group 2025).

 155

8 Discussion
This section provides crosscutting insights based on information in the preceding sections and
other sources. Other literature models the system dynamics of the PV supply chain, such as
modeling how reshoring or friendshoring would affect the outcomes of disruptive events like
supply shortages, export restrictions, or a bilateral trade conflict (Gervais et al. 2025).

8.1 Technology Installation Trends
Over the past 10 years, U.S. PV installations have grown across all sectors (Figure 101). The
utility-scale sector grew the most, from a cumulative installed capacity of 15.3 GWdc in 2015 to
159.5 GWdc in 2024—an average annual growth rate of 23%. During the same period,
cumulative commercial and industrial capacity grew from 5.6 to 20.4 GWdc (13% per year), and
cumulative residential capacity grew from 6.3 to 44.2 GWdc (19% per year). In 2024, annual PV
installations grew 36% year over year, including 40.3 GWdc of utility scale, 2.1 GWdc of
commercial and industrial, and 4.7 GWdc of residential. While annual utility-scale installations
grew 62% and commercial and industrial installations grew 5% in 2024, residential installations
declined 39% to their lowest level since 2020, owing to high financing costs, installer
bankruptcies, and reduced residential PV demand in California caused by state net metering
reform in 2023 (Wood Mackenzie Power & Renewables and Solar Energy Industries Association
2025b; Masson, de l’Epine, et al. 2025). These installation trends pushed the U.S. solar supply
chain to be even more concentrated in sourcing utility-scale PV and BESS products while at the
same time allowing a higher percentage of residential PV products to be sourced from a growing
base of domestic residential PV and BESS equipment manufacturing.

  Figure 101. Annual and cumulative U.S. installation of residential, commercial, utility-scale PV.
 Source: (Seel and Kemp 2024; Masson, de l’Epine, et al. 2025; U.S. Energy Information Administration 2025a)

Within the utility-scale sector, systems using single-axis tracking have become the dominant
configuration over time, growing from 43% of annual installations in 2012 to 98% in 2024—
across both c-Si and CdTe systems (Figure 102). The United States has been the world’s leading
market for tracking systems because of the efficiency advantages, a robust domestic supply

 156

chain, and strong relationships between tracker manufacturers and developers. For this reason,
U.S. companies represent most of the leading global PV tracker companies, many of which have
grown domestic manufacturing capacity (see Section 4.2). In contrast, in 2024 only 3% of
ground-mounted PV used tracking in China, where fixed-tilt systems are preferred for their low
up-front costs and rapid installation (Joe Shangraw 2024d).

Figure 102 also shows the dominance of utility-scale systems using c-Si. Still, systems using
thin-film (CdTe) modules capture a much larger share of the U.S. market versus the global
market. In 2024, 93% of global CdTe module sales occurred in the United States, and a growing
number of those modules were made domestically (see Section 3.2) (First Solar 2025a; Mints
2025).

 Figure 102. Annual and cumulative installed capacity of utility-scale PV by technology and
 tracking types.
 Source: (U.S. Energy Information Administration 2025a; Seel and Kemp 2024)

Figure 103 illustrates the annual installed capacity of energy storage associated with residential,
commercial, and utility-scale projects in the United States from 2012 to 2024. As with PV
installations, the storage market is driven by utility-scale applications, which have played an
increasingly large role in supporting grid operations and integrating renewable resources. Utility-
scale storage capacity grew rapidly beginning in 2020 and reached 10.9 GW installed in 2024,
bringing the cumulative installed capacity to 29 GW. Residential storage installations have
grown more slowly, reaching 1.3 GW installed in 2024, while commercial installations have
stagnated around 0.1 GW/yr through 2024. This growth has corresponded with larger growth in
domestic utility-scale BESS manufacturing, followed by growth in residential BESS
manufacturing (see Sections 6.2 and 6.3).

 157

Figure 103. Annual installed capacity of residential, commercial, and utility-scale storage systems.
 Source: (Allison Feeney et al. 2025)

PV module technologies have changed over time, driven by the pursuit of higher efficiencies and
lower costs. Figure 104 shows the changing market shares of PV modules across different
efficiency bins for distributed PV installed in California and New York during 2012–2024,
indicating a variety of technological changes. For example, the dominance of relatively low-
efficiency modules in the mid-2010s coincided with increasing market shares of low-cost
multicrystalline-silicon products. By the early 2020s, however, higher-efficiency
monocrystalline products had driven multicrystalline products out of the market, and thus c-Si
modules with efficiencies as low as 16%–19% disappeared (Metz et al. 2015). Changes in cell
architecture also drove increased efficiencies. Modules with aluminum back surface field cells
captured most of the market until 2018, and then higher-efficiency PERC-based modules
dominated into the early 2020s (Zuboy et al. 2024).

By 2024, another rapid technological shift was underway globally, and to some extent in the
United States (Metz et al. 2015), this time toward higher-efficiency TOPCon-based modules.
High-efficiency modules with silicon heterojunction or back-contact cells have played a smaller
role in the market to date (Fischer et al. 2025). As of Q2 2025, commercial modules with back-
contact cells had efficiencies as high as 24.4%, those with silicon heterojunction cells as high as
23.5%, those with TOPCon cells as high as 23.2%, and those with PERC cells as high as 21.7%
(Chunduri 2025). Although products using each cell technology exhibit a range of efficiencies,
and the ranges can overlap across technologies, these high efficiency points help with inferring
c-Si technology trends from the efficiency bins in Figure 104. In 2024, most modules deployed
and manufactured in the United States used PERC cells (see Section 2.4).

 158

Figure 104. Share of efficiencies of modules in distributed PV systems in California and New York.
 Source: (NYSERDA 2025; California Public Utilities Commission 2025)

The adoption of bifacial modules in utility-scale and large distributed PV systems has increased
dramatically in recent years. In California and New York, for example, the share of bifacial c-Si
modules used in distributed PV systems grew between 2020 and 2024: from less than 10% to
about 50% for systems of 100–500 kWdc, to about 70% for systems of 500 kWdc–5 MWdc, and to
about 80% for systems larger than 5 MWdc (Figure 105) (NYSERDA 2025; California Public
Utilities Commission 2025). Drivers include the improved energy yield offered by bifacial
modules over monofacial modules (when used in ground-mounted and flat-roof systems, but not
in typical residential rooftop systems), the disappearing difference in price between bifacial and
monofacial c-Si modules, and U.S. trade policy favoring bifacial c-Si modules. Between June
2019 and May 2024, imported bifacial c-Si modules were exempt from Section 201 tariffs.

Comprehensive national statistics on bifacial module use in the utility-scale sector are
unavailable. 79 However, utility-scale systems are the primary application for bifacial c-Si
modules worldwide (Fischer et al. 2025). In addition, bifacial modules provide synergies when
used with single-axis tracking systems (Pelaez et al. 2019), and single-axis tracking systems
dominate the U.S. utility-scale market (Figure 102)—suggesting that bifacial modules are the
technology of choice for U.S. utility-scale systems. Bifacial CdTe modules are available as well,
although they offer a much smaller bifacial energy gain compared with c-Si modules (Fischer et
al. 2025; First Solar 2023c).

79
  In 2023, EIA began collecting information on use of bifacial modules in utility-scale (>1 MWac) PV systems, but
reporting has been incomplete to date. For 2024, the module type (monofacial vs. bifacial) was specified for only
43% of capacity in the EIA data. Of that 43% of specified capacity, almost all modules were reported as bifacial
(Feldman, Zuboy, Dummit, Heine, Narayanaswami, et al. 2025).

 159

 Figure 105. Bifacial share of installed capacity by system size in California and New York.
 Source: (NYSERDA 2025; California Public Utilities Commission 2025)

The share of MLPE—including microinverters and dc optimizers—used in residential PV
systems has increased over the past decade as the share of traditional string inverters has
declined. California data show the MLPE share in stand-alone residential PV installations
reaching 78% in 2024: 54% microinverters and 24% dc optimizers (Figure 106). The
microinverter share has been on the rise since 2019, mostly taking share from dc optimizers. 80
Drivers for MLPE use include declining costs and benefits in terms of shade tolerance, module-
level monitoring, system safety, and rapid shutdown compliance. National data through 2023
show MLPE market patterns similar to those in California (Barbose et al. 2024). This trend has
driven the bulk of domestic residential inverter manufacturing capacity to be focused on MLPE
products (see Section 5.2).

80
 The reason microinverters regained market share over dc-optimized inverter systems is unclear. Proponents of
microinverters argue this technology is perceived as more reliable with fewer points of failure (and no single point
of failure) and more user friendly, and it has longer standard warranties, removes all possibility of string loss, has
more modular design capabilities, and has lower voltages, which increase safety. Many of these claims, however, are
disputed. Each technology is dominated by a single company (Enphase for microinverters and SolarEdge for dc-
optimized inverter systems), and perhaps one corporation has simply outcompeted the other to date.

 160

 Figure 106. MLPE share of installed residential stand-alone PV capacity over time.
 Source: (California Public Utilities Commission 2025)

ramping up during 2024) and omission of some products whose production is wholly unknown.

In addition to the 45X MPTC, manufacturing companies also received federal (and state) support
from a variety of programs in 2024, including:

 • 48C Qualifying Advanced Energy Project Tax Credit (hereafter referred to as the “48C
 ITC”): Between May 2024 and January 2025, the IRS allocated $10 billion in tax credits
 (worth 30% of eligible project costs) to approximately 250 applicants over two rounds of
 funding (U.S. Department of the Treasury 2025). Although the 48C ITCs were awarded,
 owners of projects do not claim them until after the project is placed in service, so it is
 very unlikely that many of these credits were received in 2024. Manufacturing projects
 cannot claim both the 45X MPTC and the 48C ITC; in most cases, the 45X MPTC
 provides more value to a project (and does not need to be competitively awarded), so
 most projects that sought the 48C ITC were not eligible for a 45X MPTC. However, this
 is not always the case, due in part to some projects having long development timelines
 that would only allow a few years to claim 45X MPTCs before the program sunsets.
 While not required, 59 applicants have self-disclosed their 48C awards to date. This list
 has not been verified, but the list includes three companies that intend to recycle PV
 modules, one that intends to produce solar-grade silicon, and one that intends to produce
 solar-grade glass (Table 24).

 • The 48D Advanced Manufacturing Investment Tax Credit provides a 25% tax credit for
 purchasing and commissioning property to build a facility whose primary purpose is the
 manufacturing of semiconductors or semiconductor manufacturing equipment. The U.S.
 Treasury released its final rules for 48D in October 2024 (U.S. Federal Register 2024),
 which clarified that solar wafer manufacturing facilities were eligible for the credit.
 Shortly after, Norsun signed an agreement with an EPC, Clayco, to build its wafer facility
 in Oklahoma (Clayco 2024). Corning also confirmed soon after the final rule release that
 it would manufacture solar wafers in the United States (Pickerel 2024c).
 • In October 2024, the U.S. Department of Commerce announced a $325 million award to
 Hemlock Semiconductor from the CHIPS Incentive Program to support construction of a
 new manufacturing facility that will produce polysilicon for the solar and semiconductor
 industries. The award was finalized in January 2025 (Fischer 2025a).
 • In December 2024, the DOE Loan Programs Office awarded a $1.45 billion loan
 guarantee to Qcells to produce ingots, wafers, cells, and finished PV modules in Georgia
 (U.S. Department of Energy 2024c).
 • Since the domestic content bonus credit was introduced and a safe harbor table published,
 providing clearer pathways to qualification, several manufacturers have announced solar
 products with higher levels of domestic content that help projects qualify for the bonus.
 MLPE companies Enphase (Metea 2025a) and SolarEdge (SolarEdge 2025a); tracking
 companies Nextracker (Nextracker 2024c), Array Technologies (Array Technologies
 2025b), and GameChange Solar (GameChange Solar 2024a)’ wafer, cell, and module
 manufacturers Corning, Suniva, and Heliene (C. Shaw 2025); and cell manufacturer ES
 Foundry (Fischer 2025b) have all announced increasing the domestic content of their
 products to help projects qualify for the domestic content bonus credit. While the
 domestic content bonus credit is given to electric generation asset owners, not
 manufacturers, it indirectly supports domestic manufacturing by giving those products
 and components more value.

8.3 Manufacturing Jobs
Table 25 provides partial estimates of manufacturing jobs across the PV and BESS supply chains
during 2024 based on reported labor intensities and annual production in each manufacturing
sector, as detailed in the discussion below. Job estimates could not be calculated for all supply
chain segments. The numbers of jobs in the covered segments sum to 13,310 in 2024.

This estimate is less than the approximately 33,300 solar manufacturing jobs reported by the
Interstate Renewable Energy Council (IREC) National Solar Jobs Census in 2023 (Interstate
Renewable Energy Council 2024a, 2024b). However, we do not estimate manufacturing jobs for
several areas of the supply chain, including many parts related to the structural and electrical
BOS. In addition, the IREC manufacturing jobs total includes some equipment sales and delivery
roles as well as other positions not included in our estimates.

c-Si modules: Figure 107 presents NLR-calculated totals for U.S. c-Si PV manufacturing jobs
since 2015. The figure represents the product of two primary input datasets: historical U.S.
manufacturing capacities (covered in Section 2) multiplied by labor intensity (FTE per GWdc of
annual production) (T1 Energy Inc. 2024). NLR estimates c-Si module assembly labor intensity
by using the average values collected from four confidential industry interviews and a press
release. NLR estimates that the lone c-Si cell manufacturer in 2024, Suniva, had 100 employees
by the end of 2024, based on two online sources (RocketReach 2026; PitchBook 2026). 81 Jobs
figures for domestic ingot and wafer facilities in the U.S. in 2024 were based off of confidential
industrial interviews; because those facilities were still under construction in 2024, we only
include construction jobs. NLR estimates the polysilicon for PV jobs number by prorating the
total number of employees reported by Hemlock Semiconductor (1,350) (Hemlock

81
  The estimated labor intensity for c-Si solar cell production in 2024 is significantly greater than we would expect
for the domestic c-Si cell manufacturing facilities currently under construction once they fully ramp to full scale.

 164

Semiconductor 2026), the only firm in the U.S. selling polysilicon for PV in 2024, by the
percentage of its total polysilicon capacity dedicated to PV (37% in 2024, see Figure 23).

The decline in total jobs for polysilicon production includes the improvement in net silicon
utilization from 4.4 g/Wdc in 2015 to around 2.0 g/Wdc in 2024 (Fischer et al. 2025) as well as
improved labor efficiencies and lower labor intensities tracked by NLR since 2010. If not for
startup companies, basic research, and other limited U.S. interests, there may have been no jobs
producing PV ingots and wafers from 2018 through 2023 and no jobs producing solar cells since
2020, when the last remaining manufacturing interest of SunPower closed. This changed in 2024
as the Qcells and Corning ingot and wafer facilities created construction jobs (not included in
Figure 107) and Suniva began shipping cells to domestic module assembly firms. Most PV
manufacturing job growth has been for module assembly as the growth in capacity has
accelerated faster than improvements in labor efficiency.

 Does not include construction jobs in c-Si wafer manufacturing for PV listed in Table 25.
Source: Historical domestic labor intensity multiplied by domestic production. To calculate 2015 to 2023 values, NLR
adjusts 2024 labor intensities (see Table 25) to calculate 2015 to 2023 labor intensities by historical internal data on
 changes in labor efficiency and throughput.

CdTe modules and glass: Information for estimating the total number of CdTe module
production jobs in 2024 (approximately 200 jobs per GWdc) is detailed in Section 3.3.2 based on
press releases (Kennedy 2023; First Solar 2022, 2021). To estimate the CdTe front glass jobs per
GW, we relied on the Luckey, Ohio, facility, which reported 150 jobs for a dedicated production
facility (JobsOhio 2024), which corresponds to about 3 GWdc of front glass for CdTe PV (when
operating at full capacity).

 165

Torque tubes and structural fasteners: Based on a limited number of press releases and
information on general steel labor productivity, we estimate facilities producing torque tubes
require approximately 20 jobs per GWdc-equivalent of annual production (Nextracker 2023b;
Underwood 2024; Kentucky Cabinet for Economic Development 2021; Nextracker 2024a)
where a megawatt of torque tubes requires approximately 23 metric tonnes of steel. Using
similar information, we estimate that structural fasteners only require 1 job per GWdc of annual
production.

Inverters: Based on a limited number of press releases, we estimate facilities producing MLPE
have approximately 200–300 jobs per GWac of annual production (Wilder 2023; Misbrener
2024), while facilities assembling larger inverters have roughly 30–80 jobs per GWac of annual
production (Office of the Governor, South Carolina 2022; Power Electronics 2023; SMA 2023).
For all established capacity in 2024, this corresponds to approximately 1,900 jobs (see Section
5.2.2), with approximately half originating from facilities for MLPE.

BESS: For 2024, we estimate 4,500 FTEs working in domestic BESS manufacturing out of
14,028 working in all battery manufacturing, including stationary storage, EVs, and consumer
devices (Interstate Renewable Energy Council 2024a). The NLR is estimated by prorating
Tesla’s reported labor intensity across the whole sector. Tesla, which represents approximately
half of domestic utility BESS manufacturing, told the IRS it employed 1,700 for manufacturing
its Megapack (Tesla 2024a); prorating that value across the whole utility BESS market would
translate to 3,400 domestic employees. Tesla also represents approximately half of residential
BESS and has a manufacturing capacity about one-third the size of its utility BESS capacity,
implying an employee base of roughly 500 Tesla workers (33% of 1,700) and a total of 1,000
working in domestic residential BESS manufacturing, assuming labor intensities for residential
and utility-scale BESS are similar. As a point of reference, another residential BESS
manufacturer, Lunar Energy, told the IRS it had more than 175 employees (Lunar Energy Inc.
2024). Finally, the one battery cell manufacturer in the BESS market, AESC, reported on
LinkedIn having approximately 1,000 employees. In 2024, 400 MWh of its 3-GWh
manufacturing capacity was used for stationary storage, implying another 130 workers.

8.4 Domestic Content
In May 2024, the U.S. Treasury and IRS issued updated guidance (U.S. Internal Revenue Service
2024) on how to qualify for the domestic content bonus credit, following initial guidance in May
2023 (U.S. Internal Revenue Service 2023a):

 • 100% of steel products must be produced in the United States (requires all stages from
 melting to coating) (U.S. Internal Revenue Service 2023b; 49 CFR 661.5 - General
 Requirements 2025; U.S. Federal Register 2021).
 • For solar projects with construction starting through 2024, manufactured products must
 have 40% domestic content to receive the ITC or production tax credit bonus.
This guidance included “New Elective Safe Harbor” tables, which can be used for evaluating
domestic content in manufactured products. Production costs (non-component costs) for products
manufactured in the United States only count toward the numerator of the domestic content
percentage if all components in the product are of U.S. origin. The guidance was updated further
in January 2025 to include additional domestic content credit for PV modules that use domestic

 166

c-Si wafers, while increasing the percentage of value attributed to cells in rooftop systems (U.S.
Internal Revenue Service 2025a).

To evaluate the U.S.’ ability to meet ITC manufactured product domestic content criteria, this
section assesses 2024 U.S. PV installations. Sankey diagrams are used to illustrate domestic
value in different products for different market sectors. This analysis assumes a 1-year delay
between shipping and installation; therefore, the Sankey diagrams reflect 2024 PV installation
totals but rely on 2023 manufacturing totals and sourcing. The data in this section rely on
installation totals from IEA (Masson, Van Rechem, et al. 2025) and utility-scale system technical
features reported by EIA (U.S. Energy Information Administration 2025a) (see Section 8.1).
Information on commercial and residential installation comes from multiple sources (Wood
Mackenzie Power & Renewables and Solar Energy Industries Association 2025b; Barbose et al.
2024, 2025; Masson, de l’Epine, et al. 2025; Masson, Van Rechem, et al. 2025; U.S. Energy
Information Administration 2025a).

Figure 108 shows domestic content in the total 47.1 GWdc U.S. market for PV systems installed
during 2024. Domestic content in this figure is defined following the 2024 safe harbor tables and
can be split into four system types defined in the IRS document (U.S. Internal Revenue Service
2024): ground-mount tracking, ground-mount fixed-tilt, rooftop string inverter, and rooftop
MLPE. When considering all these system types together, the domestic content is roughly 13.5%
by value, in a total market of $24.1 billion. However, this percentage varies between system
types and within individual PV system projects as well.

As shown in Figure 108, the domestic value for systems installed during 2024 primarily comes
from domestic racking, trackers, and CdTe modules as well as inverters to a much smaller
degree. Per ITC domestic content criteria, domestic CdTe modules already qualify as having a
domestic cell, and the Series 7 module produced by First Solar contains 100% domestic
components as defined by the IRS table, which means it also qualifies for the domestic
production value in the table.

Figure 109 shows the domestic content for all ground-mounted tracker systems, representing
39.5 GWdc. Because this represents 84% of all capacity installed during 2024, the domestic
content percentage for this market sector is almost identical to the percentage for the entire
market (13.8%). However, the total market value is $18.1 billion, which only represents 75% of
the total 2024 market, because large ground-mounted systems are significantly cheaper than
smaller rooftop systems.

The domestic content for 2024 U.S. ground-mounted fixed-tilt systems is shown in Figure 110,
which represents only 1.7 GWdc. This sector has a very low percentage of domestic content
(2.4%), primarily because ground-mounted fixed-tilt racking is classified as a steel product as
opposed to a manufactured product; therefore, it does not count toward the domestic percentage
calculation. Additionally, very few fixed-tilt systems were reported to use CdTe modules in the
EIA dataset. The total value of manufactured products in this market segment ($0.6 billion) is
small because of both the smaller number of installations as well as the exclusion of fixed-tilt
ground-mounted racking from the calculation.

domestic content in rooftop MLPE systems matches the weighted market average, at 13.5%.
Most domestic content in this sector is attributed to rooftop racking, which tends to be produced
domestically. While we assume 100% of U.S.-produced c-Si modules were used in this market
sector, very few of the components in these modules were sourced from domestic suppliers in
2023, resulting in very little domestic content when following IRS safe harbor table guidance.

Finally, the domestic content for all rooftop string inverter systems is shown in Figure 112,
representing 1.2 GWdc. This corresponds to $1.1 billion of manufactured products, 13.5% of
which are estimated to qualify as domestic content. Domestic racking accounts for almost all this
domestic content.

While the domestic content averages in these diagrams are well below the minimum ITC
threshold of 40%, individual systems can have different percentages. For example, ground-
mount tracker systems using domestic CdTe modules and domestic trackers can easily surpass
70% domestic content. In practice, however, developers may blend imported modules with U.S.
modules to achieve the minimum 40% domestic content threshold over the largest amount of PV
capacity possible. Reports of this practice have been published (PV Tech Research 2025).

If we assume this blending approach is used whenever possible, we can estimate a maximum
system capacity that qualifies for the domestic content bonus credit. We assume 75% of ground-
mounted tracker systems have domestic torque tubes and minimal other domestic components
(see Section 4.3), equivalent to about 10% domestic content. Therefore, if domestic Series 7
CdTe modules are used (66.2% domestic content), only 45% of all modules in the project must

 171

be domestic Series 7 for the entire project to qualify for the domestic content bonus credit. There
was 1.8 GWdc of U.S. Series 7 production in 2023, so this could qualify up to 4 GWdc of systems.

 Figure 112. Domestic content in U.S. rooftop string inverter PV systems installed during 2024
Similarly, 2.7 GWdc of U.S. Series 6 were modules produced in 2023. These modules provide
approximately 45% domestic content if used exclusively in a given PV system. When combined
with a domestic torque tube and imported modules, these U.S. Series 6 modules could qualify an
additional 4 GWdc of additional systems for the domestic content bonus credit. In total, this may
mean up to 8 GWdc of systems met the domestic content bonus credit criteria in 2024.

Some residential rooftop systems using MLPE probably also qualified for the domestic content
bonus credit in 2024, given the shorter development timeline for residential PV and the increased
domestic MLPE manufacturing in 2024. However, because little domestic MLPE production
occurred in 2023, the average domestic content estimate for MLPE is low in 2024. In addition,
industry stakeholders told us in 2024 that many domestic MLPEs did not use domestic PCBAs
during initial production ramping.

Figure 113 tracks the average value of domestic content by sector in 2023 and 2024. The average
domestic content across the total market dropped from 15.4% to 13.5% between 2023 and 2024,
despite the capacity of qualifying systems doubling (in 2023, less than 4 GWdc of systems were

 172

estimated to qualify). This is primarily due to an almost 40% increase in ground-mounted tracker
system installations, which mostly used imported c-Si modules.

 Figure 113. Annual market size and domestic content in 2023 and 2024, by sector.
 Source: (NLR Solar TEA Tableau 2025)

Because a 1-year delay is estimated between module production and installation, we can estimate
the trend in domestic content for systems installed in 2025 by analyzing modules produced and
imported during 2024. Figure 114 shows the total capacity of modules produced in or imported
into the United States during 2022–2024. Domestic modules represented 15% of shipments in
2022. This percentage dropped to 11% in 2023 owing to the doubling of module imports, even
though domestic module production increased slightly. The capacity of imported modules stayed
relatively constant between 2023 and 2024, while domestic production approximately doubled,
resulting in a 20% domestic share in 2024.

Manufacturers of other domestic products, such as inverters, were still ramping up new capacity
during 2023, so we expect increased availability of these products in 2024 as well. Thus, PV
systems installed during 2025 are more probable to have higher average domestic content than
those installed during 2024. In addition, more systems by absolute value likely will qualify for
the domestic content bonus.

Appendix A. Detailed Cost Analysis of CdTe Module
Production
Differences in materials costs for CdTe modules by location are due to the 25% Section 301
tariffs that apply to the United States only and only up to around half of all materials (Figure A-
1). The highest-cost material for CdTe manufacturing is TCO-coated glass produced in the
United States and Southeast Asia. We estimate materials other than glass have a mixture of
domestic or imported sourcing, depending on customer needs for incentives such as domestic
content bonus credits.

Costs are expected to differ by location for shipping the input materials and equipment from
different points across the globe, but for brevity these are assumed to be small differences
compared with tariff impacts. The small cost differentials between non-U.S. manufacturing
locations are mostly due to assumed model utility rates ($0.045/kWh in Malaysia, $0.070/kWh in
Vietnam, and $0.10/kWh in India) and secondarily due to model labor rates. The glass costs
(coated front and uncoated rear) for the model were published by Roth Capital (Philip Shen
2025), the cost for CdTe compound was adjusted to reflect average 2024 tellurium pricing, and
some other materials costs including junction box and encapsulant were carried over from more
recent work in c-Si.

 ($32/kWdc) includes some legacy equipment.

Appendix B. Detailed Supply Chains for Raw Materials
B.1 Steel
Steel used in PV structural BOS components originates from iron ore or scrap steel processed
through blast furnace or EAF routes. Domestic producers supply a wide variety of hot-rolled and
cold-rolled steel coils, wire rods, and structural tubes. These intermediate forms are further
processed via roll-forming, cold-forming, or welding to make torque tubes, piles, rails, fasteners,
and other structural elements. Owing to its high weight-to-value ratio, steel used in PV systems
is primarily domestically sourced. Zinc galvanizing is performed before or after shaping,
depending on the finished product.

In the PV sector, torque tubes required an estimated 23.3 t of steel per MWdc, totaling about
930,000 t across new installations in 2024. Similarly, assuming driven piles (100 kg each) were
deployed at an average of 100 piles/MWdc, then an estimated 400,000 t of piles were used in the
PV industry. These products primarily use domestically produced steel derived from iron ore,
which is mined in the United States and processed into steel billets and steel coils in major steel
mills across the country. Based on USGS estimates, domestic raw steel production was about 81
million t, with apparent consumption reaching roughly 93 million t after adjusting for exports.
While the United States imported 26 million t of semi-finished and finished steel products, the
net import reliance was approximately 13%, reflecting a relatively robust domestic steel industry
(U.S. Geological Survey 2025k). Because of their low value-to-weight ratio and the presence of
sufficient domestic steel fabrication capabilities, we assume more than 75% of torque tubes and
driven piles used in the U.S. PV industry are produced domestically using domestically produced
steel billets and coils. This production is underpinned by an upstream supply of iron ore. In 2024,
eight open-pit mines in Michigan, Minnesota, and Utah accounted for 98% of all usable domestic
iron ore shipments, which were primarily consumed by the steel sector (U.S. Geological Survey
2025k).

In 2024, steel fasteners used across the PV industry amounted to an estimated 50,000 t, based on
an average use of 1 t/MWdc of installed capacity. While steel fasteners are primarily made from
wire rods, the U.S. generic fastener manufacturing industry is not as competitive in the low-
margin, high-volume segment. Also, no publicly available data indicate whether steel wire coils
and rods used in fastener and clamp manufacturing are imported or domestic. Despite the robust
U.S. domestic steel production and local manufacturing of large structural components such as
torque tubes and piles, most generic steel fasteners are probably imported. This is primarily
because of their small size, high value-to-weight ratio, and the cost-efficiency of sourcing from
established global suppliers. So, the U.S. fastener manufacturing base tends to focus on high-
specification or niche markets like the ones used in utility-scale PV trackers, making it less
competitive in the high-volume, low-margin generic fastener segment. Consequently, domestic
production of fasteners for the solar industry remains limited despite the availability of raw
materials (Rodriguez 2021).

In 2024, rebar demand from the PV sector was modest compared to that of torque tubes and
piles, estimated at 0.3–0.7 t/MWdc on average, with an upper bound of 2–3 t/MWdc for projects
using drilled concrete piers. Unlike piles and torque tubes, rebar plays only a minor role in most
utility-scale PV systems, limited mainly to equipment pads (e.g., inverters, transformers, motor
control centers/switchgear) and some site-specific foundations. The U.S. rebar industry is robust,

 212

with annual demand exceeding 20 million t and imports representing only about 5% of the total
market (Grand View Research 2024; MEPS International 2024). Given this scale, PV demand
represents less than 1% of total U.S. rebar consumption and an even smaller share of imports.
Consequently, PV-related rebar use is not a major driver of U.S. rebar trade, though imports
from countries such as Mexico, Algeria, and Spain remain important in filling regional supply
gaps.

B.2 Aluminum
Aluminum is the second most-consumed industrial metal. In 2024, 36% of aluminum consumed
in the United States was used in transportation applications, 23% in packaging, 14% in buildings,
9% in electrical applications, 8% in consumer durables, 8% in machinery, and 2% in other
applications. In 2024, the United States produced 4.27 million t of aluminum from primary
(16%) and scrap (84%) sources and consumed 4.3 million t. Net imports of crude and semi-
manufactured aluminum totaled 3.4 million t, while net exports of scrap aluminum totaled
940,000 t. Top sources of aluminum imports during 2020–2023 included Canada (56%), United
Arab Emirates (8%), Bahrain (4%), and China (3%). In 2024, smelter capacity was 1.36 million t
in the United States and 78.4 million t worldwide. Worldwide resources of bauxite, the main
source of aluminum, are estimated at 55–75 billion t. According to USGS, these resources are
“sufficient to meet world demand for aluminum metal well into the future” (U.S. Geological
Survey 2025c).

Aluminum used in PV racking products such as rails, fasteners, and flashing/feet is primarily
extruded from billets, wire rods made via primary aluminum from bauxite or secondary
aluminum from recycled scrap. Aluminum's lightweight, corrosion-resistant properties make it
ideal for rooftop installations. Aluminum extrusion capacity is high in the United States, with a
significant portion of billet feedstock derived from post-consumer or post-industrial scrap owing
to the high electricity requirements of primary smelters. In addition to bare mill finish, some
aluminum components may be anodized, which is a surface treatment that forms a protective
oxide layer to enhance corrosion resistance and improve aesthetic durability, particularly for
rooftop flashing, rails, or clamps.

Aluminum structural components, such as rooftop rails and flashing/feet, play a notable role in
the PV BOS supply chain, particularly for residential and commercial PV systems. Roughly 80%
of these components are aluminum-based, with rails accounting for approximately 9 t/MWdc and
flashing/feet averaging 1.5 t/MWdc, amounting to a total annual aluminum demand of around
50,000 t from the PV industry. This represented approximately 1% of total U.S. aluminum
consumption, which stood at 4.3 million t in 2024. The United States has 1.4 million t of
aluminum smelter capacity but operated at roughly 50% capacity in 2024 to produce primary
aluminum valued at $1.9 billion. The net import reliance was significant, at approximately 47%
(U.S. Geological Survey 2025c). This dependence reflects both the limited smelting
infrastructure and the broader global dynamics of the aluminum supply chain. The United States
lacks large-scale domestic mining of bauxite, the primary ore used to produce alumina, and is
therefore highly dependent on imported bauxite and alumina. Even domestically produced
aluminum-based products seem to have foreign upstream origins. Aluminum ingots are primarily
imported, and these ingots are transformed into aluminum billets in a billet casting facility,
which are then processed into extrusions for racking rails and flashing/feet. Given this, we
estimate roughly 50% of extruded aluminum rails and flashing/feet used in the PV industry in

 213

2024 were imported as finished products, while the remainder were manufactured domestically
using a mix of imported and domestically sourced aluminum billets (Chatterjee 2025; Kini et al.
2025).

B.3 Zinc
Zinc plays a critical role in PV steel components by providing corrosion resistance through
galvanization. As a standardized product requirement in some states, common treatments include
hot-dip galvanizing, zinc electroplating, or proprietary coatings like Dacromet and Magni, which
offer multilayer corrosion resistance (Mudge Fasteners 2021). The coating thickness and type are
often specified by ASTM or ISO standards to meet performance criteria.

Most zinc (70%–90%) is used in the United States to galvanize steel (Baskaran and Schwartz
2025; U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2022; Galvanizers Association UK & Ireland
2025; American Galvanizers Association 2025). In 2024, the United States produced
approximately 750,000 t of recoverable zinc, primarily from mines in Alaska and Tennessee
(U.S. Geological Survey 2025q, 2025r). The U.S. hot-dip galvanizing industry is well
established. More than 70 active galvanizing facilities collectively galvanize roughly 5 million t
of steel annually. However, owing to limited domestic refining capacity (two smelters, capacity
of about 220,000 t/yr), the United States imported 77% of its refined zinc in 2024. Canada and
Mexico were the top suppliers. In 2024, PV torque tubes and driven piles represented less than
1% of total U.S. galvanization demand. 82

B.4 Copper
Copper is the third most-consumed industrial metal, after iron and aluminum. In 2024, 42% of
copper and copper alloys consumed in the United States were used in building construction, 23%
in electrical and electronic products, 18% in transportation equipment, 10% in consumer and
general products, and 7% in industrial machinery and equipment. In 2024, the United States
mined 1.1 million t of copper, refined 890,000 t from ore and scrap, recovered 150,000 t from
post-consumer scrap, and consumed 1.8 million t of refined and scrap copper. Net imports of
refined copper totaled 750,000 t, while net exports of ores and concentrates totaled 320,000 t.
Top sources of refined copper imports during 2020–2023 included Chile (65%), Canada (17%),
Mexico (9%), and Peru (6%). In 2024, copper reserves were 47 million t in the United States and
980 million t worldwide. A 2015 USGS survey estimated 1.5 billion t of identified but
unextracted copper worldwide and 3.5 billion t of undiscovered resources. In May 2024, the
COMEX copper price reached a record high owing to expectations of near-term supply
constraints combined with increasing demand (U.S. Geological Survey 2025f).

B.5 Other Alloying Inputs
While torque tubes and piles are primarily fabricated from domestically produced carbon steel,
their production also relies on alloying and galvanizing elements such as manganese, chromium,
and smaller amounts of molybdenum, nickel, vanadium, and silicon. The United States depends

82
  We assume a steel demand of 23.3 t/MWdc, which would require 3,320 t of zinc for smelting. Assuming a hot-dip

Australia 2025).

heavily on imports for many of these elements, making the supply indirectly exposed to global
markets even though alloying inputs represent only a small fraction of total mass.
